# KB CORE — Jack Daniels — Daniels' Running Formula (2022)

Companion file: `daniels-running-formula_biblioteca.md` (full plans, workouts, exercises, recipes, templates). IDs like `DRF-L2-004` point there.

## C01 Overview & Scope

### [DRF-C01-001] Overview & Scope

**What the book is.** *Daniels' Running Formula*, 4th edition (Jack Daniels, PhD; Human Kinetics, 2022). It is a training manual for distance runners, built on exercise physiology and on Daniels' decades of coaching and lab testing. The author is an exercise physiologist (PhD, University of Wisconsin; studied under Per-Olof Åstrand) and a former Olympic-medal modern pentathlete. He is a long-time collegiate and elite coach (SUNY Cortland, Nike Farm Team, Jim Ryun, Magdalena Lewy-Boulet and others).

**For whom.** All runners and coaches: absolute beginners (white plan, novice marathon), recreational runners (red and blue plans), and serious or elite runners (gold plan, high-mileage marathon plans). Events covered: 800 m, 1,500 m to 2 mi, 5K/10K, cross country, 15K–30K, marathon, ultras (as a Q&A with Lewy-Boulet) and triathlon (run component).

**Problem it solves.** Runners and coaches copy a champion's workouts without knowing why those workouts work. Daniels offers a system in which (1) every workout has a stated purpose, (2) intensity is set from current race performance (the VDOT system), (3) stress is added gradually and one new stress at a time, and (4) the greatest benefit comes from the least stress.

**Central thesis.** Five training intensities (E easy, M marathon pace, T threshold, I interval, R repetition) each target a specific physiological system. Paces come from a VDOT value derived from a recent race. A season is organized in four phases (B/FIP, IQ, TQ, FQ). Each phase adds one new stress and keeps the earlier ones as maintenance. Consistency, rest, flexibility, positive coaching and not training when sick or injured matter more than extreme volume.

**Organization.**
- Part I (Ch 1–9): success ingredients and laws of running; training principles, stride rate and breathing; physiological profiles; the types of training (E, M, T, I, R); the VDOT system; environment and altitude; treadmill; fitness plans (white, red, blue, gold); training breaks and supplemental training.
- Part II (Ch 10–18): season planning and event-specific plans (800 m, 1,500 m–2 mi, 5K/10K, cross country, 15K–30K, marathon, ultra, triathlon).
- Appendix: time and pace conversion tables.

**What it does NOT cover.**
- No detailed nutrition or hydration prescriptions. Fueling is only mentioned (practice drinking at M pace; ultra fueling in the Q&A) and fluid loss is charted by weight.
- No injury diagnosis or treatment beyond "see a professional".
- Ultras: the author states he has not coached ultrarunners and little research exists.
- Triathlon: only sample schedules; no swim or bike technique.
- Strength training: only a bodyweight circuit and general guidance.
- Gaps in the source: Tables 2.1, 5.4 (parts) were not recoverable.
tags: type:philosophy level:all sport:running subtopic:overview
src: Whole book
evidence: author-experience

### [DRF-C01-002] Chapter 1 scope: essentials of running success

Chapter 1 sets out Daniels' view of what determines success as a runner: four basic ingredients (inherent ability, intrinsic motivation, opportunity, direction), and twelve "basic laws of running" that help runners of all levels optimize the benefits of training. Runners respond differently to a given coaching treatment, program or environment, so the laws are used to evaluate and improve individual training situations. Athletes cannot be sorted into clear-cut categories: different amounts and combinations of the four ingredients give each runner individuality. There are as many pathways to success as there are individuals.
tags: type:philosophy level:all subtopic:success-ingredients sport:running
src: Ch 1 "Essentials of Running Success" | intro, closing
evidence: author-experience

### [DRF-C01-003] Chapter 7 overview: why and when to use a treadmill

Daniels argues treadmill running is useful for all runners, not only in bad weather (heat in Phoenix in July, cold in Minnesota in February, humidity in Atlanta in August), but also on nice days when control is needed and during injury rehabilitation. Training does not have to be uncomfortable to produce benefit.
Advantages: accurate control of intensity (speed AND grade can be combined; a slow pace on the right grade equals the energy demand of almost any speed); uphill-only work without downhill; easy control of work/recovery; known speed and distance; solitude. Main disadvantage: cannot share the run with a partner or group (partly solved by alternating on/off the treadmill).
Practical tips: a wall clock with a large sweep second hand placed to the side can track work/rest and take heart-rate readings; carry no excess baggage.
Treadmill is best for I-type training, T training and steady runs; use faster speeds for R training rather than steep grades at slow speed.

### [DRF-C01-004] Ch 8 Fitness Training: four color-coded plans

Daniels offers four 16-week plans distinguished by color, modeled on the color-coded fitness levels of his high school PE program (white, red, blue, purple, gold shorts):
- White: beginners, and people who ran years ago and want to try again.
- Red: runners involved in running to a limited degree.
- Blue: serious runners who want to increase their dedication.
- Gold: very serious runners with a structured program and adequate time.
Blue and gold are more than adequate to prepare for serious racing. A runner who has spent time on blue or gold can move to the event-specific programs in later chapters.

### [DRF-C01-005] Ch 10 Season-Tailored Training: scope

Chapter 10 explains how to set up a training season. Daniels says there is no single standard approach because many factors interact (e.g., a high school cross country squad mixes novices with runners doing 20-30 miles (32-48 km) a week and experienced champions). There is no one best way of training; runners are individuals. Some principles apply to everyone (see chapter 2). Daniels keeps season plans simple so he need not be at each runner's side at every session. For runners training alone or without a coach: approach a new season carefully and not too intensely, because the less fit you are, the less intense the training has to be to benefit. Aim: minimize frustration and injuries.

evidence: author-experience

### [DRF-C01-006] Chapter 11 overview: 800-meter training

The 800 is a very special track event, maybe one of the more difficult to train for and one of the most demanding. 800 runners are described as high-speed endurance athletes. The event requires both great aerobic and anaerobic power, and it is not always easy to determine the better approach for each runner.
- Runners take either a speed approach (primarily 400 specialists) or an endurance approach (less reliance on all-out speed).
- Examples given: Peter Snell and Jim Ryun were more endurance oriented; Alberto Juantorena was definitely a great 400 runner; Joaquim Cruz was fairly well trained for both speed and endurance.
- Daniels designed programs flexible enough that parts of the training can be adjusted for all 800 specialists, regardless of preferred approach.
- Recently it has become more popular to increase endurance training for 800 runners because the aerobic contribution of an 800 race is greater than once thought.
- As with all his programs: a four-phase 24-week program; the phases may be cut down to fit a shorter time frame.
- Three weekly mileage plans are given: 20-30 miles (32-48 km), 40 miles (64 km), 50-60 miles (80-97 km).

### [DRF-C01-007] Ch 12 Scope: 1,500 m to 2 miles training

Chapter 12 gives a four-phase, 24-week plan for runners racing 1,500 m, mile, 3K and 2 mile. It also prepares runners who race the 800 or 1,000 m (indoor).
Plans are given for about 30 mi (48 km), 45 mi (72 km) and 60 mi (97 km) per week (Tables 12.1-12.3). Many 1,500/mile runners also race 3K and 2 mile seriously, so one plan covers all of these distances.
tags: type:philosophy sport:running level:all subtopic:1500-to-2-mile
src: Ch 12 intro | n/a

### [DRF-C01-008] Ch 13 5K and 10K: scope and approach

Chapter 13 covers training for the 5K and 10K (track or road). Racing a 5K differs from racing a 10K, but training can be similar for both. Racing some 5Ks helps 10K performance; 10K races make a 5K seem short.
Programs apply to runners with some running background moving up to longer events. First-time runners: use the conservative first-time marathon approach in chapter 16, which also suits a first 5K or 10K. Many people today register for a 5K, 10K or marathon as their first serious event (a change from the past, when runners first trained at shorter distances).
Before specific workouts, schedule some weeks of relatively easy running; this initial period may combine walking and running.
Plans are given for phases II, III, IV only (phase I assumed done, or not needed after recent training/racing for other events), at two weekly mileages: 40-50 miles (64-80 km) and 60-70 miles (97-112 km). More or less mileage can be done; the two plans give enough information to design other totals.

### [DRF-C01-009] Cross country: how it differs from track and scope of chapter 14

Cross country races differ from track and some road races: large fields, varied footing (hard surfaces, grass, mud), hills of various grades, and wind that may be a headwind for several minutes at a time (on a track wind alternates each lap). Tough terrain or headwind affects how the race is run. Narrow trails on poorly designed courses stretch out the field (one or two runners abreast).
Chapter motto: it is better to start races too slowly than too fast.
Chapter content: conservative-start approach, race preparation/warm-up, and a four-phase season plan (phases I-IV) for the short 10-12 week school season.
tags: type:philosophy sport:running level:all subtopic:cross-country
src: Ch 14 "Cross Country" | intro
evidence: author-experience

### [DRF-C01-010] Chapter 15: 15K to 30K - scope and approach

- Chapter covers races from 15K to 30K, which are mostly road events, not typical track events. Guiding line: "Pay more attention to time spent training than to distance covered."
- Where to train: do most, if not all, **R** training on a track (compare times over specific distances with past times). Some **I** training on a track is also beneficial. Do most **T** runs on the road (avoids boredom). For T runs find fairly flat roads so running speed stays constant throughout.
- Preparation from other programs: a successful 10K training plan prepares a runner well for 15K-20K races; marathon training is adequate for preparing for a 25K or 30K race.
- Relative novice runners: follow the novice marathon program in chapter 16 before going into the program in this chapter.
- The program applies to runners who have been training a fair amount and are ready to get serious about a distance from 15K to 30K.

### [DRF-C01-011] Ch 17 Ultradistance: scope and source of advice

- Daniels states there has not been extensive research on events of ultra duration, and that he has not coached ultrarunners himself.
- An Ironman-distance triathlon is ultra in nature but involves three sports; ultraevents are mostly running with some walking.
- The chapter is a question-and-answer session with Magda Lewy-Boulet (coached by Daniels in her pre-ultra days). Her lifetime bests at age 39 in the 1,500, 5,000 and marathon all had VDOT values of about 68 (see the VDOT tables in chapter 5). Her marathon of 2:26 qualified her for the US Olympic team. She has won major ultraraces in the US and worldwide.
- Opening maxim: "Try to make everything you do benefit your race performances."
- The advice is the experience of one elite athlete, not research-based method.

### [DRF-C01-012] Chapter 18 Triathlon: scope and thesis

Chapter 18 applies Daniels' approach to triathlon (swim, bike, run, generally in that order). Triathlon training demands all-around fitness. The run is the last leg, so a strong run requires enough fitness to push through after already reaching a fair degree of exhaustion. Chapter epigraph: "Sometimes going a little slower brings you to the finish line faster." Most triathletes have strengths and weaknesses, and training demands significant time in each event. There are many approaches to multievent training; what works for one athlete may not work for another, so the author suggests trying the different approaches presented to see what works best.
tags: type:philosophy sport:triathlon level:all subtopic:multisport-training
