## C02 Philosophy & Core Principles

### [DRF-C02-001] Core principles (summary)

**What Daniels optimizes for:** the greatest benefit from the least training stress; staying healthy and consistent; each runner performing at his or her own best.

**Core beliefs, each with his reasoning**
1. **Purpose.** Always be able to answer "What is the purpose of this workout?" If you cannot, you may be better off not training. *Reason:* workouts done at the wrong intensity do not hit the intended system (for example, running intervals faster than vVO2max adds no time at VO2max).
2. **Stress, then recovery, produces adaptation.** Strengthening happens during rest. Rest is training, not avoidance. *Reason:* overstress breaks tissue down rather than strengthening it.
3. **Least stress for most benefit.** When unsure between two sessions, choose the easier one. Raise stress only when a workout feels easier or a race shows readiness. *Reason:* diminishing returns and accelerating setbacks mean the productive zone lies between them.
4. **Hold a stress level 4 weeks (for loads, mileage and supplemental work) or 6–8 weeks (for training intensity) before raising it.** Change only one training variable at a time.
5. **Individualize.** Abilities, limits and responses differ. Do not impose a star's workout on everyone. Work on weaknesses early, emphasize strengths near key races.
6. **Race performance (VDOT) sets intensity,** not age, goals or a star's pace. "Prove to me in a race that you are ready to train faster."
7. **One new stress per phase;** keep earlier work as maintenance (maintenance principle).
8. **Consistency is the single most important factor.**
9. **Positive, respectful coaching;** focus on the task at hand; set intermediate goals. Person first, student second, runner third.
10. **Eat and sleep well;** do not train when sick or injured; have chronic health issues checked.

**What he rejects:** extreme volume or intensity copied from champions; yelling at unmotivated talent; making each workout harder than the last; training through injury; the claim that altitude is required for a great distance runner; the 220−age formula as a personal max HR; going out too fast in races.

**Non-negotiables:** rest and illness/injury rules; time-based caps (L run ≤150 min); session caps as a share of weekly mileage; purposeful workouts; conservative race starts (most race mistakes happen early).
tags: type:philosophy type:principle level:all sport:running subtopic:core-principles
src: Ch 1, 2, 4, 10
evidence: author-experience

### [DRF-C02-002] Four ingredients of running success (order of importance)

The four ingredients, in order of importance:
1. Inherent ability: most important because it is born-in (height, design of the cardiovascular system); some people are built for running.
2. Intrinsic motivation: the person's own desire to pursue running; without it even a very talented person may not reach potential.
3. Opportunity: varies with where one lives, finances, and the influence of people around; running is available in almost any climate/terrain and needs little money.
4. Direction: a coach, teacher, or training plan to follow (even something read in a book). Listed last and possibly least important, because direction can be none, some, or worse than none (for example, prescribing 150 miles/241 km per week to a raw beginner is worse than no direction).
Daniels says that the more he coaches, the more he realizes each ingredient matters for success in any running event.
tags: type:principle level:all subtopic:success-ingredients sport:running
src: Ch 1 | intro, "Inherent Ability", "Opportunity", "Direction"
evidence: author-experience

### [DRF-C02-003] Opportunity and the school/club system; multiple coaches

- Opportunity includes weather, facilities, training partners, finances. Running needs little money and works in any climate/terrain; indoor facilities or a treadmill help in extreme cold or heat. Lacking opportunity may strengthen dedication. Runners training alone sometimes surprise competitors.
- Daniels' explanation for why the U.S. does not dominate running: lack of physical education that identifies talented runners, and running is not promoted like other sports; children seeing only TV team sports never see great runners.
- U.S. runners are mostly in school systems rather than clubs; multiple coaches (middle school, high school, cross country vs track, college) may give differing approaches and undermine a continuous program. A runner should hope each coach supplies a missing idea; a new coach should find out what each runner has previously done in order to know which techniques to apply.
tags: type:principle level:all subtopic:opportunity subtopic:coaching sport:running
src: Ch 1 "Opportunity"
evidence: author-experience

### [DRF-C02-004] Direction and what makes a good coach

- Direction is only helpful if appropriate: always ask about current running, past running, and past sports before prescribing; do not impose high loads on beginners.
- Positive coaching is preferred: note progress even when it seems absent; emphasize improvement over a couple of years rather than every season. Runners progress at different rates.
- A good coach can always answer "What is the purpose of this workout?" and "Why are we doing this workout today?"; produces beneficial reactions to training, good race results, and makes athletes better runners and better human beings. Coach evaluation is hard because it is judged by performances; in U.S. collegiate system success often reflects recruiting rather than improvement.
- Talented athletes can mask a poor coach; great coaches with little to work with may be overlooked.
- Coaches must be available, care for athletes as people first, and give positive individual attention. Priority order told to college runners: "First you are a person, second you are a student, and third you are a runner—and don't ever rearrange that order of importance while you are in school." Elite runners need a support system with the athlete's best interests in mind.
- Treat runners with respect and as individuals; focus on individual improvement, not comparison with others; recognize every improvement.
tags: type:principle level:all subtopic:coaching subtopic:direction sport:running
src: Ch 1 "Direction", "Intrinsic Motivation"
evidence: author-experience

### [DRF-C02-005] Daniels' Basic Laws of Running (1-12)

1. Every runner has specific individual abilities (unique strengths and weaknesses). Example: one runner has high fraction of slow-twitch fibers and high VO2max; another with lower VO2max may have outstanding running economy from ideal mechanics. Spend much training time improving known weaknesses, but when approaching important races emphasize known strengths. Example: runner weak in speed but strong in endurance works on speed early and midseason, then emphasizes endurance in the latter weeks.
2. A runner's focus must stay positive. Do not dwell on the negative; find positives in every session (e.g., instead of "You sure looked bad," say "Sorry you weren't feeling great, but your arm carriage looked like what you've been working toward").
3. Expect ups and downs; some days are better than others. Even record holders have off days. The longer the race, the less desirable to race when unwell (a poor marathon needs more recovery than a poor 5K). Daniels recommends dropping out of a race when not feeling well rather than struggling through and needing a long time before running well again.
4. Be flexible in training for the unexpected. Example: if Monday's workout day has cold rain and high wind and Tuesday looks nicer, move the workout to Tuesday.
5. Set intermediate goals that pave the way to long-term goals (which may take years). Set goals for most races, preferably fairly easy to achieve (e.g., start slower and count passes; try a different warm-up). One can learn from mistakes as well as success.
6. Concentrate on the task at hand; do not worry about others. If you executed your plan and were beaten, accept that others had a better day; reflect on what you would do differently. We learn from losing as often as (or more than) from winning.
7. Most mistakes in races are made early. Typically the best runner goes out too fast and slows; the pack following faces even greater initial stress and fades worse. The day a third- or fourth-best runner paces evenly is the day the best runner is beaten.
8. Training should be rewarding: not always fun, but always rewarding. Understanding each workout's purpose shows progress is being made. Learn something useful from any race or workout.
9. Eat and sleep well. Rest and good nutrition are parts of training, not outside it. If you regularly eat and sleep well, one bad meal or night will not hurt; if you regularly eat poorly or sleep too little, one good meal or night will not help much.
10. Don't train when sick or injured; doing so often causes a more prolonged setback than a few days off.
11. Chronic health issues should be checked by a professional. Feeling below par now and then is fine; consistently feeling out of sorts usually needs medical attention.
12. A good run or race is never a fluke (a bad one sometimes is); a great race means you are capable of it.
tags: type:principle level:all subtopic:basic-laws subtopic:racing-pacing subtopic:goal-setting sport:running
src: Ch 1 "Daniels' Basic Laws of Running"
evidence: author-experience

### [DRF-C02-006] Consistency and living in the present; making the laws habitual

From a runner's standpoint, consistency in training is the single most important thing leading to success. It comes from concentrating on the task at hand, neither dwelling on the past nor looking too far ahead; the present is the only thing a runner controls. The laws should become part of everyday life so a runner does not have to think about how he or she treats themselves; not following them can lead to disappointing performance and poor relationships with other runners. Periodically reviewing the laws helps avoid overtraining and neglecting the body. Runners are encouraged to make changes when alterations Daniels offers bring success.
tags: type:philosophy level:all subtopic:consistency subtopic:overtraining sport:running
src: Ch 1 closing
evidence: author-experience

### [DRF-C02-007] Chapter 2 stance: understand why you train; no single best system

- Runners often copy what others did (coach trained as they were coached, or champions' methods) without understanding why. Daniels asks: do runners understand why they are doing what they do?
- There is probably no single training method that is best for every runner; basic principles exist that improve different bodily functions. Many systems produce results with a large enough team, but those who did not survive a system (driven out by injury or perceived lack of commitment) may have been better than those who did.
- At all times a runner or coach should be able to answer: "What is the purpose of this workout?"
- Be open to different types of training and to arranging them in different orders of importance through a season; expose all runners to various approaches.
- Always seek the greatest possible benefit from the least amount of training, rather than the greatest benefit from the hardest training possible.
- Training is not always fun but should be rewarding; do not overdo it. Be conservative when increasing stress.
tags: type:philosophy sport:running level:all subtopic:purpose-of-workout subtopic:least-stress-greatest-benefit
evidence: author-experience
src: Ch 2 intro, "Training Plan Development" | no page markers

### [DRF-C02-008] Principle 1: The body reacts to stress

Every imposed stress produces immediate reactions (e.g., after one lap: faster heart rate, harder breathing, some leg-muscle discomfort, blood pressure up, blood flow diverted to working areas). The same stress gives the same immediate reactions each time, but over time, repeated stress produces a different reaction: the body strengthens itself. The body adapts well to varied stresses but some tissues take long (muscle fibers take months to fully adjust to regular running). Understand the principles to avoid overstressing via amount, intensity or frequency.
tags: type:principle sport:running level:all subtopic:stress-response subtopic:adaptation
evidence: author-experience
src: Ch 2 "Principles of Training", Principle 1

### [DRF-C02-009] Principle 2: Specificity

The tissues being stressed are the ones that react. Stress the heart and it reacts; breathing muscles likewise; running muscles likewise; feet react to running/walking. Besides immediate reactions, there is a second reaction: stressed parts become stronger and better prepared for future stress, provided the body is in good health. Applies to muscles, tendons, bones and other tissues.
tags: type:principle sport:running level:all subtopic:specificity
evidence: author-experience
src: Ch 2, Principle 2

### [DRF-C02-010] Principle 3: Overstress (rest is when strengthening happens)

- More stress leads to more adaptation, but overstress can make body parts weaker or break them down.
- Strengthening occurs during recovery/rest between bouts of stress. Rest and recovery are a vital part of training, not avoidance of it. Sometimes rest beats another run, and a less stressful workout can give more benefit than a harder one.
- Rule: when unsure which of two sessions to do, select the less stressful one (being unsure means you cannot justify the harder one).
- Example: choosing between solid 1,000 m intervals and a fartlek in undesirable weather/heavy wind: the 1,000s would be slower than desired and discouraging; fartlek has no time targets and may give equal benefit.
- Adapt workouts to individuals: if a coach prescribes 8 × 400 in 75 s with 400 jog because star Bob did it, those who cannot hold pace (78, 80 or slower) get less recovery and lose technique; then economy/speed are not being developed and only Bob benefits, the others get negative feedback. Coaches and runners must adjust to each athlete present.
- The slowest runner may eventually become the best if not discouraged, quit or injured.
- Team philosophy: prefer top seven cross country runners all healthy and a little below capability than three at peak and four injured; get each member performing at her best regardless of rank. Author states the US never sends its top runners to the Olympics because some of the best are hurt at selection time.
- Reduce physical and psychological stress going into championships. Example: Tom Von Ruden, feeling unready weeks before the 1968 Olympic trials (South Lake Tahoe), was advised to go alone to Leadville, Colorado for the last couple of weeks; he made the US team and finished 9th in the Olympic 1,500.
tags: type:principle type:decision-rule sport:running level:all subtopic:overstress subtopic:rest subtopic:individualization subtopic:less-stressful-option
evidence: author-experience
src: Ch 2, Principle 3

### [DRF-C02-011] Principles 6 and 7: Diminishing return and accelerating setbacks (Figure 2.3)

- Diminishing return: early in training benefits are substantial relative to effort; the fitter you are, the less benefit from training harder (time scale can be years). Examples: 1-mile 6:10 to 5:40 is easier than 5:40 to 5:10, and 4:30 to 4:00 takes far more work; improving marathon by 1 min at 2:08 is harder than by 4 min from 3:00 to 2:56. Corollary: the worse your fitness, the more benefit from moderate training (reassuring after illness or injury).
- Accelerating setbacks: at low training stress the chance of setback (injury or loss of interest) is low; at some point as intensity rises, setback chance rises rapidly (dashed curve in Fig 2.3, right axis percent chance of setback; left axis percent of fitness gained).
- Combined: most training should be in the shaded ideal zone (substantial benefit, minimal setback risk). No amount of stress can be assigned to that window: it varies with experience, e.g., 30 miles (48 km)/week for one runner, 120 miles (193 km)/week for another; depends on years of experience, body design, time available and commitment.
tags: type:principle sport:running level:all subtopic:diminishing-return subtopic:accelerating-setbacks metric:volume
evidence: author-experience
src: Ch 2, Principles 6-7; Fig 2.3

### [DRF-C02-012] Principle 8: Maintenance

It is easier to maintain a level of fitness than to achieve it; partly psychological (repeating a performance is easier) and physiological (stronger heart, more vessels opened to running muscles, muscle cells more efficient at converting fuel to energy). Support: tapering, reducing stress while maintaining or improving results. Use in planning: allows shifting emphasis (e.g., from interval to threshold work) while keeping earlier gains; include some, but less, of the earlier training type. Also, between seasons (e.g., cross country to track) or during injury/illness/break, supplemental activity (other sports) helps maintain some conditioning.
tags: type:principle sport:running level:all subtopic:maintenance subtopic:taper
evidence: author-experience
src: Ch 2, Principle 8

### [DRF-C02-013] Improve as many running variables as possible

- Principle: improve as many variables as possible to perform better. From Figs 3.1-3.2, training must improve aerobic power (VO2max) and running economy; as either or both improve, vVO2max improves.
- A person can run at various fractions of vVO2max for different durations: about 93% of vVO2max for any event lasting about 30 minutes. As vVO2max improves, so does race speed for any specific time.
- Daniels used this to develop the VDOT tables (discussed in chapter 5).
tags: type:principle sport:running system:aerobic subtopic:vvo2max subtopic:vdot level:all
evidence: author-experience
src: Ch 3 "Running Variables and Improvements"

### [DRF-C02-014] Purpose of every workout

- Always be able to answer "What is the purpose of this workout?" If you cannot, you may be better off not training at that time.
- Gain the maximum benefit from the least amount of stress, not the maximum benefit from the most stress.
- Think in terms of time spent at each intensity rather than distance, so runners of different ability are equally stressed (a slower runner doing the same distance spends much more time and takes more impacts).
- Never overlook the mental side of training.
tags: type:philosophy level:all subtopic:workout-purpose subtopic:least-stress sport:running
src: Ch 4 intro, "Reaching VO2max", "Repetition Training"
evidence: author-experience

### [DRF-C02-015] Using race performance and VDOT: principles and limits

- Use your highest VDOT from several race times (the best recent performance) to read training paces from Table 5.2.
- Do not use a time from perfect conditions to predict a race under poor conditions. A mile is a poorer predictor of a marathon than a half marathon is.
- Treat VDOT values as good predictors of physiological capability. They work best for estimating times at the distances you are currently training for.
- Historical note: women's 1,500 and 3,000 m records represented VDOT 71+ while the marathon record was considerably lower. This led to the prediction that women's marathon times would fall below 2:20, which happened. World records for men and women are close in VDOT; men's are a little more than 11% higher. A woman with VDOT 70 outperforms a man in the mid-60s.
- Suggestion: VDOT/performance could be used to assign starting boxes in races.
- Oxygen Power (1979, Daniels and Gilbert) has 81 pages of VDOT tables for 40+ distances.
tags: type:principle level:all subtopic:vdot subtopic:race-prediction sport:running
src: Ch 5
evidence: author-experience

### [DRF-C02-016] Train under the conditions of the target race

- To be prepared for a race under adverse conditions, train under the same conditions where important races will take place. If you train in ideal temperatures you can predict outcomes fairly accurately; if you train in one weather and race in different weather, do some of your training under the weather you will encounter.
- Do not train a lot under adverse conditions: you cannot train as hard as usual. Do not lose fitness by training under poor conditions all the time.
- Personal data (chart) replaces formulas for deciding how much to slow down for conditions.
tags: type:principle sport:running level:all subtopic:environment-specific-training
evidence: author-experience
src: Ch 6 "Temperature Considerations" | no page markers

### [DRF-C02-017] Altitude is not required for distance-running success

- Author disagrees with the claim that without altitude training you may as well not try to be a great distance runner; he considers it untrue and a poor message for youth.
- Evidence he cites: South American countries with altitude residents lack many great distance runners; Rocky Mountain youth grow up at altitude without automatic success. Social and genetic factors may matter; look at what all great runners have in common.
- Weigh altitude against weather, facilities, housing, food, medical attention and social atmosphere of the training site.
tags: type:philosophy sport:running level:all subtopic:altitude
evidence: author-experience
src: Ch 6 "Altitude: The Best Training Ground?"

### [DRF-C02-018] General advice for all runners (Ch 8)

- Less fit people benefit from a low-stress regimen. Only after a high fitness level is reached must workouts be very demanding to produce improvement. At the start, stick to the program and do not overdo it.
- Rest, but not avoidance of running, is an essential part of training.
- Consistency in rest, nutrition and training is the key to maximum benefit.
- Never train when injured or ill.
tags: type:principle level:all subtopic:consistency subtopic:rest sport:running
src: Ch 8 intro
evidence: author-experience

### [DRF-C02-019] Rest and breaks as part of training

- Rest is part of training, not an avoidance of it. A full break (ceasing running completely for several days to a month or two) can be beneficial at times.
- Just as the body reacts positively to easy days between quality sessions, time away from training lets body and mind reach a new level of performance once regular training resumes.
- You are better off taking a few rest days to heal an injury than working through it (which prolongs healing). The same applies to minor illness: training through it can cause a major setback.
- Illustrative case: an NCAA champion, Pan American Games champion and Olympian broke 30 min for 10K at age 40. VO2max was 78.6 at age 24 and 76.0 at age 50. He took more than 1,200 days off over 25 years (a few days at every minor injury or illness), and did cross-country skiing instead of running in winter (skiing days not counted as days off). Lesson: many small breaks do not harm long-term fitness.
tags: type:philosophy level:all subtopic:rest-as-training subtopic:injury-illness sport:running
evidence: author-experience
src: Ch 9 "Training Breaks" | intro and "Taking Breaks"

### [DRF-C02-020] Individualization in marathon training

Each runner needs an individualized program. Some handle higher mileage; some cannot handle a tough Q session as often as others handle the same schedule. Beginners include people who never ran or did any physical activity. Each person reacts differently to the same program; no single program is best for everyone. Eat well, stay hydrated, get adequate rest, and believe the training improves running and health. Running for fun makes the day enjoyable.
Motto: "Run with your brain the first two-thirds of every race and finish with your heart."
tags: type:philosophy sport:running level:all subtopic:individualization subtopic:marathon
src: Ch 16 Marathon | intro
evidence: author-experience

## C03 Integral Model

### [DRF-C03-001] Integral Model

**1. Chain of reasoning: beliefs → mechanisms → methodology → decisions**

*Beliefs* (see Core principles (summary)): stress plus recovery yields adaptation; the best way to improve a bodily function is to stress that function (specificity); least stress for most benefit; every runner is different.

*Mechanisms* (see Aerobic profile and economy curve; vVO2max; Blood lactate profile):
- Performance is the combination of VO2max (aerobic power), running economy and lactate/threshold ability. Together VO2max and economy give vVO2max, the best single predictor of potential.
- E runs strengthen the heart (stroke volume), vascularization and muscle fibres.
- T runs improve lactate clearance (endurance).
- I runs stress VO2max (it takes about 90–120 s to reach VO2max; time at VO2max is the target).
- R runs improve anaerobic power, speed and economy.
- Recovery is when adaptation occurs.

*Methodology:* the five intensities E, M, T, I, R, each with a purpose, a pace taken from VDOT and a per-session cap; a four-phase season; mileage-banded plans.

*Decisions:* what pace, how much, which phase, when to add stress, when to back off.

**2. Key variables and priorities**
- Variables: workload, intensity, recovery and frequency (Ch 2). Change only one at a time.
- Priority order:
  1. Health and consistency.
  2. Purpose of each session.
  3. Correct intensity (from a recent race through VDOT).
  4. Volume (weekly mileage or time).
  5. Progression (hold 4 weeks, or 6–8 weeks for intensity).
  6. Race specificity (final phase).
- Per-session caps: I = lesser of 10K and 8% of weekly mileage; T = 10%; R = lesser of 5 miles and 5%; M = lesser of 110 min/18 mi and 20% of weekly miles; L ≤30% of weekly mileage if under 40 mi/wk, otherwise lesser of 25% and 150 min.
- Work-bout limits: I bouts ≤5 min; R bouts ≤~2 min; T tempo ~20 min steady, or up to 30 min as cruise intervals.
- Time rather than distance is the common unit that makes runners of different ability comparable.

**3. How the tools fit**
- *Runner profile (Figure 3.8)* → current mileage, longest run, races, time and facilities.
- *VDOT (Tables 5.1–5.3)* → paces for E, M, T, I, R. The 6-second rule gives R, I, T from mile pace. Table 5.3 is for VDOT 20–30.
- *Season model (Ch 10)* → four phases, 6 weeks each in a 24-week model (Figure 10.2 shows which weeks to drop when time is short). Emphasis: E/strides, then R, then I, then T. Prior work is kept as maintenance.
- *Weekly structure:* L on day 1; Q1 is the most important session; Q sessions with E days between; races count as Q; 1 E day per 3K of race after racing.
- *Event chapters (11–18)* apply that model to specific distances at specific mileage levels (tables in the library).
- *Fitness plans (Ch 8)* serve non-competitors and bridge to event plans.
- *Modifiers:* altitude (T slowed 12–16 s/mile, I slowed 3–4 s/400, R unchanged with longer recovery), heat/wind/hills (use effort or HR rather than pace), treadmill (speed plus grade, Table 7.1).
- *Breaks (Ch 9):* Table 9.1 factors (FVDOT), weight adjustment (Figure 9.2) and Table 9.2 staged loads.
- *Monitoring:* resting HR, stress table, points system (Figure 4.5), weight loss in heat.

**4. Decision flow for a new runner**
1. Collect the Runner Profile.
2. Is the runner healthy? If sick or injured, do not train; seek professional advice for chronic issues.
3. Determine VDOT from the best recent race (or a conservative estimate of the mile). Use a race of at least 10K for marathon plans.
4. If returning from a break, apply Figure 9.2 and Table 9.1, then Table 9.2 loads.
5. Choose the plan by level (white → red → blue → gold) or by event and weekly mileage band.
6. Set the season length (24-week model; shorten by Figure 10.2). Skip phase I if running regularly for 6+ weeks; skip to phase III if 3–4 weeks of demanding work have gone without setbacks.
7. Phase I: E, L, strides, supplemental work. Phase II: R. Phase III: I (the hardest). Phase IV: T plus a few R, races, rest.
8. Apply caps; hold loads 4 weeks (6–8 weeks for intensity); raise VDOT no more often than every 3rd–4th week and only after races.
9. Adjust for conditions (altitude, heat, wind, terrain).
10. Race rules: 2–3 E days before; final Q 3–4 days before at T pace; start conservatively (2-2 breathing the first two-thirds).
11. After the race, 1 E day per 3K.
12. Re-evaluate speed versus endurance (Table 10.1) and work on the weakness first, then use strengths.

**5. Tensions and trade-offs the author acknowledges**
- Genetic ability and opportunity outweigh direction, yet bad direction is worse than none.
- Heart rate versus speed: HR gives intensity, pace gives speed; choose by the workout's purpose.
- Race performance versus training progression: races, not eagerness, justify faster training.
- Individual versus group sessions: R sessions suit groups poorly.
- Altitude: not essential, but useful for learning to hurt; training at altitude is slower in T and I yet fully stresses the aerobic system.
- Works from VDOT's representative economy: the lab VO2max may differ from the VDOT value without harming paces.
- Plans are templates: days and order may be rearranged; the order of Q sessions matters more than the specific days.
- Races as training: they replace I sessions (5–20 min races).
- Ultras and triathlon rely on practitioners' experience, not on research.
tags: type:philosophy type:methodology level:all sport:running subtopic:integral-model
src: Whole book
evidence: author-experience

## C04 Mechanisms

### [DRF-C04-001] Inherent ability: genetic and physiological differences between runners

- Body builds suited to gymnastics, basketball, shot put, or riding are not achieved by training; they are inborn. Distance runners vary more in anatomy (tall/short, lean/muscular), but better runners share physiological and biomechanical traits: for example, how far from a joint a muscle tendon attaches, and cardiovascular efficiency (heart size, cardiac output).
- Much of what makes a great runner is not visible. Two runners of the same height and weight, eating, sleeping and training the same, can differ by 30 seconds in a mile because of unseen physiological, biomechanical or psychological factors.
- Hemoglobin (carries oxygen to exercising muscle) varies considerably among runners; Daniels has seen more than a minute difference in 5K times associated with relatively small hemoglobin differences.
- Not all body types have equal chance in a sport; weight classes in boxing, wrestling, weightlifting recognize inherent differences.
tags: type:mechanism level:all subtopic:inherent-ability subtopic:hemoglobin sport:running
src: Ch 1 "Inherent Ability"
evidence: author-experience

### [DRF-C04-002] Breathing discomfort is caused by CO2, not lack of O2

- Breathing discomfort in hard running is not due to lack of O2 in the lungs; the urge to breathe harder is caused by increased CO2. Normal air is about .04 percent CO2. Lung air is about 4 or 5 percent CO2 even at rest (comfortable), because blood delivers CO2 from tissues; exercise muscles produce much more, raising it.
- Sensing a rise above 4-5% makes you breathe harder to remove CO2; O2 is always plentiful in normal air. Example: breath-holding underwater urge is CO2 buildup; with true O2 insufficiency one would pass out, and CO2 drive fortunately acts first.
- The harder you run, the faster CO2 is delivered to lungs, driving breathing, which also keeps O2 well above what the work needs.
- People with asthma or other breathing issues should discuss ventilation problems with their doctor.
tags: type:mechanism sport:running level:all subtopic:co2 subtopic:ventilation
evidence: author-experience
src: Ch 2 "Breathing While Running", "Impact of CO2"

### [DRF-C04-003] Aerobic profile and economy curve

- Aerobic profile: the response of the aerobic system to steady, periodic increases in running speed. In Daniels' test the speed was increased by the same amount every 5 minutes. 5 minutes per workload guarantees steady state (response not changing after the 2nd or 3rd minute), so the response shows what each speed demands aerobically.
- VO2 (oxygen consumption) responds quite linearly to increases in running speed (calm conditions, flat surface). Blood lactate does not respond linearly.
- Economy curve: the VO2 response curve against speed; it shows how much aerobic involvement is associated with increasing workloads.
- Some runners are more economical than others: they use less oxygen at the same speed.
- Running economy can differ greatly between well-trained runners.
tags: type:mechanism sport:running system:aerobic metric:pace subtopic:running-economy level:all
evidence: author-experience
src: Ch 3 "Aerobic Profile" | Fig 3.1

### [DRF-C04-004] vVO2max: velocity at VO2max

- vVO2max (velocity at VO2max): the running speed associated with a runner's combined VO2max and running economy. Daniels says he originally termed and presented it.
- Obtained by extrapolating the economy curve (VO2 at several submax speeds) out to VO2max and reading the speed at that point (Fig 3.1: four submax speeds; example VO2max about 70 ml/kg/min at about 330 m/min).
- Daniels: vVO2max is without question a better way to compare potential running performance than either economy or VO2max independently.
- Example (Fig 3.2): two teammates with similar race times. Runner 1 VO2max = 73, Runner 2 VO2max = 63 ml/kg/min (Runner 1 over 15% higher). Runner 1 is less economical. Both reach VO2max at the same speed, about 325 m/min (the figure shows between 320 and 330). At 270 m/min (81% of VO2max for both) Runner 1 uses 59 and Runner 2 uses 51 ml/kg/min. Runner 2 competes equally because of better economy.
- Example (Fig 3.3): three elite women, VO2max 73, 69 and 60; vVO2max 331, 329 and 328 m/min (listed in the figure as 328, 329, 331); 3K times 9:04, 9:07 and 9:08. Representative VDOT 65.4. The lowest-VO2max runner was a U.S. collegiate national champion at 10,000 m; the highest-VO2max runner was successful internationally.
- Improving VO2max and/or economy improves vVO2max.
tags: type:definition sport:running system:aerobic metric:pace subtopic:vvo2max level:all
evidence: author-experience
src: Ch 3 "Aerobic Profile" | Figs 3.1-3.3 | pp. n/a

### [DRF-C04-005] Example of improvement in an elite runner, early vs midseason (Fig 3.4)

- Elite runner tested early and later in the track season: max HR the same (196) in both tests.
- VO2max rose from about 73 to nearly 78 (about 7%) within a few months.
- With improved economy, vVO2max rose from 358 to 387 m/min (8%).
- Speed at 5.0 mmol blood lactate rose from 330 to about 355 m/min (another 7.5% improvement).
- In both sessions he reached 4.0 mmol lactate at about 85 to 87% of VO2max, which Daniels calls typical for well-trained runners.
tags: type:evidence sport:running system:lactate metric:pace subtopic:season-progress level:elite
evidence: case-study
src: Ch 3 "Aerobic Profile" | Fig 3.4

### [DRF-C04-006] Variations in running economy: altitude, track vs treadmill

- Tested well-trained runners at sea level and at 2,000 m altitude, each on treadmill and track (Fig 3.5).
- VO2max was the same on track and treadmill, at sea level and at altitude.
- VO2max was lower at altitude on both surfaces: about 13% lower on arrival at altitude.
- The aerobic demand of running is lower at altitude (less air resistance, better economy). Some of the lost ability is regained: vVO2max (and performance) differs by only about 6%.
- Economy is much better on a treadmill than on the track, both at sea level and at altitude.
- Figure values for end speed (vVO2max): sea level treadmill about 387, sea level track about 357, altitude treadmill about 356, altitude track about 337 m/min (read from the figure description).
tags: type:mechanism sport:running system:aerobic subtopic:altitude subtopic:treadmill subtopic:running-economy level:all
evidence: author-experience
src: Ch 3 "Variations in Running Economy" | Fig 3.5

### [DRF-C04-007] Gender differences in VO2max and running economy

- Men race longer distances faster partly because the best men have higher VO2max than the best women.
- Variation is large: Daniels tested Olympic-caliber men with VO2max from 68 to 86 ml/kg/min.
- Runners with lower VO2max are usually better at 800 and 1,500 because of the greater anaerobic demand. These runners are also more economical at faster speeds than longer-distance specialists, probably because they spend more time perfecting mechanics at faster speeds.
- Men are slightly more economical than women, but the difference is small. Comparing at the same submax speed is unfair to women because they run at a higher fraction of their lower VO2max (and the faster you run, the less economical you are).
- Fair comparison: same fraction of each person's VO2max, expressed as VO2 per kg per km.
tags: type:mechanism sport:running subtopic:sex-differences subtopic:running-economy level:all
evidence: author-experience
src: Ch 3 "Gender Differences in VO2max and Running Economy"

### [DRF-C04-008] Blood lactate profile and its right shift with training

- A lactate profile is built by testing a runner at several submaximal speeds; lactate is non-linear (slow rise then steep).
- Desirable: the curve shifts right with improved endurance, meaning faster speed before lactate reaches the earlier value (Fig 3.6).
- Causes: better lactate clearance, plus improved VO2max and economy raise the speed at which a given lactate value is reached.
- Logic: a given lactate value corresponds to a fixed fraction (e.g. 86%) of VO2max; the same fraction of a higher VO2max or better-economy vVO2max is a faster speed.
- The same applies to heart rate: a specific HR is tied to a specific lactate value; after improvement the same HR corresponds to a faster speed. Subjective effort (RPE) can also be tied to fractions of max HR, aerobic power or lactate.
tags: type:mechanism sport:running system:lactate metric:hr,pace,rpe subtopic:lactate-profile level:all
evidence: author-experience
src: Ch 3 "Running Variables and Improvements" | Figs 3.6, 3.7

### [DRF-C04-009] Easy (E) running: purpose and benefits

- E is conversational, always comfortable; 59-74% VO2max or about 65-79% max HR. Used for L runs, most warm-up and cool-down, and recovery jogs between hard bouts. An E day may be a day off.
- Benefits: builds resistance to injury; ideal for base building when starting or returning after a break; strengthens the heart muscle (maximum force per stroke at about 60% max HR; stroke volume increases only minimally at faster speeds); increases vascularization and muscle-fiber characteristics (accept more oxygen, convert more fuel), which depend on time spent stressing fibers, easier to accumulate at easy pace; builds confidence in lasting long.
- Pace: E range in VDOT tables is about 2-3 min/mile slower than 1-mile race pace. Maintain good mechanics even when going slow; on days when tired or the stride is off, not running may be better than forcing a run and risking injury.
tags: type:mechanism type:principle sport:running system:aerobic metric:hr metric:pace level:all subtopic:easy-running
src: Ch 4 "Easy Running", "Easy Running Pace"
evidence: author-experience

### [DRF-C04-010] Interval (I) training: definition and VO2max time

- Daniels defines I by purpose: maximize aerobic power (VO2max). To improve a function, stress it, so intensity is at or very close to VO2max (and max HR), with work:rest optimized for that.
- Studies with Jimmy Gilbert (VDOT tables): a person can exercise at VO2max for about 11 min. It takes about 90-120 s (about 2 min) from rest/complete recovery to reach VO2max, so good I bouts are 3-5 min (about 3K-5K race pace); can be less than 3 min if recoveries are short.
- Bouts over 5 min are too demanding to repeat at 3K-5K pace. Avoid too-long recovery: VO2 will not fully recover, and the next bout reaches max faster (important for bouts under 2-3 min).
- Fig 4.3 example: at 5:30/mile (292 m/min, 82.5 s per 400) a 5-min run gives about 3 min at max; running faster gives no more time at max; at slower (5:40) pace max is never reached (0 min at max). Going too fast on early reps (e.g., first of five 5-min runs) makes later reps too slow, giving no time at VO2max (about 3 min in reps one and two, none in three to five). Pace beyond vVO2max gives no extra benefit.
- Fig 4.4: with 1-min bouts and short recoveries (about 45 s), VO2 stays elevated so max is reached quickly and time at max accumulates (about 13 min in a 24-min plot). Short bouts require recovery shorter than the bout time at I pace.
tags: type:mechanism type:definition sport:running system:vo2max metric:pace level:all subtopic:interval-training subtopic:time-at-vo2max
src: Ch 4 "Interval Training", "Reaching VO2max", Figs 4.2-4.4
evidence: author-experience

### [DRF-C04-011] Repetition (R) training: purpose and recovery rules

- Purpose: improve anaerobic power, speed and running economy; intensity 105-120% VO2max. Must be recovered enough to run fast with good technique; struggling sacrifices mechanics.
- Shortening recovery (e.g., 10 × 400 at 70 s with 2 instead of 3 min) can make it a worse workout because the purpose (speed with good mechanics) is lost.
- Recovery for distance runners: about 2-3 times the time of the R run (time, not distance); or jog an easy distance equal to the fast run (e.g., jog 400 between R 400s, walking the last 10-20 m).
- R sessions are poorly suited to groups of mixed ability (slower runners cannot keep up; faster runners benefit, slower suffer).
- Cold days: wear extra clothing/jacket during recovery. Sprinters do little mileage (e.g., 2 miles) because they need long recoveries.
- Equalize by time: a runner with R pace 90 s/400 running 8 × 400 spends 2 min more than one at 65 s; 6 × 400 at 90 s matches the stress time of 8 × 400 at 65 s.
tags: type:principle type:decision-rule sport:running system:anaerobic system:neuromuscular metric:pace level:all subtopic:repetition-training subtopic:recovery-intervals
src: Ch 4 "Repetition Training"
evidence: author-experience

### [DRF-C04-012] How VDOT is derived: economy and fraction of max

- Data for the VDOT tables came from years of testing many runners of varied ability. Three key variables: V̇O2max; running economy over at least four submaximal speeds; and the fraction of each runner's V̇O2max at which they performed when racing different distances (durations).
- Fig 5.1: average economy curve (V̇O2 in ml/kg/min vs velocity in m/min; linear rise to V̇O2max; the velocity at V̇O2max is vV̇O2max). Axes: V̇O2 30-90; velocity 230-350 m/min.
- Fig 5.2: curve of fraction of V̇O2max (FV̇O2max) vs running time (15-120 min). It starts above 1.10 at very short durations, passes 1.00 at about 15-20 min and flattens to about 0.85 near 120 min.
- Worked example: the oxygen demand at 6:00/mile is about 51.7 ml/kg/min. A 30-min (5-mile) race has a fraction of .936 (93.6%), so VDOT = 51.7 / .936 = 55.2.
- If a lab-measured max is above the awarded VDOT (e.g. VDOT 56.5, lab 61.6), the runner is less economical than the formula assumed. If the lab max is lower than the VDOT, the runner is more economical than assumed. Either way is fine, because training intensities and race predictions use the same VDOT data with the actual performance times.
- Race times predict training intensities and other race performances far better than lab tests. They reflect max, economy, threshold and mental approach to racing in one measure.
tags: type:mechanism type:formula level:all subtopic:vdot subtopic:running-economy subtopic:fraction-of-vo2max sport:running system:aerobic
src: Ch 5 | pp. n/a
evidence: author-experience (Daniels, Fitts, Sheehan 1978)

### [DRF-C04-013] Effects of altitude on performance (five points)

1. Low-speed endurance events (running longer than about 2 minutes) are slower at altitude: the slight benefit of less dense air does not make up for the loss of aerobic power from less oxygen delivered to muscles.
2. High-speed events (sprints) benefit from less dense air; reduced air resistance more than offsets reduced oxygen pressure.
3. With acclimatization (2 or more weeks) endurance performance at altitude improves. Some runners after 3 weeks raced a mile at altitude more than 10 seconds faster than on initial exposure; some improvement is just learning to race at altitude.
4. Regardless of acclimatization time, low-speed endurance performance never reaches sea-level performance.
5. Some researchers report sea-level performance improves after altitude training, but the author warns of confounders: e.g., students leaving 90 °F/80% humidity and exam stress for 80 °F/10% humidity with only eat, sleep, train, then running 5K PBs at sea level. Was it altitude or everything else that changed?
tags: type:mechanism sport:running level:all subtopic:altitude subtopic:acclimatization
evidence: author-experience
src: Ch 6 "The Effects of Altitude on Performance"

### [DRF-C04-014] Why altitude does not cheat training by intensity (E, R, T, I, M)

Purpose-of-workout reasoning: five intensities E, M, T, I, R at any location.
- E runs (about 85 percent of weekly mileage, about 59 to 74 percent VO2max): a 60 percent effort at 68 percent of reduced altitude max is the same pace; leg power/speed not closely tied to ~70 percent of max, so no difference.
- R runs (about 30 to 90 seconds): altitude does not hurt short fast running if recovery is adequate; purpose is mechanics and speed, which needs adequate rest.
- Longer I and T runs are the only ones slower at altitude. Usually no more than about 10 percent of a week's training is quality work. T purpose (teach body to clear lactate) is still accomplished at slightly slower pace. I purpose (stress aerobic system to maximum): central components (lungs, heart, vessels) work as hard at altitude; cells work as hard as they can with the oxygen available.
- To keep sea-level speed in I work: use a slight downhill course or schedule that phase when not at altitude. Author thinks the learn-to-hurt effect outweighs slightly slower pace.
- Reduced intensity at altitude can help overtraining and minor injury: athletes who train faster than necessary are brought to a pace just right; runners with minor injuries often become injury free after a couple of weeks.
tags: type:mechanism sport:running level:all subtopic:altitude system:threshold system:vo2max system:neuromuscular
evidence: author-experience
src: Ch 6 "Maintaining Speed at Altitude"

### [DRF-C04-015] Fitness loss during a break (Figure 9.1)

- Fitness drops off, usually not as much or as fast as expected, because many physiological benefits earned in regular training are slow to go away (e.g., gains in strength of heart and running muscles, small changes in muscle fibers and the blood vessels feeding them are retained for a while).
- Figure 9.1 (simplified): at the start of a program, relatively little training produces sizable benefits that taper off over time. With a stop in training, benefits are lost slowly at first, so a few days off now and then have little or no negative effect. The chart is a symmetric inverted U: rise over 6 weeks of training, then decline over a 6-week break back to the initial level (markers: start training, 3 weeks, 6 weeks of training, start break, 3-week break, 6-week break).
- This is why tapering (more rest before an important race) can produce a better-than-usual performance.
tags: type:mechanism level:all subtopic:detraining subtopic:taper sport:running
evidence: author-experience
src: Ch 9 "Taking Breaks" | Figure 9.1

### [DRF-C04-016] Physiological demands of 1,500 m to 2 miles

- The 1,500 is a demanding aerobic event that also relies heavily on speed and anaerobic energy systems.
- A hard-raced 1,500 requires an effort about 10 to 12 percent higher than vVO2max.
- The 3,000 m and 2-mile distances are run right at vVO2max, about the speed of demanding I sessions.
- Races of 400, 800, 1,000 m (and even 1,500 and mile) stress anaerobic and speed capabilities more than 3K and 2-mile races.
- The I-intensity effort is one you could maintain in a race lasting about 10 to 12 minutes.
tags: type:mechanism sport:running system:vo2max system:anaerobic level:all subtopic:vvo2max
src: Ch 12 intro; Phase IV; 60-mile Phase III | n/a
evidence: author-experience

### [DRF-C04-017] 5K/10K physiology and race intensity

- The race really starts about two-thirds of the way through the event: in a 5K, be ready to race after the first couple of miles; in a 10K, the race begins at about the 4-mile (6.5 km) mark. Up to that point, see how relaxed you can be while sticking with the planned pace (or competitors).
- Both are primarily aerobic events. 5K races are run at about 95 to 98 percent of VO2max; 10Ks at about 90 to 94 percent of VO2max. Not fun intensities to hold; the mental aspect is important.
- Training must maximize aerobic power, economy of movement and lactate threshold, requiring a good mix of R running, I training and T running. Some runners succeed by emphasizing one system; so spend a fair amount of time on each system to learn which gives the most return for time spent.
- Differences in physiological values between runners (VO2max, economy; see Figure 3.3 in ch. 3) may be inherent or due to training emphasis. Include each type of training so nothing is overlooked or underemphasized.
- Examples:
  - Kenenisa Bekele exceeded the feats of Haile Gebrselassie in part due to running a final lap in slightly over 50 seconds, which he attributed to focused hill and altitude training at relatively low volume (80 miles per week).
  - Molly Huddle achieved American women's records in the 5K and 10K averaging around 85 miles per week supported by a smart nutritional plan.
tags: type:mechanism sport:running system:vo2max system:threshold level:all subtopic:race-intensity
evidence: author-experience
src: Ch 13 intro | n/a

## C05 Glossary

### [DRF-C05-001] Breathing rhythm (e.g., 2-2, 3-3, 2-1, 1-1)

The pattern of breathing coordinated with stride cadence, expressed as the number of running steps taken during inhalation followed by the number of steps taken during exhalation. For example, a 2-2 breathing rhythm consists of taking two steps while breathing in and two steps while breathing out (a complete breathing cycle taking 4 steps).
tags: type:definition sport:running level:all subtopic:breathing-rhythm metric:cadence
src: Ch 2 "Breathing Rhythms"

### [DRF-C05-002] Cruise intervals

"Sessions that involve running a series of shorter-duration runs at T pace, with short recoveries between the T-pace runs, I refer to as cruise intervals." Work-to-recovery ratio is typically 5:1 (e.g., 5 × 1 mile T with 1 min rest, or 3 × 2 miles T with 2 min rest).
tags: type:definition sport:running system:threshold metric:pace level:all subtopic:cruise-intervals
src: Ch 4 "Threshold Running"

### [DRF-C05-003] Dead-space air

The air that does not reach the lungs for gas exchange during inhalation. Because a portion of each breath moved through the mouth and nose is dead-space air, a very fast breathing rate (such as 1-1) results in a greater portion of each breath and of each minute that is not involved in O2 and CO2 exchange.
tags: type:definition sport:running level:all subtopic:ventilation subtopic:dead-space
src: Ch 2 "Breathing Rhythms"

### [DRF-C05-004] Easy (E) running

Running at an intensity of 59% to 74% of VO2max or 65% to 79% of maximum heart rate. Characterized as conversational pace that is always comfortable to manage. Used for long (L) runs, base building, warm-up, cool-down, and recovery jogs between quality bouts.
tags: type:definition sport:running system:aerobic metric:hr metric:pace level:all subtopic:easy-running
src: Ch 4 "Easy Running", "Training Suggestions"

### [DRF-C05-005] Economy curve

The curve representing the VO2 response to increasing running speed; it shows how much aerobic involvement is associated with increasing workloads. More economical runners use less oxygen at the same speed.
tags: type:definition sport:running subtopic:running-economy level:all
src: Ch 3

### [DRF-C05-006] Fast repetitions (FR)

Workouts performed at approximately 115% to 120% of VDOT, at speeds associated with 800-meter race pace. Worth 2.0 points per minute in the Daniels training stress point system.
tags: type:definition sport:running system:anaerobic metric:pace level:all subtopic:fast-repetitions
src: Ch 4 "Tracking Training Intensities"

### [DRF-C05-007] Glossary: abbreviations and units (jg, jog, K, km, m, mph, W, w/) and organizations

- jg — Jogging or slow running.
- jog — Slow running.
- K — Kilometers of distance.
- km — Expression of distance covered in kilometers.
- m — Expression of distance covered in meters.
- mph — Miles per hour.
- W — Walking rather than running.
- w/ — With.
- Athletics West — A former Nike track and field elite-athlete club in Eugene, Oregon.
- Farm Team — A former Nike elite running team in Palo Alto, California.
- Run SMART Project — Running organization that provides training for runners.
tags: type:definition sport:running level:all subtopic:abbreviations
evidence: author-experience
src: Glossary

### [DRF-C05-008] Glossary: intensity and session codes (E, M, T, I, R, H, FR, L, Mod, Q, P, ST)

Author's glossary definitions, as printed:
- E — Refers to easy (low-stress) running or easy day of training.
- M — Marathon (race) pace of running.
- T — Threshold intensity of running, based on current VDOT.
- I — Interval intensity type of repeated bouts of running hard.
- R — Repetition intensity of training (about current mile race pace).
- H — Subjective hard intensity of running.
- FR — Fast repetition training (about 800-meter race speed).
- L — Long (prolonged) run.
- Mod — Moderately long (Mod) pace about 20 to 30 seconds per mile faster than typical E (L) pace.
- Q — Quality (special amount of training stress beyond resting).
- P — Peak of weekly mileage.
- ST — Strides, which are light, short, quick R-pace runs.
tags: type:definition sport:running metric:pace level:all subtopic:intensity-codes
evidence: author-experience
src: Glossary

### [DRF-C05-009] Glossary: physiology and measurement terms (aerobic, BLa, CO2, O2, economy, heart rate, Hgb, RPE, VDOT, VO2, vVO2max)

- aerobic — Production of energy through the use of oxygen.
- BLa — Blood lactate (substance produced if inadequate O2 available).
- CO2 — Carbon dioxide.
- O2 or O — Oxygen.
- economy — Relationship between running speed and energy required.
- heart rate — Number of heart beats per minute associated with exercise.
- Hgb — Hemoglobin value measured in the blood.
- RPE — Rating of perceived exertion as a mental judgment of stress.
- VDOT — Measure of running ability based on race results.
- V̇O2 — Volume of oxygen consumed per minute.
- vV̇O2max — Velocity of running associated with individual V̇O2max.
tags: type:definition sport:running system:aerobic metric:hr metric:rpe level:all subtopic:vdot
evidence: author-experience
src: Glossary

### [DRF-C05-010] Glossary: training-type terms (interval, repetition, cool-down, overstress, stress, supplemental, profile, time, WO)

- interval — Type of training that alternates hard runs and recovery runs.
- repetition — Fairly fast R-pace running alternated with adequate recovery.
- cool-down — Activity performed following a quality training session.
- overstress — Working beyond what is expected of a training workout.
- stress — Imposing a reaction on the body through training.
- supplemental — Additional activity for runners, aside from running.
- profile — Description of the response to various types of training.
- time — Durations of training bouts and recovery bouts in a workout.
- WO — Workout (training session).
- ultra — Running events of extraordinary duration or distance, longer than a marathon.
- elite — High-quality athlete of Olympic caliber.
tags: type:definition sport:running level:all subtopic:training-terms
evidence: author-experience
src: Glossary

### [DRF-C05-011] Hard (H) running

Interval-type running performed by subjective effort for time rather than a fixed distance, at an intensity the runner feels could be sustained for about 10 to 12 minutes in a race. Used frequently for fartlek sessions and when training at altitude to stress the central aerobic system without pace discouragement.
tags: type:definition sport:running system:vo2max metric:rpe level:all subtopic:hard-pace subtopic:altitude
src: Ch 4 "Running Hard"

### [DRF-C05-012] Interval (I) training

Intermittent training performed at or very close to VO2max (and maximum heart rate), corresponding to 95% to 100% of VO2max. Defined specifically by its purpose: to maximize aerobic power (VO2max). Optimal work bouts last 3 to 5 minutes with work-to-rest ratio typically 1:1 or less.
tags: type:definition sport:running system:vo2max metric:pace metric:hr level:all subtopic:interval-training
src: Ch 4 "Interval Training"

### [DRF-C05-013] Marathon-pace (M) running

Training performed at projected marathon race pace, corresponding to 75% to 84% of VO2max or 80% to 89% of maximum heart rate. Serves primarily to adjust mentally to race pace and practice fluid/energy intake at speed.
tags: type:definition sport:running system:aerobic metric:pace metric:hr level:all subtopic:marathon-pace
src: Ch 4 "Marathon-Pace Running"

### [DRF-C05-014] Mod (moderately long) pace

Mod (moderately long) pace is about 20 to 30 seconds per mile faster than you typically go on E and L runs. Used in phase I Q sessions.
tags: type:definition sport:running metric:pace subtopic:mod-pace level:all
src: Ch 12 Phase I (30 and 45 miles) | n/a

### [DRF-C05-015] Physiological acclimatization vs competitive acclimatization

Two types of acclimatization with altitude training. Physiological (e.g., learning to ventilate larger amounts of air): lost after some time back at sea level. Competitive (learning how to race at altitude): much is not lost, even after weeks or months at sea level; stays fairly permanently, like learning to race a new distance (a miler adjusting to the 5K).
tags: type:definition sport:running level:all subtopic:acclimatization
evidence: author-experience
src: Ch 6 "The Effects of Altitude on Performance"

### [DRF-C05-016] Plan notation (Ch 8)

- W = walk. E = easy running. L = long run, easy and steady.
- ST (strides) = light, quick 15- to 20-second runs (not sprinting), with 45 to 60 seconds of rest between strides. Strides improve running economy and prepare for faster running.
- I (intervals) = hard runs at a pace you could race at for 10 to 15 minutes. Blue plan: "hard" runs are interval in nature at that same pace.
- T = threshold pace, comfortably hard, a pace you could handle for 40 minutes.
- R = repetition pace, a pace you could race at for 5 minutes (blue plan definition).
- jg = jog, at an easy pace. K = kilometer. H = hard (3 min H = 3 min hard).
- Bolded workouts in the tables mark the minimum participation (white and red plans) or the recommended quality days (blue and gold).
tags: type:definition level:all subtopic:notation sport:running
src: Ch 8 Tables 8.1-8.4 intros
evidence: author-experience

### [DRF-C05-017] Repetition (R) training

Intermittent training at 105% to 120% of VO2max (105-110% VDOT) designed primarily to improve anaerobic power, speed, and running economy. Work bouts are limited to about 2 minutes maximum, with generous recovery (2 to 3 times work duration or equal jog distance) so that each bout is run with good mechanics without struggling.
tags: type:definition sport:running system:anaerobic system:neuromuscular metric:pace level:all subtopic:repetition-training
src: Ch 4 "Repetition Training", "Tracking Training Intensities"

### [DRF-C05-018] RPE (rating of perceived exertion)

Relating numbers to feeling of comfort or discomfort. Example scales: 1-10 (1-3 easy; 4-5 comfortable hard; 6-8 degrees of hard; 9-10 very hard) or a 5-point scale (1 easy, 5 hardest).
tags: type:definition sport:running metric:rpe level:all
src: Ch 3

### [DRF-C05-019] Strides (ST) as used in Ch 12

Strides (ST) are 15- to 20-second light, quick runs on the flat or up a gradual hill, not all-out sprints. Take full recoveries between strides. When done uphill, finish the final two on flat ground or a track to feel good leg turnover; take care coming down.
tags: type:definition sport:running system:neuromuscular subtopic:strides level:all
src: Ch 12 Phase I | n/a

### [DRF-C05-020] Stroke volume

The amount of blood pumped with each heartbeat. Daniels notes that stroke volume reaches its maximum force of each stroke when heart rate reaches approximately 60% of maximum; beyond this intensity, stroke volume increases minimally while heart rate continues to rise.
tags: type:definition type:mechanism sport:running system:aerobic metric:hr level:all subtopic:stroke-volume
src: Ch 4 "Easy Running"

### [DRF-C05-021] Tempo run

Daniels defines a tempo run as "any run during which the pace of the entire run is performed at T pace, and when a runner progresses from an easier pace to true T intensity, only the T-pace portion of that overall run is tempo. I consider T-pace runs that last about a steady 20 minutes as true tempo runs." Differs from coaches who use the term for runs where only the final miles reach threshold intensity.
tags: type:definition sport:running system:threshold metric:pace level:all subtopic:tempo
src: Ch 4 "Threshold Running"

### [DRF-C05-022] Threshold (T) running

Running at a "comfortably hard" intensity, corresponding to 85% to 88% of VO2max (88% to 92% of maximum heart rate for well-trained athletes; 80% to 86% for lesser-trained runners). A pace maintainable for about 60 minutes when peaked and rested. Designed to improve lactate clearance and endurance.
tags: type:definition sport:running system:threshold system:lactate metric:pace metric:hr level:all subtopic:threshold-running
src: Ch 4 "Threshold Running"

### [DRF-C05-023] Ultrarace (definition of distance)

Technically, anything longer than a marathon is considered an ultra. The 50K distance is the most common distance where ultrarunning begins.
tags: type:definition sport:running level:all subtopic:ultrarunning
evidence: author-experience
src: Ch 17 Q&A

### [DRF-C05-024] VDOT

Originally a short form for the V̇O2max value. V̇ has a dot because the volume is a 1-minute volume (V̇O2 = "V dot O2"). Without the dot, the volume may be measured over more or less than 1 minute, so volumes are converted to 1-minute values to make them comparable. In the tables, VDOT is a calculated (pseudo) V̇O2max derived from race performance, depending on a representative running economy. The higher VDOT is associated with the better runner regardless of age or sex, because VDOT represents performance.
tags: type:definition level:all subtopic:vdot sport:running
src: Ch 5 | intro, "Using VDOT to Establish Training Intensities"
evidence: author-experience

### [DRF-C05-025] vVO2max (velocity at VO2max)

The speed of running associated with a runner's combined VO2max and running economy; Daniels' term, a better predictor of potential performance than economy or VO2max alone.
tags: type:definition sport:running subtopic:vvo2max level:all
src: Ch 3

## C06 Quantitative Reference

### [DRF-C06-001] Reference values: lactate, heart rate and vVO2max fractions

- Race effort for about 30 minutes: about 93% of vVO2max.
- Blood lactate of 4.0 mmol: typically at about 85 to 87% of VO2max in well-trained runners (Fig 3.4 runner); text elsewhere cites a specific lactate value at 86% of VO2max.
- Example: HR 164 at 4.0 mmol lactate, which is about 88 to 90% of max HR.
- Fig 3.7 runner: VO2max 75, max HR 190; at 4.0 mmol (considered lactate threshold for this subject) VO2 = 66 ml/kg/min and HR = 170 bpm; lactate and RPE rise steeply after about 315 m/min; vVO2max about 353 m/min.
- Example RPE scales: 1-10 (easy 1 to 3; comfortable hard 4 or 5; 6, 7, 8 different degrees of hard; 9 and 10 very hard) or a 5-point scale (1 easy, 5 hardest).
tags: type:threshold sport:running system:lactate metric:hr,rpe subtopic:lactate-threshold level:all
evidence: author-experience
src: Ch 3 | Figs 3.4, 3.6, 3.7

### [DRF-C06-002] Hemoglobin reference values

- Hemoglobin (Hgb) carries oxygen; important for endurance athletes.
- Typical normal values: 12 to 18 g/dL of blood (depends on age and gender).
- Anemic: below 13.5 (men) or 12 (women) (per Daniels, g/dL).
- Performance: the difference between Hgb 12 and 13 could be about 30 to 40 seconds in a 5K.
- Normal Hgb is mainly good nutrition and iron-containing foods.
tags: type:threshold sport:running metric:hr subtopic:hemoglobin subtopic:iron level:all
evidence: author-experience
src: Ch 3 "Hemoglobin Content"

### [DRF-C06-003] Figure 4.1 Types of training (E, M, T, I, R)

E, M, T, I, R = Easy, Marathon-pace, Threshold, Interval, Repetition.

| Type | Intensity (%VO2max) | Duration per workout / bout | Weekly-mileage share per session | Work:Recovery | Benefit |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| E | 59-74% | 30-150 min | 25-30% of weekly miles | n/a | Heart and periphery (muscle) |
| M | 75-84% | 40-110 min | 15-20% | n/a | Practice pace |
| T | 85-88% | 20 min max steady, or 5-30 min (cruise intervals) | 10% | 5:1 W/R | Endurance |
| I | 95-100% | 5 min max per bout | lesser of 10K and 8% | 1:1 W/R | Aerobic power |
| R | 105-120% | 2 min max per bout | lesser of 5 miles and 5% | 1:2-3 W/R | Anaerobic power, economy, speed |

Heart-rate equivalents given in text: E about 65-79% max HR; M about 80-89% max HR; T about 88-92% max HR (well-trained); I at or very near max HR.
Adapted from J. Daniels and N. Scardina, Sports Medicine 1(4), 1984: 327-334.
tags: type:zones sport:running system:aerobic system:threshold system:vo2max system:anaerobic metric:hr metric:pace metric:volume level:all subtopic:training-intensities
src: Ch 4 Figure 4.1
evidence: author-experience

### [DRF-C06-004] Per-session limits for each intensity

- L (long) run: below 40 miles (64 km)/week: no more than 30% of weekly mileage. 40+ miles/week: lesser of 25% of weekly mileage or 150 min. Longest steady run (non-ultra) 150 min (2.5 h).
- E run minimum: 30 min.
- M run: lesser of 110 min or 18 miles (29 km); M-pace running in one session not more than lesser of 20% of weekly mileage or 18 miles.
- T: not more than 10% of weekly mileage in one workout; steady tempo about 20 min; cruise intervals up to 30 min total (for runners who can do a steady 20 min T); well-trained runners may do two or three 20-min T runs in one session.
- I/H: lesser of 10K or 8% of weekly mileage (e.g., 40 mi/week gives 3.2 miles). Over 75 miles/week the maximum is 10K (about 30 min). Bouts normally 3-5 min.
- R: lesser of 5 miles (8 km) or 5% of weekly mileage (30 mi/week gives 1.5 miles). Over 100 miles/week keep max at 5 miles (not 6 at 120). Single bouts no longer than about 2 min (reps 200-600 mostly; 800s only if R pace is about 60 s/400, i.e., near or under 4:00 milers).
tags: type:threshold type:dose sport:running metric:volume metric:load level:all subtopic:session-limits
src: Ch 4 sections on each intensity
evidence: author-experience

### [DRF-C06-005] Marathon pace (M): estimating pace, purpose, practice

- M pace = projected marathon race pace; M is 75-84% VO2max, about 80-89% max HR.
- Estimate M pace from VDOT tables (chapter 5); use a recent longer race (half marathon better than a mile). Alternative: from recent serious 10K races, M pace is about 3 min per 10K slower than 10K race pace.
- Purpose: adjust to the specific race pace, practice drinking/fueling at that pace, build confidence. Physiological benefits are not different from E running. Non-marathoners can use M runs for confidence in a prolonged run slightly faster than E.
- Mix M with E and T running; accumulated M time may then be less than a steady M run.
- On some long steady E runs, do not take energy drinks so the body learns to conserve carbohydrate/glycogen and use more fat; still take water. In the marathon itself fuel is helpful, and M sessions are the time to practice it.
tags: type:zones type:principle sport:running system:aerobic metric:pace metric:hr level:all subtopic:marathon-pace subtopic:fueling-practice
src: Ch 4 "Marathon-Pace Running"
evidence: author-experience

### [DRF-C06-006] Threshold (T) running: definition, pace, tempo vs cruise intervals

- T is "comfortably hard": hard but manageable for 20-30 min; rested, peaked racing at T pace lasts about 60 min (elites run 20K/half marathon at T pace).
- Physiological: 85-88% VO2max (88-92% max HR) for well-trained; 80-86% for lesser-trained.
- Purpose: improve ability to clear blood lactate and keep it below a manageable level; improve endurance (hold a slightly demanding pace longer).
- Check for first-time T: ask whether the pace could be held 30-40 min; if not, slow down. Hard (not comfortably hard) belongs to I.
- Tempo run = steady run of about 20 min entirely at T pace (builds confidence). Runs that start slower and build up to T (e.g., 10 miles with only last 4-5 miles at T) count as tempo only for the T portion. A 60-min tempo is not a training run.
- Cruise intervals = series of T-pace runs with short rests; work:recovery about 5:1; more total time at T intensity. Do not run them faster than T; to add stress, shorten the recovery. Use same T pace from VDOT tables.
- Examples: 5 x 1 mile T with 1 min rests (5 × 1 T with 1 min rests); 3 × 2 T with 2 min rests (1-mile reps get 1 min rest, 2-mile reps get 2 min).
- Advanced: up to 15 miles (24 km) at T in a session for runners at about 150 miles/week: 5 mi T, 5 min rest, 4 mi T, 4 min, 3 mi T, 3 min, 2 mi T, 2 min, 1 mi T.
tags: type:zones type:definition sport:running system:threshold system:lactate metric:pace metric:hr level:all subtopic:tempo subtopic:cruise-intervals
src: Ch 4 "Threshold Running"
evidence: author-experience

### [DRF-C06-007] Hard (H) running and I session volume rules

- H runs = hard runs by feel, for time rather than distance; pace should feel maintainable about 10-12 min. Example notation: 6 × 3 min H with 2 min jg (jg = recovery jog).
- Max per I/H session: lesser of 10K or 8% weekly mileage; 5 min of H = 1 mile when counting miles in an I session. Over 75 miles/week max is 10K (about 30 min).
- Recovery jogs usually a little shorter than the H run; may be equal, never longer.
- Use H runs more at altitude: VO2max pace is much slower there and discouraging; running hard by feel stresses the central aerobic system without worrying about pace.
- Varying H work totaling 20 min: 2 × 4 min H w/ 3 min jg + 4 × 3 min H w/ 2 min jg; or 1 × 4 H w/ 3 jg + 2 × 3 H w/ 2 jg + 3 × 2 H w/ 1 jg + 4 × 1 H w/ 30 sec jg.
tags: type:zones type:dose sport:running system:vo2max metric:rpe level:all subtopic:hard-pace subtopic:altitude
src: Ch 4 "Running Hard"
evidence: author-experience

### [DRF-C06-014] Moderate altitude range and VO2max/performance loss at altitude

- Moderate altitude: generally 1,200 to 2,500 meters (about 4,000 to 8,000 feet). Author's studies mostly at 2,130-2,255 m (7,000-7,400 ft).
- Real altitude problems do not begin until about 3,000 ft; 7,000 ft imposes almost double the stress of 5,000 ft; going from 5,000 to 7,000 ft is about the same as sea level to 5,000 ft.
- On arrival at altitude, VO2max is reduced about 12 to 16 percent, but performance only about 6 to 8 percent, because running cost (aerobic demand) is lower in less dense air: some aerobic power is lost, some regained in running economy.
- Figure 6.1 (line graph, VO2 vs running velocity m/min): sea-level and altitude economy curves; altitude curve lies below; altitude VO2max is -12% vs sea level; velocity at VO2max (vVO2max) is -6%. Runners lose about half as much in vVO2max/performance as in VO2max.
tags: type:threshold sport:running level:all subtopic:altitude subtopic:vo2max metric:pace
evidence: author-experience

### [DRF-C06-019] Table 9.1 VDOT Adjustments for Training Breaks

FVDOT-1 = no leg-associated aerobic training during the break. FVDOT-2 = break included leg-associated aerobic cross-training. Multiply pre-break VDOT by the factor.

| Training break | FVDOT-1 | FVDOT-2 |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Up to 5 days | 1.000 | 1.000 |
| 6 days | .997 | .998 |
| 7 days | .994 | .997 |
| 10 days | .985 | .992 |
| 14 days | .973 | .986 |
| 21 days | .952 | .976 |
| 28 days | .931 | .965 |
| 35 days | .910 | .955 |
| 42 days | .889 | .944 |
| 49 days | .868 | .934 |
| 56 days | .847 | .923 |
| 63 days | .826 | .913 |
| 70 days | .805 | .902 |
| 72 days or more | .800 | .900 |

Notes: no loss with up to 5 days off. At 42 days, VDOT drops about 11% without leg training (.889); with good cross-training the loss is about half (.944). After about 10 weeks off, you have probably lost about as much as you will, about 20%; but unnecessary weight gain lowers VDOT further until back to ideal running weight. Current VDOT dictates training speeds. Source: J. Daniels, Daniels' Running Formula, 4th ed. (2022).

### [DRF-C06-021] 800 m plans: pace-setting rules by phase (all mileage levels)

Phase I (20-60 mile plans): Use recent race time for VDOT to set paces. If no recent race, make a conservative estimate of current 1-mile race time and treat as R pace. I pace = 8 s per 400 slower than R. T pace = 8 s per 400 slower than I.
Phase III: R pace = 1 s per 200 (2 s per 400, 3 s per 600) faster than during last 3 weeks of phase II. I pace: from recent race/VDOT, or 8 s per 400 slower than new R. T pace = 16 s per 400 slower than new R (8 s per 400 slower than I).
FR (fast repetitions) vs current R pace:
- 20-30 and 40 mile plans: 3 s per 200, 6 s per 400, 12 s per 600 faster.
- 50-60 mile plan: 2 to 3 s per 200, 4 to 6 s per 400, 9 to 12 s per 600 faster.
Phase IV: R pace again 1 s per 200 (2 s/400, 3 s/600) faster than last 3 weeks of phase III; phase III info otherwise applies.
Definitions used: H (hard) = I-pace effort. Mod (moderately long) / M-labelled run in 20-30 plan: about 20 to 30 seconds per mile faster than typical E (L) pace. Strides (ST): light, quick 15- to 20-second runs (not sprints) with 45 to 60 seconds recovery; may be on gradual uphill, be careful coming back down. Jg = jogging or easy running. Numbers with no distance unit = miles (e.g. 2 T = 2 miles threshold; 2 E = 2 miles easy). Phase III/IV: eight ST (flat or uphill) during two weekly E runs.
tags: type:zones sport:running metric:pace level:all subtopic:R-I-T-pace-relations subtopic:fast-repetitions subtopic:strides
evidence: author-experience
src: Ch 11 mileage sections

### [DRF-C06-022] Session limits per workout type (as restated in Ch 15)

- R-pace running in a single session: no more than the lesser of 8K (5 miles) or 5 percent of weekly running mileage.
- I-pace running in a single session: no more than the lesser of 10K or 8 percent of weekly mileage.
- T runs: total no more than 10 percent of weekly mileage.
tags: type:dose sport:running level:all subtopic:session-limits system:threshold system:vo2max system:neuromuscular metric:volume
evidence: author-experience
src: Ch 15 intro

### [DRF-C06-023] 2Q program: choosing training paces (VDOT method and non-VDOT method)

**VDOT method:** use a realistic VDOT from a race of at least 10K; the longer and more recent the race, the better. If it has been a while, conservatively estimate what you could race on terrain similar to training and the marathon.
- First 6 weeks: use the lesser of (VDOT equal to a recent race) and (2 VDOT units lower than anticipated marathon VDOT).
- Second 6 weeks: increase VDOT by 1 unit.
- Final 6 weeks: increase another VDOT unit.

**Non-VDOT method:** choose a realistic goal M pace.
- Final T pace = 15 seconds per mile faster than goal M pace.
- Final I pace = 6 seconds per 400 m faster than T pace.
- Final R pace = 3 seconds per 200 m faster than I pace.
- Example: M 6:00 per mile (3:43/km) → final T 5:45/mile (about 86 sec/400 and 3:35/km); final I 80 sec/400 or 3:20/km; final R 74/400 and 37/200.
- First 6 weeks: paces 10 seconds per mile (6 sec/km) slower than goal and final paces. Middle 6 weeks: within 4 seconds per mile (2.5 sec/km) of goal and final paces. Final 6 weeks: goal and final paces.
tags: type:formula sport:running level:intermediate metric:pace subtopic:vdot subtopic:pace-progression
src: Ch 16 Marathon | 2Q Program
evidence: author-experience

### [DRF-C06-024] Training pace selection for the 26-week marathon plan (VDOT or goal M pace)

**Using VDOT**
- Be realistic; use a VDOT from a race of at least 10K. The longer and more recent the race, the better. If it has been a while since a race, conservatively estimate what you could race on terrain similar to training and the marathon.
- First 8 weeks: use the lesser of (the VDOT equal to a recent race) and (3 VDOT units lower than the anticipated marathon VDOT).
- Each additional 8 weeks: increase the VDOT by 1 unit.
- If a race or two suggests a VDOT greater than the previous value plus 1, the race VDOT may be used, particularly if the course was legitimate.

**Not using VDOT** (choose a realistic goal M pace)
- Final T pace = goal M pace minus 15 seconds per mile.
- Final I pace = T pace minus 6 seconds per 400 m.
- Final R pace = I pace minus 3 seconds per 200 m.
- Example: M = 6:00/mile (3:43/km) gives final T 5:45/mile (about 86 sec/400, 3:35/km); final I 80 sec/400 or 3:20/km; final R 74/400 and 37/200.
- Pace phasing: first 8 weeks, training paces 10 seconds per mile slower than goal and final paces; middle 8 weeks, within 4 seconds per mile (2.5 sec/km) of goal and final paces; final 10 weeks use the goal and final paces.
tags: type:zones type:formula sport:running metric:pace system:threshold system:vo2max level:all subtopic:vdot subtopic:pace-progression
evidence: author-experience
src: Ch 16 "4-Week Cycle"

### [DRF-C06-025] Pace definitions and relationships used in the 5-week cycle

- E: easy, conversational pace; used in most L runs and in warm-up, cool-down and recovery runs between faster bouts.
- T: threshold pace, comfortably hard; estimate from VDOT tables (chapter 5). Cruise intervals = repeated T-pace runs with short rest.
- I (interval pace): subjectively hard, a pace you could race at for 10 to 12 minutes; from VDOT tables.
- R (repetition pace): current mile or 1,500 race pace.
- M: marathon pace, from anticipated M race pace or VDOT tables.
- In general: I pace is about 6 to 8 seconds per 400 slower than R; T pace is 6 to 8 seconds per 400 slower than I. M pace is 12 seconds per mile slower than T for faster runners and 15 to 20 seconds per mile slower than T for slower runners.
- Strides (ST): 15- to 20-second light, quick (or uphill) runs with 45 to 60 seconds rest between them.
