## C07 Testing & Assessment

### [DRF-C07-001] Determining maximum heart rate (hill or 800 m repeats)

- Age formulas (e.g. 220 minus age; for 50 years = 170) may work for large groups but can mislead for an individual. Know your personal max HR if using fractions of max HR.
- Examples: a male runner had max HR 148 at age 30 and 146 at age 55 (86% would be 163 by formula, impossible). Another had 186 at age 25 and 192 at age 50.
- Test (easiest for runners): run several hard 2-minute uphill runs. Take the HR reading at the top of the first run. If the second is higher, do a third; if the third is higher, a fourth, and so on until HR does not increase over the previous run. That reading is the max.
- If no hill: a few 800 m runs at a solid pace, comparing repeated efforts in the same way.
tags: type:test sport:running metric:hr subtopic:max-hr level:all
evidence: author-experience
src: Ch 3 "Maximum Heart Rate"

### [DRF-C07-002] Creating your own treadmill intensity table (1-5 rating)

Procedure: on each treadmill session try different speed/grade combinations and record how demanding each feels.
- Start at 6 mph with 2.5% grade, then 6 mph at 5%, then 7.5, 10, 12.5, 15, 17.5, finally 20%. In another session try 7 mph at a variety of grades, then 8, 9, 10 mph with whatever the treadmill allows.
- For each combination run steady 5 or 6 minutes; in the final minute record a number 1-5: 1 = very easy, 2 = some demand, 3 = comfortably hard, 4 = hard, 5 = very hard or maximal effort.
- Equivalence: 1 = what you could easily do for an L run; 2 = about M pace; 3 = T pace; 4 = about 5K race effort; 5 = about what you could handle for 5 to 6 minutes.
- Do not try many combinations in one session. Build a table of suitable combinations for different workouts; it takes weeks to fill. Once doing a couple of sessions per week, what felt hard may become easier, so be willing to change settings used for intensities.
- Heart rate can be recorded with each combination: 1 = up to 80% of max HR, 2 = 81 to 85%, 3 = 86 to 90%, 4 = 91 to 99%, 5 = max HR, or about equal to current mile race pace effort.
- Daniels notes this "ministudy" makes time fly.
tags: type:test sport:running metric:rpe,hr level:all subtopic:treadmill-intensity-table
evidence: author-experience
src: Ch 7 "Treadmill Training Intensity"

### [DRF-C07-003] Treadmill calibration procedure

Purpose: know true belt speed for I, T and other quality workouts.
A. Belt length:
1. Power off. Mark the belt side with white tape; lay a measuring tape along the belt, pushing it slowly around (pencil marks to track distance) until back at the start. Record total to nearest quarter inch or 0.5 cm. If in feet/inches, convert inches to cm by × 2.54. Example: 542.9 cm.
2. Meters = cm / 100 (542.9/100 = 5.429 m).
3. D = belt length × 10 = distance of 10 revolutions (5.429 × 10 = 54.29 m).
4. Put a visible identifying mark (e.g., duct tape) at the start point of the belt, visible when moving fast.
B. Timing 10 revolutions:
1. Belt at desired speed; choose a fixed spot at which timing starts when the mark reaches it.
2. Start stopwatch when mark reaches the spot (Daniels often starts/stops when mark disappears off the end, hard alone). Ideally time with someone running on the belt, since a runner can slow the belt; best if someone other than the runner times.
3. Count each time the mark passes the spot, starting at 0 when the watch starts; stop on count of 10. Repeat until timings agree within a few tenths of a second.
4. T = 10-revolution time, D = distance. Speed: V (m/min) = (D × 60) / T. Example: T = 13.03 s: (54.29 × 60)/13.03 = 250 m/min.
5. For a target V, T = (D × 60) / V. Example: 268 m/min: 54.29 × 60 / 268 = 12.15 s. So 268 m/min (6:00 mile pace) is achieved by adjusting speed until 10 revolutions take 12.15 s.
tags: type:test type:formula sport:running metric:pace level:all subtopic:treadmill-calibration
evidence: author-experience
src: Ch 7 "Treadmill Calibration"

### [DRF-C07-004] Speed versus endurance assessment (Table 10.1 use)

Procedure: in Table 10.1 circle your best times in the 400 m, 800 m and 1,500/mile columns, then connect the circles.
Interpretation:
- Line straight across: speed and endurance equal.
- Slopes down to the right (typical of young runners): speed better than endurance.
- Slopes up to the right: endurance better than speed.
- Down from 400 to 800 then up to 1,500: better speed and endurance than the 800 suggests; should be able to race a better 800.
- Other up-and-down lines: a lower event has been raced less or is of less interest; a higher event means the body is better designed (possibly muscle fiber type) for that distance, or it was raced most often or with more enthusiasm.
Example: 400 = 60 s, 800 = 2:20.8, 1,500 = 5:06 means speed better than endurance; work more on endurance.
Training logic: work on the weakness first. If after a season the slope does not flatten somewhat, the runner is likely a speed type; then concentrate on the strength (speed), which may improve both, with slope staying about the same. Re-check the table each season end to see how speed and endurance respond.
tags: type:test type:decision-rule sport:running level:all subtopic:speed-vs-endurance
evidence: author-experience
src: Ch 10 | p. [n/a]

## C08 Methodology & Progression

### [DRF-C08-001] Principle 4: Training response, 6-8 weeks per stress level, and the four training variables

- A new, more demanding stress raises fitness quickly at first, then the benefit tapers; if the same training continues, fitness stops improving. Example (Fig 2.1): starting runner able to run ~30 min and repeat 1-mile runs at 8:00; program 3 × 1 mile at 8:00 with 10 min rests, 3 days/week; curve rises sharply in weeks 1-2 and plateaus around week 6.
- Benefit of a given stress is typically attained in about 6 to 8 weeks. To reach a higher level, stress must increase again (Fig 2.2: more stress every 6-8 weeks, each step rising then plateauing; a third step may bring "No improvement?"). Some runners deliberately hold steady fitness until later in the year or before a competitive season.
- Four variables of a program: Workload (total work; 3 miles in the example); Intensity factor (speed, 8:00 per mile); Recovery (10 min after each 8-min mile); Frequency (3 times/week).
- All sessions have at least workload, intensity and frequency; steady runs lack recovery (single bout).
- Ways to increase stress (change only ONE variable): raise workload (4+ one-mile runs instead of 3); raise intensity (7:40 per mile with load held at 3 miles); cut recovery to 5 min; raise frequency to 4 or 5 days/week.
- Number of repeats in a session should be a function of weekly mileage. With stable weekly mileage, repeat count usually stays; more likely speed rises with recovery held similar.
- Improvement after each increase follows the same pattern: fast at first, then tapering over weeks.
- When increasing stress, stay at a chosen degree of stress 6 to 8 weeks before changing. It is a mistake to try to make every week or workout better than the last; better that runners say a workout is starting to feel too easy than struggle to go faster each time. Let race performances indicate when to increase stress.
tags: type:principle type:methodology sport:running level:all subtopic:training-response subtopic:progression subtopic:workload subtopic:intensity subtopic:recovery subtopic:frequency metric:load
evidence: author-experience
src: Ch 2, Principle 4; Figs 2.1, 2.2; "Training Plan Development"

### [DRF-C08-002] Scheduling with twice-weekly races; back-to-back quality days

- Question: racing every Tuesday and Saturday in cross country; when to train? Answer: (1) races are a serious part of training with physiological and psychological benefits; a 4,000-5,000 m race (15-20 min) twice weekly is about the optimal stress on systems taxed by interval sessions, so little or no interval training is needed then. (2) Do a workout on Wednesday after the Tuesday race.
- Rationale: muscular discomfort peaks about 48 hours after stress, not 24, so a Wednesday quality session can be done well, even better than Thursday. Benefits: 2 easy days before a Saturday race and 2 easy days after it before next Tuesday; knowing another quality session follows tends to stop runners over-running the first day (example: Tue 6 × 1,000 m at 3:20 with 3-min recoveries; Wed 6 × 1 mile at 5:44 with 1-min rests); prepares for 2-day track meets with races on consecutive days.
tags: type:methodology type:decision-rule sport:running level:all phase:build subtopic:back-to-back-quality subtopic:racing-schedule
evidence: author-experience
src: Ch 2 "Training Plan Development"

### [DRF-C08-003] Gather a personal profile before planning training

- Before setting up a program, runners or coaches should gather information about past and current fitness and time availability. Daniels collects it from all his runners; it is critical when coaching by email and for school/college/club coaches.
- Knowing current or recent mileage and workouts allows appropriate training loads and intensities; know the most important races in the weeks and months ahead.
- Write down available facilities (even as a school coach) to plan for any weather. Individual or team profiles help plot a season; Daniels refers to profiles when writing season schedules.
tags: type:methodology sport:running subtopic:runner-profile level:all
evidence: author-experience
src: Ch 3 "Personal Training and Racing Profiles"

### [DRF-C08-004] Increasing weekly running amounts

- Common advice of +10% per week is not preferred (10 mi to 14.6 mi in 4 weeks). Daniels prefers staying at the initial amount (e.g., 10 miles) for 4 weeks, then adding a larger step (e.g., about 5 more miles).
- Stay at any weekly time/mileage for at least 3 or 4 weeks before increasing.
- Once the season's maximum is reached, weekly amounts may go up or down with weather or available time; the maintenance principle means a small decrease does not hurt.
- L runs should be built up very gradually and stay similar for several weeks; reduce an L run when feeling poor, when conditions make it much more stressful, or before a race.
- E days are for recovery from Q (quality) sessions; do not replace an E session with an extra Q session. A day off counts as part of training.
- Example: 40-mile week = 10-mile L + 8 miles total on two quality days; remaining 14 miles over 4 days (3-5 E miles each, or 5-6 on two days, 3 on one, one day off).
tags: type:methodology type:principle sport:running metric:volume level:all subtopic:mileage-progression
src: Ch 4 "Training Suggestions", "Long Runs and Increasing Mileage"
evidence: author-experience

### [DRF-C08-005] Altitude training: volume and speed adjustments

- No need to change training amount from sea-level norm (e.g., 80 miles/129 km per week continues). Time spent on T, I, R runs can remain unchanged. Do not immediately increase mileage because you have more time; progress as usual.
- Speeds are a little slower because they relate to a reduced maximum aerobic power.
- R pace: keep sea-level speed; may need longer recovery between R runs (purpose is speed and economy).
- I (true interval sessions): slow longer intervals by 3 or 4 seconds per 400, normal recovery.
- T pace: slow by about 12 to 16 seconds per mile (8 to 10 sec/km).
- E and L runs: go by feel with normal breathing pattern; breathing effort should be no more stressful than at sea level.
tags: type:dose sport:running level:all subtopic:altitude-pace-adjustment metric:pace metric:volume
evidence: author-experience
src: Ch 6 "Altitude Training and Racing Considerations", "Incorporating Altitude Training"

### [DRF-C08-006] Alternating between altitude and sea level

- Distance runners improve altitude performance considerably over 2-3 weeks at altitude; more when periodically training/racing at sea level during a series of stays.
- Pattern that works: a few weeks at altitude, then a week or so at sea level, then back. Daily up and down is not necessary.
- Benefits: psychological boost from realizing fitness was not lost; often better sea-level performance in those days ("learned to hurt": same hurt now corresponds to a faster time). Author believes learning to hurt is among the most significant benefits of altitude.
- Fitness loss on return: author believes gains are real fitness, not temporary; loss usually occurs because runners finish their season and quit training. Examples: athletes left after a month, raced in Europe, returned and raced faster at altitude; a runner spent 6 weeks at altitude, set a 5K PB at home, stayed at sea level 10 months, ran many PBs, became national and Pan American Games champion without returning.
- Keep stressing the necessary systems; backing off mileage a little does not mean loss.
- Sea-level trips of as much as a week at a time recommended, for mental and physiological benefit.
tags: type:periodization sport:running level:all subtopic:altitude subtopic:live-high-intermittent
evidence: author-experience
src: Ch 6 "Alternating Between Altitude and Sea Level"

### [DRF-C08-007] Steady running vs intermittent running

Only two types of training exist: steady running (nonstop at consistent intensity) and intermittent (work/recovery) running.
- Steady: may be very easy (warm-up, cool-down, recovery between harder bouts) or moderate (M pace or T pace).
- Any intensity greater than T pace is usually intermittent (I or R workouts), which stress the cardiovascular system maximally or work on mechanics, speed and economy. Recovery may or may not involve light work.
- Usually the harder the work, the more recovery time; easy to control on a treadmill.
- A session mixing intensities, durations and recoveries is typically called fartlek.
tags: type:methodology sport:running metric:pace level:all subtopic:steady-vs-intermittent
evidence: author-experience
src: Ch 7 "Steady Running and Intermittent Running"

### [DRF-C08-008] White plan: structure and use guidelines

- 16 weeks, 4 phases of 4 weeks. Sessions start at 30 min and top out at 45 min. Daily running is allowed but not required.
- 3 or 4 days per week produce positive results. With 3 days, spread them over at least a 5-day period rather than 3 in a row then 4 off; 3 consecutive days followed by 4 off is still better than not running.
- Strides are added later in the plan. Mixing strides into E runs breaks up steady running; do them where footing is flat and soft.
- If phase I is minimal stress, jump to a later phase after a couple of weeks. If phase IV is also minimal discomfort, consider the red plan.
- After 16 weeks: repeat phase IV, or repeat favorite sessions.
- Low-key road races become possible after the plan.
tags: type:methodology level:beginner subtopic:white-plan sport:running phase:base
src: Ch 8 "White Starting Plan"
evidence: author-experience

### [DRF-C08-009] Red intermediate plan: structure and use guidelines

- For runners who completed the white plan, or who are running and can handle a bit more stress. It prepares for recreational track or road races, including an hour or a little longer. Short races are fine afterward, but a bit more training is advised before a marathon (see chapter 16).
- Minimum 4 training days per week (bolded in the original). Extra days are suggested. Days may be shuffled for time or weather.
- With 4 days, avoid 3 in a row. With 5 days, generally separate the 2 non-training days, though 2 days off in a row is not necessarily bad.
- If you race during the plan, find the VDOT from the race time and use the associated training paces (VDOT tables, chapter 5).
- If starting red without white, read through the white plan; also look at blue.
- After finishing: you should know the feel of E, T and I paces and the L run. Options: blue/gold, distance-specific plans, or a break with E runs only.
- After a total break of some weeks, restart with a few weeks of E running only before adding quality sessions.
tags: type:methodology level:intermediate subtopic:red-plan sport:running
src: Ch 8 "Red Intermediate Plan"
evidence: author-experience

### [DRF-C08-010] Blue advanced plan: structure and use guidelines

- For runners who recently completed red or have considerable experience, including occasional races.
- 5 to 7 days per week; may run more than once a day to accumulate weekly mileage. Quality days are bolded in the original.
- Weekly mileage about 40 to 52 miles (64 to 84 km), or 4.5 to more than 7 hours per week depending on speed.
- If racing during the plan, rearrange so there are at least 2 E days before races (this may mean deleting a training day). Races are an important part of training and improve fitness.
- If too demanding: go back to red or take a few weeks off; see chapter 9 for returning from a break.
- After blue you may not yet be ready for a marathon but are fit enough to consider one (see marathon programs). If it stimulates you to train harder, look at gold or distance-specific programs.
tags: type:methodology level:advanced subtopic:blue-plan sport:running metric:volume
src: Ch 8 "Blue Advanced Plan"
evidence: author-experience

### [DRF-C08-011] Gold elite plan: structure and use guidelines

- For runners who completed blue phase IV, or with considerable experience and time, who want to be prepared for races over a variety of distances.
- 6 or 7 days of running per week, sometimes twice in one day. Weekly mileage typically more than 60 miles (97 km), varying with weather and commitments.
- Could be used for the marathon, but chapter 16 marathon programs are recommended.
- Six workouts per week (bolded in the original). Day 4 is optional; any day may be a day off. Sunday is typically day 1, but day 1 can be any day.
- Allow extra time beyond the listed session time for stretching, supplemental training, showering, changing, travel, etc.
- Racing rules: 2 or 3 E days before races; 1 E day after races for every 3,000 meters of race distance (e.g., 3 E days after a 10K; 5 E days after a 15K). The last quality session before a race: a T session of three 1-mile runs at T pace with 2 minutes rest between.
- If too demanding, move back to a less demanding plan or pick parts of any plan as time allows. What works best for one may not suit another.
- Completing gold should prepare you for almost any distance; use distance-specific programs for an important race.
tags: type:methodology level:elite subtopic:gold-plan subtopic:race-recovery sport:running
src: Ch 8 "Gold Elite Plan"
evidence: author-experience

### [DRF-C08-012] Planned vs unplanned breaks and returning to training

- Two categories: planned breaks (running and other training are always still possible) and unplanned breaks (injury or illness may prevent even alternative exercise). A long break can also come from major injury needing surgery or from frustration with training.
- Whatever the reason, do not jump back to the pre-break volume. Fitness has dropped, so training need not be as hard as before to still give benefit. Do not work extra hard to make up for lost time.
- Planned breaks: most runners should plan time off within the year. Base the break on anticipated races; have a long-range plan including breaks, understanding an unplanned break may alter it.
- Length depends on how stressful the preceding weeks/months were. If several minor injury/illness breaks occurred during the year, a planned break may not be needed, but do not assume future injuries make planning unnecessary.
- Author's preference: minimum 2 weeks, maximum about 6 weeks for a planned break, if continuing serious running. Unplanned breaks may be longer.
tags: type:methodology type:periodization level:all subtopic:planned-break subtopic:unplanned-break sport:running phase:transition
evidence: author-experience
src: Ch 9 intro, "Planned Breaks"

### [DRF-C08-013] Table 9.2 Adjustments to Training Loads After a Break

E = easy running; ST = strides; R = repetition training; T = threshold training. Load = percent of prebreak weekly mileage/workload.

| Category | Time off | Adjustment to workload | % of prebreak VDOT |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| 1 | Up to 5 days | 5 days E @ no more than 100% load | 100% |
| 2 | 6-28 days | 1st half E @ 50% load | 93.1-99.7% or 96.5-99.8% (see table 9.1) |
| 2 | 6-28 days | 2nd half E @ 75% load | 93.1-99.7% or 96.5-99.8% (see table 9.1) |
| 2 (example) | 6 days | 3 days E @ 50% load + 3 days E @ 75% load | 99.7-99.8% |
| 2 (example) | 28 days | 14 days E @ 50% load + 14 days E @ 75% load | 93.1-96.5% |
| 3 | 4-8 weeks | 1st 3rd E @ 33% load | 84.7-93.1% or 92.3-96.5% (see table 9.1) |
| 3 | 4-8 weeks | 2nd 3rd E @ 50% load | 84.7-93.1% or 92.3-96.5% (see table 9.1) |
| 3 | 4-8 weeks | Final 3rd E @ 75% load | 84.7-93.1% or 92.3-96.5% (see table 9.1) |
| 3 (example) | 29 days | 9 days @ 33% load + 10 days @ 50% load + 10 days @ 75% load + some strides (ST) | 93.0-96.4% |
| 3 (example) | 8 weeks | 18 days E @ 33% load + 19 days E @ 50% load + 19 days E @ 75% load + some ST | 84.7-92.3% |
| 4 | 8 weeks or more | 3 weeks E @ 33% load, but not more than 30 miles/wk | 80.0-84.7% or 90.0-92.3% (see table 9.1) |
| 4 | 8 weeks or more | 3 weeks E @ 50% load, but not more than 40 miles/wk | 80.0-84.7% or 90.0-92.3% (see table 9.1) |
| 4 | 8 weeks or more | 3 weeks E @ 70% load + ST, but not more than 60 miles/wk | 80.0-84.7% or 90.0-92.3% (see table 9.1) |
| 4 | 8 weeks or more | 3 weeks E @ 85% load + ST & R, but not more than 75 miles/wk | 80.0-84.7% or 90.0-92.3% (see table 9.1) |
| 4 | 8 weeks or more | 3 weeks E @ 100% load + ST & T & R, but not more than 90 miles/wk | 80.0-84.7% or 90.0-92.3% (see table 9.1) |

Four categories based on time missed:
- Category 1: Up to 5 days missed. Return: 5 days E @ no more than 100% load (100% prebreak VDOT).
- Category 2: 6 to 28 days missed. Return: 1st half of return duration E @ 50% prebreak load, 2nd half E @ 75% prebreak load.
- Category 3: 4 to 8 weeks (29 to 56 days) missed. Return: 1st third E @ 33% load, 2nd third E @ 50% load, final third E @ 75% load + some strides (ST).
- Category 4: 8 weeks or more missed. Return: 5 consecutive 3-week blocks (total 15 weeks):
  - 3 weeks E @ 33% load, maximum 30 miles/wk
  - 3 weeks E @ 50% load, maximum 40 miles/wk
  - 3 weeks E @ 70% load + ST, maximum 60 miles/wk
  - 3 weeks E @ 85% load + ST & R, maximum 75 miles/wk
  - 3 weeks E @ 100% load + ST & T & R, maximum 90 miles/wk
- After completing the return progression, resume pre-setback training volume; adjust returning VDOT for any body weight changes (Figure 9.2).
tags: type:methodology type:dose level:all subtopic:return-to-training subtopic:load-reduction sport:running metric:volume
evidence: author-experience
src: Ch 9 "Adjusting Mileage or Duration of Training" | Table 9.2

### [DRF-C08-014] Progression of supplemental training stress: 4-week rule

- Increase supplemental stress carefully, like running. General rule: give the body a good 4 weeks at one level of training stress before increasing; better to undertrain than overtrain.
- Run mileage: stay at a set weekly mileage about 4 weeks before increasing; the increase can then be a bit more than 2 or 3 miles.
- Same for resistance training: about 4 weeks at one level before moving to greater stress.
tags: type:methodology type:principle level:all subtopic:progression subtopic:4-week-rule sport:running metric:load
evidence: author-experience
src: Ch 9 "Adding Supplemental Training"

### [DRF-C08-015] Four phases of a season (B/FIP, IQ, TQ, FQ)

Daniels divides a season into four progressive phases (Figure 10.1):
- Phase I = B/FIP: base training with foundation and injury-prevention emphases.
- Phase II = IQ: initial quality training.
- Phase III = TQ: transition quality; generally the most demanding phase.
- Phase IV = FQ: final quality; designed for peak performances; the time when races matter most.
Phase I starts the season (for students possibly the summer before school). Non-students start phase I whenever it best prepares them for the key races. The middle two phases advance fitness and racing ability toward the most important part.
Order of design (numbers under Figure 10.1: B/FIP 1, IQ 4, TQ 3, FQ 2): 
1. Phase I: determine what each runner needs first, using the runner profile.
2. Phase IV: decide what specific training types and amounts will produce best performance (an 800 runner and a 10K runner differ).
3. Phase III: decide what best prepares for phase IV (800 runner: more speed work; 10K runner: more T; both need solid I, with variation from the primary focus).
4. Phase II: decide what best prepares for phase III.
Record dates and distances of all races in the season so daily training can be arranged around them.
tags: type:periodization sport:running level:all phase:base phase:build phase:peak subtopic:four-phase-season
evidence: author-experience
src: Ch 10 "Break your season into phases" | p. [n/a]

### [DRF-C08-016] Phase content: I, II, III, IV

Phase I (B/FIP): mostly E running. If more than 3 weeks available, add light strides (10- to 15-second light, quick runs, full recoveries) to daily E runs, plus supplemental training (light resistance training, dynamic flexibility) after some runs. One easy L run weekly = 25 to 30 percent of the week's total mileage.
Phase II (IQ): R training. Two R sessions per week with two E days between R sessions; weekly L run continues. Rationale: add only one new stress per phase; E to R adds only a speed stress (little demand on aerobic or lactate-clearance systems), whereas E to I adds two (faster running and aerobic stress). Doing R first means I-pace speed is not new in phase III because R reps were faster. Do not use last season's best race times for R pace; use a current race time or best estimate of current 1-mile race.
Phase III (TQ): primarily I; adds aerobic stress but no faster speed. Shorter-distance specialists: one good I session weekly plus an R session (maintain speed). Longer-distance runners: often two I sessions weekly, but if a 3,000 m or longer race is in that week it counts as a tough aerobic workout and one I session is enough. Include an easy L run each week.
Phase IV (FQ): T running (still quality but less stressful than I; runner feels better for races). Longer-distance specialists: concentrate on T, drop I sessions (unless the race stresses aerobic system to maximum). With an L run and a race each week, one T session per week suffices. Add a few short R runs (four to six R 200s) at the end of each T session. Shorter-distance specialists: may combine a Q session with T and R work to stay sharp.
Summary: E to R to I to T. During R phase, E runs most days; when moving R to I, occasional R sessions maintain gains. Each phase = primary training type plus secondary training maintaining the previous phase's gains.
Races as training: races lasting about 5 to 20 minutes stress the aerobic system fully, equal to I training, so I training can be reduced when racing medium distances regularly; hence I is dropped from a typical school program in phase IV.
tags: type:methodology sport:running level:all phase:base phase:build phase:peak system:aerobic subtopic:one-new-stress
evidence: author-experience
src: Ch 10 Phase I-IV Training | p. [n/a]

### [DRF-C08-017] Adapting phase lengths: 24-week model and Figure 10.2

Full model: 24 weeks, 6 weeks per phase. When fewer weeks are available:

**Option 1 (school cross country):**
Do phases I and II in the summer after spring track; the first 6 weeks of the school year are phase III (toughest); the final 6 weeks are phase IV.

**Option 2 (fewer than 6 weeks per phase using Figure 10.2):**
Weeks are allocated according to the priority numbers 1 through 24 in Figure 10.2:

### Figure 10.2 Numbering System for Phase Lengths
| Phase I (B/FIP) | Phase II (IQ) | Phase III (TQ) | Phase IV (FQ) |
| :---: | :---: | :---: | :---: |
| 1 &nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp; 2 &nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp; 3 | 7 &nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp; 8 &nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp; 9 | 10 &nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp; 11 &nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp; 12 | 4 &nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp; 5 &nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp; 6 |
| 13 | 18 | 14 | 17 |
| 21 | 19 | 15 | 22 |
| 23 | 20 | 16 | 24 |

Allocation logic:
- Numbers 1–12 indicate sequence of phase inclusion: 1, 2, 3 in Phase I; 4, 5, 6 in Phase IV; 7, 8, 9 in Phase II; 10, 11, 12 in Phase III.
- If only 3 weeks total: all 3 weeks in Phase I (weeks 1, 2, 3).
- If 6 weeks total: 3 weeks Phase I (1, 2, 3) followed by 3 weeks Phase IV (4, 5, 6).
- If 9 weeks total: 3 weeks Phase I (1, 2, 3), 3 weeks Phase II (7, 8, 9), and 3 weeks Phase IV (4, 5, 6) — skipping Phase III entirely.
- If 20 weeks available (working backward from 24): drop weeks 24, 23, 22, 21 -> 4 weeks Phase I (dropping 21, 23), 6 weeks Phase II, 6 weeks Phase III, 4 weeks Phase IV (dropping 22, 24).
- If 23 weeks available: drop week 24 in Phase IV (5 weeks in Phase IV).
Weeks dropped when time is limited are those with the least impact on ideal performance.

*Source: Adapted by permission of Runner's World Magazine. Copyright 1993, Rodale Press Inc.*

src: Ch 10 "Adapt the phase lengths as necessary" | Fig. 10.2

### [DRF-C08-018] Sample weekly plan (Figure 10.3)

Week structure (Day 1 considered Sunday):
- L run is scheduled on Day 1 (Sunday).
- Days 5 and 6 are always E days.

### Figure 10.3 Sample Training Week
| Day 1 | Day 2 | Day 3 | Day 4 | Day 5 | Day 6 | Day 7 |
| :---: | :---: | :---: | :---: | :---: | :---: | :---: |
| L | Q1<br>E | (Q1)<br>E OR Q1 | Q2<br>(Q2)<br>E OR Q1 | E | E | Q3<br>(Q3)<br>Race<br>(Q) |

Scheduling rules and options:
- Without weekly races: Q1, Q2, and Q3 fall on Days 2, 4, and 7; or Days 3, 4, and 7 if using the back-to-back training system.
- Q3 can be an actual race, which counts as a quality session.
- Priority: Always make Q1 the single most important workout of the week, focused on the primary training type of that phase. If weather or circumstances disrupt the week, the primary stimulus is still accomplished even if only one Q session is completed.
- Phase II: Q1, Q2, and Q3 may all be R sessions; or Q1 and Q2 are R, and Q3 is a T session. For runners with fewer than 4 weeks of Phase I running, limit Phase II to only two R sessions (Q1 and Q2) and move Q2 to Thursday (Day 5).
- Phase III example: Q1 = I session; Q2 = T training + a few Rs; Q3 = another I session or a race.
- Phase IV (shorter distances): Q1 and Q2 both T + R; Q3 = race or solid R session.
- Phase IV (longer distances): Q1 and Q2 both T sessions followed by a few short Rs.
- Important race on weekend: Schedule only one quality workout (Q1 = T + a few Rs) 4 days before the race; the race serves as Q2 to close the week (no Q3).
tags: type:plan sport:running level:all subtopic:weekly-structure subtopic:q-sessions
evidence: author-experience
src: Ch 10 "Sample weekly plan" | Fig. 10.3

### [DRF-C08-019] 800 m: four-phase season guidance (phases I-IV)

Phase I: builds base and resistance to injury. Weeks available vary (some high school runners only a few weeks; others more than 2 months). Have an overall plan for the entire season so each phase benefits from the previous.
Phase II:
- Three Q sessions per week; days not specified. Typical: Q1 and Q2 Monday and Tuesday, Q3 Friday. A race counts as a Q session, so replacing a Friday Q with a race does not reduce the week's Q count.
- Alternatives: Mon/Wed/Fri or Sat, or Tue/Wed/Sat; try different spreads.
- Add a fairly long run on weekends (prolonged cool-down after Saturday meet, or Sunday).
- Arrange Q sessions to fall when not fatigued from a previous Q or race. It is fine to skip a Q now and then if not up to it; rest is part of training.
- Phase II is not the most stressful part: imagine you could do more than is scheduled. Better to feel you could have done more than to wish it hadn't been so hard.
- Have a reason for moving to a harder intensity (usually racing faster than earlier training was based on; VDOT tables, ch 5).
Phase III:
- Hardest training. Long run on Saturday (if no race) or Sunday if Saturday race; a prolonged easy cool-down after Saturday meet can be the long run.
- Three Q sessions per week with weekend L runs counting as Q. Saturday meets: midweek Qs Mon+Wed or Tue+Wed, leaving Thu and Fri as E days. Friday meet: Mon and Tue for midweek Qs.
- Use VDOT values for paces, but do not increase training speed more often than every 3rd or 4th week, even if a race says go faster. Let the body spend several weeks at one stress level.
Phase IV:
- Most weeks three Q sessions, often an additional Q day (race) in race weeks.
- When a race is very important (championship or qualifier): just one Q session early in that week, limited duration, not faster than usual recent training.
- All non-Q days are E days; run once, twice or not at all depending on desired weekly mileage. Arrange Q and E days to feel fresh for important races.
- Place L runs right after meets (immediately after or early next day).
- Race weeks: final Q session 3 or even 4 days before the race, T-pace effort with maybe light repetitions of 200 m. Try different pre-race approaches; what works may differ between runners.
tags: type:periodization sport:running phase:base phase:build phase:peak level:all subtopic:q-sessions subtopic:800-meter
evidence: author-experience
src: Ch 11 Phases I-IV

### [DRF-C08-020] 800 m: long-run and stride rules by mileage level

- 20-30 miles: Phase II Q1 = 40-60 min L run (not more than 30% of week's total mileage) plus six ST; add 6-8 ST (may be gradual uphill) to middle or end of two weekly E runs. Phase I: three Q per week, L counts as Q.
- 40 miles: Phase II Q1 = 60 min L run (not more than 25% of weekly mileage) plus six ST; add 6-8 ST to two E runs.
- 50-60 miles: Phase II Q1 = lesser of 10 miles (16 km) and 25% of week's total mileage, plus 6 ST; add 6-8 ST to two E runs. Phase I Q sessions listed in distances rather than times.
- 50-60 miles Phase I: running twice on several days is recommended; the second run may be on the same day as a Q session. A morning run = minimum 30 min E plus 8 to 10 ST in the middle or after. Experiment with which days have morning runs (every other day, or 2 consecutive days followed by 1-3 days without). Check whether afternoon runs feel better on days with or without morning runs. If study/work is very demanding, add morning runs only on days with adequate recovery time. Use second runs to reach weekly mileage totals.
tags: type:methodology sport:running level:advanced subtopic:long-run-limit subtopic:double-runs subtopic:strides
evidence: author-experience
src: Ch 11 mileage sections

### [DRF-C08-021] Four-phase approach for 1,500 m to 2 mile

- Phase I: easy mileage (E), strides (ST) on easy days, and supplemental training (light resistance or circuit work). Avoid running immediately after supplemental exercises; run before it or several hours afterward. Phase I is mostly E and untimed running, so it is easy to do alone. In a school team, divide into groups of equal fitness so less-fit runners are not pulled to too demanding a pace.
- Phase II: Q sessions begin: a weekly L run and a couple of R workouts. Runners just off a season (for example fall cross country) are in top aerobic shape and can skip phase I and start with R work. That speed and economy work sets them up for an indoor track season of shorter races. R is usually less stressful than I and is seen as a break from intervals and 5-12K racing.
- Phase III: the most demanding phase, which determines how much better a runner gets in the season. It adds more stressful I workouts and weekly T sessions (T improves endurance). The key is handling I without overdoing it. The goal of I is maximum benefit from least work, using training speeds from recent races via the VDOT tables.
- Phase IV: competitive track season. Avoid overracing: vary race distances and adjust training in multi-race weeks. Vary scheduled workouts, especially outdoors, as weather affects what works mentally and physiologically. Drop a scheduled session if two Q days in a week would leave you inadequately recovered for an important Friday or Saturday race.
tags: type:periodization sport:running phase:base phase:build phase:peak level:all subtopic:four-phase-season
src: Ch 12 Phases I-IV | n/a
evidence: author-experience

### [DRF-C08-022] 30 mi/wk plan: phase rules

Phase I: three Q sessions per week, none demanding. One is a relatively long L or Mod run; the other two include strides (ST), 15-20 second light, quick runs on the flat or a gradual hill, not all-out sprints. If on a hill, finish the final two on flat or track. Take full recoveries between all strides.
- Mod (moderately long) pace is about 20 to 30 seconds per mile faster than typical E and L pace.
- On non-Q days take an E run of at least 30 minutes. Use E days to accumulate weekly mileage; an E day may be no running if mileage allows. If you have not run for weeks before phase I, choose the minimum amount recommended.
Phase II:
- Q1 is an L run, usually Sunday (or add miles to a Saturday session if Saturday is not a stress day). For about 30 mi/wk, L is 30 percent of weekly mileage.
- If the day after L is an E day, add 6-8 ST to its end. Add 6-8 ST to the end of two other E days each week. Strides on flat or gradual hill; be careful coming down from uphill strides.
- Two more Q days per week, with two E days between them if possible. Every second or third week, if all is going well, add an extra Q session, repeating Q2 as Q4.
- With L plus three more Q: best days Sunday (Q1), Monday, Thursday, Friday, leaving Saturday or Sunday for L [as printed]. With L plus two Q: Monday and Thursday, or Tuesday and Friday.
- Q1 L run in weeks 7 through 12.
- Base R pace on a conservative estimate of what you could currently race for 1 mile.
Phase III:
- Increase R pace by 1 sec per 200, 2 sec per 400, 3 sec per 600 versus phase II. If going well, add another 1 sec per 200 after the 3rd week of phase III.
- Set I pace from a recent race VDOT, or as 6 seconds per 400 slower than the R pace you handle well.
- Racing often starts. In race weeks schedule the two other Q days Monday (or Tuesday) and Wednesday for a Saturday race, Monday and Tuesday for a Friday race. On relatively easy race days consider ending with 6 x 200 R w/200 jg after the final race.
Phase IV:
- Adjust paces to recent race-predicted VDOT; with no races, increase all training paces by 1 second per 400.
- Usual weekend race most weeks. In weeks 19, 20, 22 put Q2 and Q3 on Monday and Wednesday for Saturday races, Monday and Tuesday for Friday races. Back-to-back Q days are often better than an E day between (for example Tuesday and Wednesday before a Saturday race). Skip Q3 in weeks ending in a Friday or major Saturday race.
- Numbers without a distance mean miles (2 T = 2 miles at threshold).
tags: type:methodology type:periodization sport:running level:intermediate subtopic:30-miles-per-week metric:pace
src: Ch 12 Training on 30 miles | n/a
evidence: author-experience

### [DRF-C08-023] 45 mi/wk plan: phase rules

Phase I: as for the 30 mi plan: three Q sessions, none demanding; one L or Mod run; the other two include R 200s or strides (15-20 s, not all-out; finish final two flat if uphill; full recoveries). Mod is about 20 to 30 seconds per mile faster than typical E and L pace. Non-Q days: E run of at least 30 minutes; E days accumulate mileage and may be no running. Runners are assumed to have been running regularly for some weeks.
Phase II:
- Q1 L run, usually Sunday (or extra miles at the end of a non-stress Saturday). L is 25 percent of weekly mileage at about 45 mi/wk.
- If the day after L is an E day, add 6-8 ST; add 6-8 ST to the end of two other E days. Be careful coming down from uphill strides.
- Between Q2 and Q3 try to have 2 E days. Every 2nd or 3rd week, if all is going well, add a fourth Q session, repeating Q1 as Q4.
- With L plus three more Q: best days Monday, Thursday, Friday, leaving Saturday or Sunday for L. With three Q total: Sunday plus Monday and Thursday, or Sunday plus Tuesday and Friday.
- Schedule Q1 L plus 6 ST in each of weeks 7 through 12.
Phase III:
- R pace +1 sec/200, +2 sec/400, +3 sec/600 versus phase II; another +1 sec/200 after the 3rd week of phase III if all is going well. I pace from a recent race VDOT or 6 seconds per 400 slower than current R pace.
- With races at the end of weeks 14, 16 or 18, drop Q2 or Q3 and schedule only two Q sessions plus the race: Monday and Wednesday (Saturday race) or Monday and Tuesday (Friday race). On relatively easy race days consider adding 6 x 200 R w/200 jg after the final race.
Phase IV:
- Adjust to recent race-predicted VDOT; with no races, add 1 second per 400 to all paces.
- In weeks 19, 20, 22 put Q2 and Q3 on Tuesday and Wednesday for Saturday races; skip Q2 or Q3 when there is a Friday race. Back-to-back Q days before a Saturday race are suggested.
- The 24-week plan can be reduced by some weeks if 24 are not available. Numbers without distance are miles.
tags: type:methodology type:periodization sport:running level:intermediate subtopic:45-miles-per-week metric:pace
src: Ch 12 Training on 45 miles | n/a
evidence: author-experience

### [DRF-C08-024] 60 mi/wk plan: phase rules

- Runners are assumed to have run a fair amount (maybe moved up from lower mileage or taken a 2-week break) and be familiar with about 60 mi/wk.
- Phase I may be unnecessary for those who have run a lot; they can go to phase II. If comfortable with higher mileage but without recent quality work, spend time (maybe only 2 or 3 weeks) in phase I. Look ahead at what you will face before deciding how advanced to be; if you have time and want to build up to faster running, go through phase I.
- Do strides on a gradual uphill when possible, with the last couple of strides of a set on the flat. Many runners at 60 mi/wk or more feel more rested running twice on many days: it allows rehydrating and recovering between runs, and feel/performance are better than one daily session.
- Phase II: introduces a fair amount of R. Afternoon R workouts feel better several hours after a morning E run. Assume regular morning runs most days: about 30 minutes each, with 8-10 ST in the middle or at the end, on the flat. Q1 (usually Sunday) is a comfortable L run of about 10 to 12 miles (16 to 19 km) plus 6 to 8 ST.
- Phase III: a fair amount of I-intensity running, as specified distances at I pace or hard (H) runs for set durations, at an intensity you could maintain in a race of about 10 to 12 minutes.
- Phase IV: adjust paces to recent race-predicted VDOT; with no races add 1 second per 400. In weeks 19, 20, 22 put Q2 and Q3 on Tuesday and Wednesday for Saturday races, Monday and Tuesday for Friday races. Back-to-back Q days are often better. Use non-Q days and morning miles to accumulate weekly mileage.
- The plan can be reduced by some weeks per phase if 24 weeks are not available. Numbers without distance are miles.
tags: type:methodology type:periodization sport:running level:advanced subtopic:60-miles-per-week metric:volume
src: Ch 12 Training on 60 miles | n/a
evidence: author-experience

### [DRF-C08-025] 5K/10K Phase I (building resistance to injury)

Who: runners recently training for other events can go straight to phase II. After a recent break in training, follow phase I for 4 to 6 weeks.
- If not in very good shape, do not run very hard or very much; plan a reasonable weekly average and keep it for about 3 or 4 weeks before increasing.
- Mileage increase: go up 1 mile (1.6 km) every 3rd or 4th week for each run per week. Five runs/week: add 5 miles (8 km). Twice-daily running (14 runs/week): increase by a maximum of 10 miles (16 km) after 3 or 4 weeks at previous mileage.
- Daily runs easy and comfortable; 30 minutes is a good start.
- Supplemental training: light resistance weights or light plyometrics; circuit programs (ch. 9). Work on technique first; do not increase resistance until comfortable with relatively light loads.
- Workouts: E runs, light uphill runs, strides (ST). Strides are NOT all-out sprints: light, quick runs of about 15 to 20 seconds each with about 45 to 60 seconds rest after each. Uphill runs comfortable, not blasting; light bounding up a gradual hill is possible; be careful going back down (easy to overdo and get a minor impact injury).
- After a couple of weeks of E running, add one L run per week, no more than 25 percent of total weekly mileage, conversational pace, terminated any time you start struggling with mechanics.
- Stride rate: go for 180 steps per minute; breathing: comfortable 2-2 rhythm.
- Summary: all runs at E pace, one L run per week, add 8 to 10 ST to the middle or end of three or four E runs each week, begin supplemental training (technique, light resistance).
tags: type:methodology type:periodization sport:running phase:base level:beginner subtopic:phase-1 metric:cadence
evidence: author-experience
src: Ch 13 "Phase I" | n/a

### [DRF-C08-026] 5K/10K Phases II, III, IV: emphasis and limits

- Phase II: R training is the first true quality work. R runs not to exceed 5 percent of week's total mileage. Set R pace by a conservative estimate of current 1-mile race pace; or, if recently raced other distances, use a current race to find VDOT and its R pace. Chapter 4 tables can be used for R sessions and amounts.
- Phase III (for runners getting serious): intervals (I pace) become primary and are demanding. Total I-pace running per session not more than 8 percent of weekly mileage and not more than 10K regardless of weekly mileage. Two I sessions per week are enough. I pace: use recent race/VDOT; with no race, make I pace 6 to 8 seconds per 400 slower than the R pace recently used.
- Phase IV: less demanding than phase III, less I training. Main emphasis on T-pace workouts, with R and I work added now and then.
- Races in phase IV: a 5K can be a tune-up for a more important 5K or a coming 10K. Take 2 or 3 E days before races, which means dropping at least one Q session that week.
- Race recovery: one E day after races for each 3K of race distance: 3 E days after a 10K, 2 E days after a 5K; more for longer races (e.g., 5 E days for a 15K).
- E days accumulate the week's mileage goals and can be a day off if needed.
tags: type:methodology type:periodization sport:running phase:build phase:peak phase:taper level:intermediate subtopic:phase-limits subtopic:race-recovery
evidence: author-experience
src: Ch 13 "Phases II-IV" | n/a

### [DRF-C08-027] 5K/10K plan phase II content summaries

40-50 mi plan phase II: weeks 1, 2, 4, 5 each have an L run, an R session, and a session with some T and some R; weeks 3 and 6 have an M-pace run plus an R session and an H session. Emphasis on R, with a little T and occasionally M, I or H to prepare for phase III.
60-70 mi plan phase II: weeks 1 and 4: L run, R session, R+T+R session; weeks 2 and 5: L run, R session, session with T and R; weeks 3 and 6: M-pace run, R session, H session.
tags: type:periodization sport:running phase:build level:intermediate subtopic:plan-structure
evidence: author-experience
src: Ch 13 | n/a

### [DRF-C08-028] Cross country season plan: structure and per-session limits

- School seasons are only 10 or 12 weeks, not enough for four 6-week phases. Preferred: complete all of phase I and II in the summer before school, entering phase III when school starts.
- New runners or those with little summer running: spend time in phase I, little or no phase II once school begins (base work before I work and early races). The chapter's plan has four 6-week phases, with parts or all of phase II and a couple of weeks of phase I cut as needed.
- Adjust quality running per runner by weekly mileage. Per-session limits (suggested maximums, not required amounts, per session not per week):
  - I session: lesser of 8% of weekly mileage or 10K. Example: 30 mi (48 km)/week -> 2.4 mi at I pace per session; 60 mi (97 km)/week -> 4.8 mi.
  - R session: 5% of weekly mileage.
  - T session: 10% of weekly mileage.
tags: type:periodization type:dose sport:running level:all phase:build metric:volume subtopic:session-limits
src: Ch 14 "Developing a Season Plan"
evidence: author-experience

### [DRF-C08-029] Cross country Phase I rules

- L run on Sunday each week: lesser of 30% of weekly mileage and 60 minutes.
- Remaining days: minimum 30 min of E running; no single day more than 25% of week's total mileage.
- Relative beginners or those with little recent running: consider phase IV of the white plan (chapter 8) as their phase I. The table plan suits non-beginners without a long break.
- If not running for more than 4 weeks: eliminate an L run; weekly mileage about 20 miles (32 km) for 3 weeks, then about 25 to 30 miles (40 to 48 km) for the next 3 weeks.
- Plan can be shortened if 6 weeks unavailable, but relative beginners should use at least 3 or 4 weeks.
See library: Table 14.1 Phase I Training Plan for Cross Country.
tags: type:methodology sport:running level:intermediate phase:base subtopic:phase-I metric:volume
src: Ch 14 "Phase I" | Table 14.1
evidence: author-experience

### [DRF-C08-030] Cross country Phase II rules (light R training)

- Prerequisite: consistent running in past several weeks; ready for light R training.
- Weekly mileage not more than 10 miles (16 km) above that of the final 3 weeks of phase I.
- R-pace running per session: no more than 5% of week's total mileage, or not more than 5 miles (8 km) if doing more than 100 miles (160 km)/week.
- Choose workouts from table 4.4 (chapter 4) by weekly mileage: ~35 mi/week choose R sessions from category B; ~50 mi/week category C or D. Use E days to accumulate weekly mileage; with low mileage an E day may be a day off now and then.
- R workouts flat or uphill. Uphill improves speed and economy; if late-season key races are hilly, make some R sessions uphill. Go by feel, use time not distance. Example: eight 60-second uphill runs with full recovery on the return down; the way down must not be steep (downhill stresses the legs). Finish a hill R session with a few flat R runs to feel fast again. Avoid uphill training in the final couple of weeks before an important race (better recovery).
- L runs under 25% of weekly mileage; cut any L run short if mechanics deteriorate.
- Q sessions R1/R2/R3 options (see L1 Table 14.2). Race-week adjustments: Tuesday race, if on R1 or R3 switch to R2 with Q1 = Tuesday race, Q2 the day after, Q3 Saturday. Friday race: follow R3 but move Q2 from Wednesday to Tuesday, Friday race replaces Q3. Saturday race: follow R2 as is, race replaces Q3.
- Where: do all Q sessions on terrain of the key races (usually grass or dirt); E and L runs on roads or smooth paths. If key races are hilly, do one or two Q sessions over partly hilly terrain, but always run the final couple of R-pace runs on flat footing. Try to have 2 E days before any race (L counts as E pace). Early in the season, do a desired Q session the day after a race if wanted.
tags: type:methodology sport:running level:intermediate phase:build subtopic:phase-II subtopic:hill-repetitions system:neuromuscular
src: Ch 14 "Phase II"
evidence: author-experience

### [DRF-C08-031] Cross country Phase III rules (I-pace phase)

- Most stressful phase; races often thrown in and always replace a Q session in race week. Base amount of I running per session on weekly mileage (table 4.3, chapter 4).
- Do not include work bouts at I pace longer than 5 minutes. Example: VDOT giving 6:00 mile I pace: no I miles; 1,200s the longest, but could go up to 5-minute H runs if running by time.
- Weekly I session is Q1 (after weekend L run). Races give similar physiological benefit to a solid I session; so Q1 = I session, Q2 = T session ending with some R 200s or strides.
- T running in phases III and IV: do T workouts on a flat surface to control pace. Heart-rate monitors on undulating terrain are avoided by the author because they do not always reflect desired speed.
- No race in the week: Q3 OK if feeling good; best either a time-based I session (e.g., 5 or 6 × 3 min H w/2 min jg rather than 5 × 1,000 I w/2 min jg), or a combination of T, I, R (e.g., 2 T + 3 × 2 min H w/1 min jg + 4 × 200 R w/200 jg).
- Race replacement schedule: Tuesday race replaces Q1; Saturday race replaces Q3; Friday race: drop Q2 on Tuesday [Note: text states "drop Q2 on Tuesday, make Q2 the Friday race", even though Tuesday is labeled Q1 in Table 14.3], make Q2 the Friday race, drop Q3 on Saturday.
See library: Tables 14.3 and 14.4.
tags: type:methodology sport:running level:intermediate phase:build subtopic:phase-III system:vo2max metric:pace
src: Ch 14 "Phase III"
evidence: author-experience

### [DRF-C08-032] Cross country Phase IV rules (races, staying rested)

- Weekly races common and important; need to be relatively rested.
- Less important Saturday race: follow the phase III Saturday-race week (table 14.4).
- Important Saturday race: moderately long Sunday L run and a T-type workout on Tuesday; all other days E days; author even stops strides after late-week E runs.
See library: Table 14.5.
tags: type:methodology sport:running level:intermediate phase:peak subtopic:phase-IV system:threshold
src: Ch 14 "Phase IV"
evidence: author-experience

### [DRF-C08-033] Alien training plan: concept and how to use it

- The "alien training plan" is a generic plan, somewhat different from the author's usual approach, for race distances 15K-30K. Assumes the runner has been running regularly for a while and knows the training types (E, L, M, T, I, H, R) and how to adjust speeds (from VDOT) and amounts, as outlined in chapter 4.
- The author specifies the TYPE of workout; the runner determines the DETAILS. E.g. if a session is "R", the runner knows R speed, recovery and maximum amount, and picks a session from the chapter 4 lists (R sessions: table 4.4).
- Procedure: (1) look up current VDOT in chapter 5 tables from a recent race or estimated race time; (2) decide which set of workouts applies based on VDOT and current weekly mileage; (3) pick specific workouts from chapter 4 tables.
- No series of training phases: a series of workouts repeated every 2 weeks.
- May also work well for races shorter than 15K or longer than 30K, and the author thinks it may be a good marathon program for some runners (see 18-week novice marathon program, table 16.2).
- Scheduling: the 7 days (day 1-7) are flexible. Author usually treats Sunday as day 1 (Q1), Q2 Tuesday, Q3 Friday. Keep Q1, Q2, Q3 in written order and schedule E days between Q sessions. Strides (ST) are added to a couple of E days each week. Warm-up and cool-down for each Q session: do what you feel is adequate.
- Variation occurs only in prerace weeks and the following recovery period. Within a week of a race, switch to the prerace week.
tags: type:methodology sport:running level:intermediate subtopic:alien-program subtopic:q-sessions metric:pace
evidence: author-experience
src: Ch 15 "Alien Training"

### [DRF-C08-034] Table 16.1 Six Approaches to Marathon Training

Six approaches, each with variations by weekly mileage (or time running).

| Program | Features | Reason for choosing |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Novice | Training 3-5 days per week | Beginner runner or little previous running training. |
| 2Q | Two Q sessions per week | Running fairly regularly; able to set aside 2 days each week for a more demanding Q session. |
| 4-week cycles | Two Q sessions per week for 3 of every 4 weeks, with a 4th week of only E running | Like 2 Q sessions each week; like a high-mileage week with no Q sessions every 4th week. |
| 5-week cycles | 5 weeks of training that can be repeated as often as time allows | Interested in regular L and M-pace runs while concentrating on T runs; keeps some R and I work; allows selecting workouts for runners logging a variety of distances. Can be demanding, so for the final 3 weeks before a marathon follow what is provided for your mileage category in table 16.3. |
| Final 18 weeks | Programs based on miles, kilometers, or time | Like scheduling by miles, kilometers, or time rather than distances run. Typically for runners logging a lot of mileage who prefer not to have 2 Q sessions each week. Q sessions every 4th or 5th day, so some weeks have one and others two. |
| Final 12 weeks | Some fairly demanding final weeks prior to a marathon | Training regularly and prefer a program for the final 12 weeks before the chosen marathon. |
tags: type:methodology sport:running level:all subtopic:program-selection subtopic:marathon
src: Ch 16 Marathon | Table 16.1
evidence: author-experience

### [DRF-C08-035] Novice 18-week plan: how to use it (sessions, strides, T pace)

- Weeks 18 through 10: train 3, 4, or 5 days per week (4 or 5 preferable). 3 days: sessions A, C, E with at least 1 day between each. 4 days: A, C, E and either B or D. 5 days: all sessions.
- Strides (ST): runs of 15 to 20 seconds each at a comfortably fast pace you might race at for 1 mile; 45- to 60-second rests between strides.
- T-pace: comfortably hard running at a pace you could keep up for at least 30 minutes.
- Week 10: try to complete a steady 10K run. If run as a race, take it very easy.
- Final 9 weeks: bump up or keep training 5 days a week. Two weekly Q sessions. Fit them on days with nice weather and a lot of time. Separate the two Q sessions by at least 2 E days. The other 5 days are E days, including potential rest days or E running for at least 30 minutes.
Abbreviations: E easy, W walk, T threshold, M marathon pace, L long, ST strides, WO workout.
See library: Table 16.2 18-Week Marathon Training Plan for Novices.
tags: type:methodology sport:running level:beginner phase:base subtopic:novice-plan subtopic:strides
src: Ch 16 Marathon | Novice Training
evidence: author-experience

### [DRF-C08-036] 2Q program: structure and scheduling rules

- Prerequisite: at least 6 weeks of running before starting any 2Q 18-week program.
- All 2Q plans have two Q sessions per week. Run on at least 6 days each week.
- Suggested Q1 on Sunday (or the same weekday as the marathon); Q2 on Wednesday or Thursday. Days can be rearranged, but always keep 2 or 3 E days between Q days.
- E days: recovery and easy running to accumulate weekly mileage; may be one, two, or more E runs per day. If a day off is taken, use the remaining E days to reach the weekly mileage goal.
- Add six to eight strides to the middle or end of at least 2 E days each week. Strides: light, quick 15- to 20-second runs, about 45 to 60 seconds recovery between; fast, controlled, not sprints.
- Races within the plan: schedule in place of Q1 that week; perform the displaced Q1 at midweek and drop that week's Q2 altogether (Sunday race starts the week, then Q1 a few days later; next week starts with its Q1 as normal). Try to get 3 E days before a race, and take 1 E day for each 3,000 to 4,000 meters of race distance (e.g., 3 E days after a 10K).
- Bolded sessions in table 16.3 may be eliminated and replaced with an E day if overly tired or overwhelmed.
- In table 16.3, the number before the intensity is miles at that intensity (3 E = 3 miles easy; 4 M = 4 miles marathon pace; 1 T = 1 mile threshold). Workouts are nonstop unless rest is shown. jg = jog recovery.
tags: type:methodology sport:running level:intermediate phase:build subtopic:2q-program subtopic:race-in-plan subtopic:strides
src: Ch 16 Marathon | 2Q Program
evidence: author-experience

### [DRF-C08-037] 4-week cycle marathon plan: structure and rules

- The programs give 26 weeks of training and assume the runner has been running regularly before starting. With sufficient training one may enter later than 26 weeks out. Look through the full program to decide when to enter; if a few weeks of base running are needed first, do them.
- All 4-week cycles are 2Q plans: two Q sessions per week for 3 consecutive weeks, then a 4th week with no Q sessions. The non-Q week contains only E running, with 6 to 8 ST added to two of the training days.
- Suggested Q1 day is Sunday, or the same weekday as the coming marathon. With Q1 on Sunday, Q2 is best on Wednesday or Thursday. Q days may be rearranged to suit the schedule, but always keep 2 or 3 E days between Q days.
- E days are for recovery, easy (non-Q) runs and accumulating weekly mileage. An E day may hold one, two or more E runs. If a day off is taken now and then, use the remaining E days of that week to reach the weekly mileage goal.
- Strides: add six to eight strides to the middle or end of at least 2 E days every week. ST are light, quick 15- to 20-second runs with about 45 to 60 seconds of recovery between each. They are fast, controlled runs, not sprints. They may be run up a moderate hill, but be careful jogging back down because downhill running can be stressful.
- Notation: if no distance unit is given, the distance is miles. 8 M = 8 miles at M pace; 3 × 1 T = three 1-mile runs at T pace.
tags: type:methodology type:periodization sport:running phase:build level:all subtopic:4-week-cycle subtopic:2q-plan subtopic:strides
evidence: author-experience
src: Ch 16 "4-Week Cycle"

### [DRF-C08-038] Weekly mileage as fraction of peak (P) in the 4-week cycle

- Weekly mileage typically varies from 80 to 100 percent of peak (P) mileage. P is the most mileage planned in any single week, most often every 4th week as indicated in the program.
- Each week's suggested fraction of P is listed in the second column of the program.
- Example: peak of 60 miles and .8P for a week means 80 percent of 60 = 48 miles that week.
tags: type:dose type:methodology sport:running metric:volume level:all subtopic:peak-mileage
evidence: author-experience
src: Ch 16 "4-Week Cycle"

### [DRF-C08-039] Races during the 4-week marathon plan

- Schedule a race in place of that week's Q1 session. Perform the displaced Q1 at midweek and drop that week's Q2 altogether.
- Example: a Sunday race starts the week; that week's Q1 follows a few days later; the next week starts with its Q1 as normal.
- Rearrange training to get 3 E days before a race, and take 1 E day for each 3,000 to 4,000 meters of race distance (e.g., 3 E days after a 10K).
tags: type:decision-rule type:methodology sport:running phase:build level:all subtopic:racing-within-plan subtopic:post-race-recovery
evidence: author-experience
src: Ch 16 "4-Week Cycle"

### [DRF-C08-040] 5-Week Cycle: session limits for R, I, T, M, L

Five-week cycles are repeated as many times as necessary to build up to an important race. How much of each type of training is done depends on current fitness and weekly mileage.

- R sessions: total no more than the lesser of 5 percent of weekly mileage and 5 miles (8 km) at R pace. Recovery between fast R bouts: two to three times as long as the R-pace run time.
- I sessions: total no more than the lesser of 8 percent of weekly mileage and 10,000 meters at I pace. Recovery between I bouts: equal to, or a little less than, time spent at I pace.
- Combined I & R sessions: limit time at R and I paces to half that set aside for individual R and I sessions.
- T sessions: total no more than the lesser of 10 percent of weekly mileage and 15 miles (24 km) at T pace; 3 miles (4.8 km) at T pace is the minimum for any T session. Recovery between T bouts: 1 minute for each 5 to 6 minutes of running time.
- M-pace running in a single session: not more than the lesser of 18 miles (29 km) and 20 percent of weekly mileage if doing more than 40 miles (64 km) per week; not more than 30 percent of weekly mileage if less than 40.
- L runs: 30 percent of weekly mileage if running 40 miles (64 km) or less per week; 25 percent if more than 40 miles. L run is the lesser of 150 minutes and the amount given by the 25/30 percent rules.
- See chapter 4 for R, I, T and M sessions to choose from.
tags: type:dose type:methodology sport:running phase:build system:threshold metric:volume level:all subtopic:session-limits subtopic:marathon
evidence: author-experience
src: Ch 16 "5-Week Cycle"

### [DRF-C08-041] Final 18-week marathon programs: general use and rationale

- No fixed amount of time suits everyone; fitness, weather and marathon date affect how much is needed or available. Author tends to write 18-week programs; some prefer longer, especially to fit shorter races. A runner in great shape with a marathon only a couple of months away can jump into the middle of a longer schedule or pick desirable weeks.
- Deena Kastor (U.S. women's marathon record holder) says she gains confidence from putting together several consecutive weeks of solid training.
- Three versions with a similar Q-session approach: miles, kilometers, and time. One may switch between distance-based and time-based programs to see which imposes the least stress.
- Miles and km programs: for runners with considerable running time who prefer NOT 2 Q runs per week; a Q session about every 4th or 5th day.
- Choose a reasonable peak (P) weekly mileage; each week gives a fraction of P (e.g., P = 100 miles, .8P = 80 miles; P = 120 km, .8P = 96 km).
- E day = 1 or 2 E runs, or no run if rest is needed; use E days to reach weekly distance goals. If a rest day is needed, use an E day and fit the total into the other 6 days.
- If long sessions are possible only on weekends, move days around but include all detailed workouts. Schedule is flexible; the most important thing is keeping the quality sessions in the listed order.
- Miles program assumes ability to handle a single steady run of at least 2 hours and a week of 100 or more miles. Km program assumes serious running beforehand, a steady run of at least 2 hours and a week of at least 125 km; use VDOT tables for M and T paces.
- Miles program: I pace is the speed you could race for about 2.5 to 3 miles, or from VDOT tables. On 2 E days each week run six to eight 20-second ST (about mile R race pace, not sprints). R pace is current mile race pace.
tags: type:methodology type:periodization sport:running phase:build level:advanced subtopic:18-week-marathon subtopic:peak-mileage
evidence: author-experience
src: Ch 16 "Final 18-Week Programs"

### [DRF-C08-042] 18-Week Program in Time: notation and rules

- Time-based training encourages running by feel rather than exact pace; useful on undulating terrain or windy days where timed distances mislead. Author encourages trying time-based workouts even in good conditions to relate feel to effort.
- Numbers are total minutes of each type of run. L = long E run. Break T totals into repeated runs: T 40 = 40 min at T pace, e.g., 8 × 5 min, 5 × 8 min, 4 × 10 min (1 or 2 min rests).
- TL 40-70 = 40 total min at T (e.g., 4 × 10 min w/2 min rests) plus a 70-min L (E) run.
- TIR 15-10-5 = 15 min total at T (e.g., 3 × 5 min, w/1 min rests) + 10 min total at I (e.g., 5 × 2 min H [hard] w/equal-time recovery jg) + 5 min total at R (e.g., 5 × 1 min R; maybe 4-5 × 400 or 8-10 × 200 w/double-time rests).
- MT 80-20 = steady 80 min at M + 20 total min at T (e.g., 4 × 5 min w/1 min rests or 2 × 10 min w/2 min rests).
- ME 80-60 = 80 min at M followed immediately by 60 min E.
- T race up to 25K = find a race of medium distance (15K to 25K); switch days if none available; if no races near this time, pick a favorite workout.
- No warm-up needed for E, L or M sessions; warm up for workouts that start with T.
- Unspecified days are E days; run as needed for weekly mileage goals (may not need to run every day). Do six to eight strides on 2 of each week's untimed days.
- Pace relations: T is 15 to 20 seconds per mile faster than M; I is 6 seconds per 400 faster than T; R is 6 seconds per 400 faster than I.
- Keep quality sessions in the listed order; schedule flexible.
tags: type:methodology type:definition sport:running metric:rpe metric:pace level:advanced subtopic:time-based-training subtopic:18-week-marathon
evidence: author-experience
src: Ch 16 "18-Week Program in Time"

### [DRF-C08-043] Final 12-week program: use rules and pace conversion

- Two Q days each week, on whichever days fit; all other days E running to accumulate desired weekly mileage. Try to have at least 2 E days between Q sessions.
- Second column of Table 16.9 = fraction of peak weekly miles (e.g., peak 110: .8 = 88 miles). Set a realistic peak for the season.
- Plan is for sea level. At 7,000 feet (2,130 m): keep R pace the same as sea level; M, T and I paces are all 4 seconds per 400 (10 sec/km, 16 sec/mile) slower. Adjust speeds for wind, heat, poor footing.
- If no distance units are shown, assume miles (8 M = 8 miles at M pace).
- Without VDOT units: choose a realistic goal M pace; final T pace = 15 seconds per mile faster than goal M pace; final I pace = 6 seconds per 400 m faster than T; final R pace = 3 seconds per 200 m faster than I.
tags: type:methodology type:formula sport:running phase:build level:advanced subtopic:12-week-marathon subtopic:altitude subtopic:pace-conversion
evidence: author-experience
src: Ch 16 "Final 12-Week Program"

### [DRF-C08-044] Ultra training structure: long runs, volume and weekly pattern (Lewy-Boulet)

For a 50-mile or 100-mile (80-160 km) race:
- Weekly long runs: mostly 2.5 to 4.5 hours; distance covered varies with ability and terrain.
- Longer effort of 50K or 100K once a month or every other month, sometimes in an actual race or event.
- Back-to-back long runs on the weekend about once a month: 3 to 4 hours Saturday, then 2 to 3 hours Sunday.
- Long run every week unless a recovery week is built in. Occasionally two long runs a week.
- Long-run pace: not fast at all. Ultrarunning is about spending time on your feet being as comfortable as possible for a very long time, and about teaching the body to fuel over hours of exercise.
- Hilly long runs when training for a hilly race, including practice of efficient power hiking up steep hills.
- Runs 1 or 2 times per day, 6 days per week, one day off weekly to optimize recovery.
- Weekly running time: 10 to 14 hours, almost half on weekends with double long runs.
- Running to and from work is used to fit in training.
- Race tune-ups: marathon-distance and 50K races as preparation for 50-mile or 100-mile races.
tags: type:methodology sport:running level:advanced system:aerobic metric:volume metric:pace subtopic:ultrarunning subtopic:long-run
evidence: author-experience
src: Ch 17 Q&A

### [DRF-C08-045] Ultra training: R and T quality work (Lewy-Boulet)

- R (repetition) workouts: once every week, mostly 200s, 8 to 12 reps per session. Purpose: keeps overall fitness up, makes her feel stronger at slower paces, and is a nice weekly change to do something fast.
- T (threshold) workouts: a mainstay of training, once a week; total 30 to 60 minutes of work at T pace or effort, broken into chunks of 5, 10 or 15 minutes.
- T workouts are sometimes done on a treadmill on an incline, to be more specific to the racing.
tags: type:workout sport:running level:advanced system:threshold system:neuromuscular metric:pace subtopic:ultrarunning
evidence: author-experience
src: Ch 17 Q&A

### [DRF-C08-046] Triathlon training: fresh sessions plus fatigued sessions

- Triathlon training requires two types of training: (1) practice each discipline when relatively fresh; (2) replicate race conditions, since in a race the athlete is already fatigued when starting the cycling and running legs. So schedule a run right after getting off the bike.
- Practice transitions swim-to-bike and bike-to-run to learn how the body reacts to these changes in demand.
- The run always starts fatigued from the swim and bike, so run training can take place before complete recovery from another event workout. However, it is good to sometimes do run training first, when fresh and rested.
- Best fresh runs: at Threshold (T) intensity or prolonged and Easy (E).
- Unlike modern pentathlon (one event per day for five days, so order didn't matter), triathlon requires attention to the order of training.
- Paired same-discipline sessions: each schedule has an afternoon session in one discipline followed next morning by another workout in the same discipline, stressing the same system. The morning workout feels like finishing a single demanding session because overnight rest does not allow full recovery from the previous afternoon.
tags: type:methodology sport:triathlon level:all subtopic:brick-workouts subtopic:transitions
evidence: author-experience
src: Ch 18 "Triathlon"

## C09 Decision Rules & Heuristics

### [DRF-C09-001] Avoiding overtraining: hold load constant about four weeks

Rule: stick with the same amount and intensity of training for about four weeks before demanding or taking on more. Let the body adjust to one level of stress before introducing a harder stress. Especially important for highly motivated runners who will do whatever is asked and want more.
tags: type:decision-rule level:all subtopic:progression subtopic:overtraining metric:load sport:running
src: Ch 1 "Intrinsic Motivation"
evidence: author-experience

### [DRF-C09-002] Extreme training approaches: lessons (cautions from anecdotes)

- Author's own early experience: as a new runner, a coach had him run 1-mile warm-up then 10 × 400 near all-out on cinder track in spikes (400 jog recoveries), 5 days/week for 6 weeks. Result: severe shin pain; and he only knew all-out running, so he ran 4,000 m races all out from the start and was in a "death trot" after about 800 m. Lessons: purpose-less hard daily repetitions cause pain and injury; runners must be taught pacing for the race distance.
- Example of a champion's extreme week (17-year-old, later world-record holder and 3-time Olympian): Sunday 10 miles (16 km) in 64 min; other 6 days a morning 4-mile (6.4 km) run plus afternoon track session. Afternoon sessions: Mon 9:55 2 mile + 2 × 5:15 miles + 3 × 2:28 800s + 6 × 65 sec 400s + weights + 4-mile cool-down; Tue 6 × 400 at 64 + 10 × 140 at 18 + 5 × 200 at 31; Wed 50 × 400 at 69 sec on 3-min send-off; Thu 18 × 800 at 2:45; Fri 1 mile + 1,200 + 800 + 600 + 400 + 3 miles easy cool-down; Sat race. Presented as NOT a model: 24 teammates did the 50 × 400, and the coach dismissed the one who did 40 as not going to be good.
- Another national record holder: several 66-mile (106 km) long runs, 380 miles (611 km) in one week, averaged 320 miles (515 km)/week for 6 weeks and 240 miles (386 km)/week for one year. Author doubts many could do and survive this.
- Conclusion: follow principles of training rather than extremes.
tags: type:caution sport:running level:all subtopic:overtraining subtopic:extreme-training
evidence: author-experience
src: Ch 2 "Extreme Approaches to Training"

### [DRF-C09-003] Principle 5: Personal limits, race-based intensity, and when to raise intensity

- Everyone has limits; not absolute limits, but seasonal limits imposed by lifestyle at a given time (e.g., class/lab schedules in college that prevent practice; work and home demands in non-students).
- Avoid overtraining; best prevention is constant runner-coach interaction.
- Training intensities should be determined by current fitness, best measured by race performances. Standard answer to a runner wanting faster training: "Prove to me in a race that you are ready to train faster."
- Exception: when a runner has trained at a specific intensity for 4 to 6 weeks without feeling any increase in stress, then, without a race to go by, intensity can be increased slightly.
- Stress table (Table 2.1) tracks life issues to relate daily activities/stresses to good or bad performances. [IMAGE NOT PROCESSED — table content not available; cells not recorded.]
- Scoring considerations for each item: quality of last night's sleep; injury, illness or unusual pains; recovery from yesterday's training; flexibility; previous 24 hours of rest, energy and nutrition; physical stress of today's session; how stressful today was for general mental/emotional state; any added category.
tags: type:principle type:decision-rule type:monitoring sport:running level:all subtopic:personal-limits subtopic:overtraining subtopic:stress-table metric:load
evidence: author-experience
src: Ch 2, Principle 5; Table 2.1

### [DRF-C09-004] Foot strike guidance and troubleshooting

- Foot strike varies by runner and event: sprinters/some middle-distance on balls of feet; many distance runners and marathoners heel-first or midfoot.
- New runners should experiment and use the strike that is most comfortable, least fatiguing, and allows light quick turnover of about 180 steps/min. Often just focusing on 180 steps/min yields the best strike.
- If calf or shin discomfort: concentrate on mid- or rear-foot landing for a few weeks to see if it resolves.
- Avoid turning toes outward on landing: have someone watch from the front to check toes point straight ahead. Turned-out landing often leads to shin pain along the inside of the lower leg.
tags: type:decision-rule type:caution sport:running level:all subtopic:foot-strike subtopic:shin-pain subtopic:calf-discomfort
evidence: author-experience
src: Ch 2 "Foot Strike"

### [DRF-C09-005] Using breathing rhythm to judge effort and pacing (2-2 for first two-thirds)

- Effort gauge: if you can breathe comfortably 3-3 during a long steady run, you are not working too hard; if you must breathe 2-2, you are not working that easily. Still, 2-2 is probably the way for easy long runs; try 3-3 for a few minutes just to gauge effort.
- Threshold check: if you cannot use 2-2 and must go to 2-1 or 1-2 during threshold running, you are going too hard.
- Racing rule for younger runners: breathe 2-2 for the first two-thirds of a race (e.g., first 2 miles of a 5K cross country race), then 2-1 or 1-2 for the final third. If unable to hold 2-2 for the first two-thirds, they went out too fast; next time set an easier early pace.
- No need to monitor breathing constantly.
tags: type:decision-rule type:monitoring sport:running level:all subtopic:breathing-rhythm subtopic:pacing subtopic:effort-gauge metric:rpe
evidence: author-experience
src: Ch 2 "Breathing Rhythms"

### [DRF-C09-006] Heart rate in training: conditions and purpose of workout

- Devices showing HR, breathing rhythm and stride rate are useful if they are tied to reasonable scientific data.
- Intensity is not always the same at the same speed. In heat, the heart works harder at any speed (more blood to skin for cooling, so higher total blood flow and HR). Thus, using HR to monitor a given speed gives a slower speed than anticipated.
- Same for headwind, hills, rough or muddy footing: shooting for a specific HR gives a slower-than-hoped speed. The slower speed under poor conditions often still reflects the desired intensity.
- Rule: ask "What is the purpose of this workout?" If the purpose is time at a specific speed, do not let HR be the guide. If intensity of effort is the goal, HR is very useful.
- HR monitoring is good as long as runners understand how HR varies with conditions.
tags: type:decision-rule sport:running metric:hr,pace subtopic:heart-rate-monitoring subtopic:environment level:all
evidence: author-experience
src: Ch 3 "Heart Rate During Training Runs"

### [DRF-C09-007] Long-run limit: use time not distance; slow marathoners

- Cap L runs by time (150 min), not distance. Top runners do 20-mile runs in 2-2.5 h; 2.5 h is enough even if a slower runner covers only 15 miles (24 km).
- A 6-7 hour marathoner doing 20-mile (about 5 h) training runs is too much stress for a beginner; non-elite runners should not train longer than the best do.
- Walking-running marathoners may stay out longer than 150 min but build gradually.
tags: type:decision-rule sport:running metric:volume level:beginner subtopic:long-run
src: Ch 4 "Long Runs and Increasing Mileage"
evidence: author-experience

### [DRF-C09-008] Reading Table 5.2: worked example and work-bout limits for I and R

Example VDOT 51 (best performance a 5:44 mile, from Table 5.1):
