- E/L pace: 4:52-5:29 per km, or 7:49-8:49 per mile; very comfortable, conversational.
- M pace: 4:27/km, 7:09/mile.
- T pace: 1:40/400, 4:11/km, 6:44/mile.
- I pace: 92/400 (3:04/800), 3:51/km, 4:36/1,200.
- R pace: 43/200, 64/300, 86/400.
Rules:
- I training: no individual work bout longer than 5 minutes. So I distances are listed only where a bout fits. I miles are not appropriate until VDOT 66.
- R training: no individual work bout longer than 2 minutes. R 600s start at VDOT 56. R 800s need a VDOT of about 77 or greater.
- It is fine to go a little outside the ranges now and then. E.g. a VDOT 70 runner could run R 800s in 2:10.
- A faster "fast rep" R pace is used for 800 m runners (see chapter 11).
tags: type:decision-rule type:zones level:all subtopic:work-bout-duration-limit subtopic:interval subtopic:repetition sport:running system:vo2max system:anaerobic metric:pace
src: Ch 5
evidence: author-experience

### [DRF-C09-009] Altitude training considerations (13 points)

1. Author likes altitude training with intermittent sea-level exposures (some days at a time) and occasionally higher altitudes; clean, dry, cool air makes E runs more enjoyable and escapes summer heat/humidity.
2. Practice altitude races to become competitively acclimatized, even months before the target altitude competition.
3. Altitude is one of several training stresses; many runners experience more discomfort and learn how to hurt, managing discomfort better.
4. Allow adaptation to sea level after altitude; racing almost immediately on return is not required, especially with intermittent sea-level exposures.
5. Benefits are not transient: fitness can be maintained months later provided the new fitness level continues to be stressed with increased training speeds at sea level (like gains from higher mileage).
6. Believe in altitude training if you try it, but if it does not work, change.
7. Runners often fare better on day 1 at altitude than a few days later; dehydration is common in the first several days: drink plenty of fluids (maintains blood volume) and rest regularly.
8. Stress adaptation to continued altitude exposure is sped up by intermittent sea-level exposure: a study of adrenaline and noradrenaline in daily 24-hour urine suggested several days at sea level reduced daily stress upon return to altitude.
9. For lowlanders preparing for an altitude competition: do a time trial or race upon acute exposure (contrary to usual advice) to face reality early and accept needed changes; a second test a couple of weeks later will invariably improve, a psychological boost.
10. Training can be normal in amount and relative intensity without being initially more demanding than at sea level; for R workouts take longer recoveries if needed.
11. Endurance athletes at altitude may adapt and also step up in fitness, so sea-level and altitude performances can both improve.
tags: type:decision-rule sport:running level:all subtopic:altitude subtopic:acclimatization subtopic:hydration
evidence: author-experience
src: Ch 6 "Incorporating Altitude Training"

### [DRF-C09-010] Racing at altitude: pacing rules

- Do not go out too fast: do not match your sea-level opening speed for the same distance. Be a little overly cautious in early minutes; going out as at sea level demands more anaerobic energy and leads to a slower latter part. Put more in only after at least halfway if not working too hard.
- Races up to and including 800 m are not affected by altitude: may hurt more but run about your sea-level time (1968 Mexico City Olympics 800 winner, a sea-level native, tied the Olympic record).
- Table 6.1 times are for runners who have spent some time at altitude; with no acclimatization, pace must be a fair bit slower.
tags: type:decision-rule sport:running level:all subtopic:altitude-racing metric:pace
evidence: author-experience
src: Ch 6 "Racing at Altitude"

### [DRF-C09-011] Racing at sea level after altitude: timing

- Best timing varies among athletes; takes a couple of weeks at altitude for altitude performances to improve.
- Weather: altitude is cool and dry. If the sea-level race is warm and humid, allow a week or more to adjust; if cool and dry, may be ready as soon as you come down.
- Longer race, longer needed at sea level. Short races (mile, 1,500): Jim Ryun ran a world-record mile (3:51.1, June 23, 1967) the evening he returned after 3 weeks at altitude and a world-record 1,500 (3:33.1) a day later. Hyperventilation (breathing more than necessary) persists for some days after descent but short races are over before it matters.
- Marathon: if warm and humid at sea level, the body will not respond well for 10 to 14 days; breathing settles over days.
- Other distances longer than the mile: a week or more is probably better than arriving just in time, especially with differing weather. Runners with several sea-level trips during altitude phase prepare better.
tags: type:decision-rule sport:running level:all subtopic:altitude subtopic:return-to-sea-level
evidence: author-experience
src: Ch 6 "Racing at Sea Level After Training at Altitude"

### [DRF-C09-012] Sharing a treadmill: alternating work and rest

Two runners can share one treadmill when both want the same intensity: one runs while the other rests off the belt, swapping every 30 or 60 seconds (30/30 or 60/60 work/rest), or every 1 minute for a 1-minute uphill rep with 1-minute rest. Daniels used this with his college teams for many years with considerable success.
tags: type:methodology sport:running system:vo2max level:all subtopic:treadmill-partner
evidence: author-experience
src: Ch 7 intro and "Treadmill Training Intensity"

### [DRF-C09-013] Using grade on the treadmill: rules of thumb

- Adding 1 percent to treadmill grade is similar to increasing running speed by about 10 to 15 seconds per mile.
- Equivalent work is possible at slow speeds and steep grades, but use faster speeds when attempting comparable R training.
- Treadmill is best for I, T and steady runs; very good I workouts are done at steep grades and slower speeds.
- Suggested I workouts: 20 × 30 seconds with 30-second rests; if not too demanding, 10 × 1 minute with 1-minute rests.
tags: type:decision-rule sport:running system:vo2max metric:pace level:all subtopic:treadmill-intervals
evidence: author-experience
src: Ch 7 "Treadmill Training Intensity"

### [DRF-C09-014] Race proximity rules in blue and gold plans

- Blue: at least 2 E days before races, deleting a training day if needed.
- Gold: 2-3 E days before; after a race, 1 E day per 3,000 m of race distance (3 E days after 10K, 5 after 15K); the last pre-race quality session is 3 × 1 mile T with 2 min rest.
tags: type:decision-rule level:advanced subtopic:race-recovery sport:running
src: Ch 8 Blue and Gold plans
evidence: author-experience

### [DRF-C09-015] Handling an unplanned break (injury)

- Accept that a break is necessary and do what is needed to fix the problem. If returning to serious training matters, supplemental training can minimize fitness loss.
- Some cross-training builds resistance to further problems, so running may be better than before. E.g., resistance training may improve body balance and running economy; research shows half-squats with relatively light weights can improve running performance. An unplanned break can be a blessing in disguise if it leads to new exercise modes.
- Injuries are of two types: those preventing use of the legs (e.g., broken leg: only arm-based aerobic work such as swimming) and those allowing aerobic involvement of some leg muscles (e.g., ankle/foot injury: deep-water running trains hip and many leg muscles seriously).
- Options without impact: elliptical trainers, cycling, uphill treadmill walking.
- Make sure cross-training does not aggravate the problem and prolong time off.
- Any injury lasting more than a couple of days: consult a physical therapist, athletic trainer or medical doctor. Something that seems minor may become a major problem; something that seems major may be simple with proper advice.
tags: type:decision-rule level:all subtopic:injury subtopic:cross-training subtopic:deep-water-running sport:running
evidence: author-experience
src: Ch 9 "Nonplanned Breaks"

### [DRF-C09-016] Hill running rules (uphill and downhill)

- Downhill: too steep a course or hard road surfaces raise the risk of hurting hips, knees and feet. Use a gradual hill of only 2 or 3 percent slope (interstate maximum is 6%; stay well below). Avoid overstriding; use light, fast leg turnover; "roll" down rather than bound; it may help to land rear-footed rather than on the balls of the feet.
- Uphill: any slope steepness is fine, since pounding is less than on flat; strengthens push-off muscles and hip flexors with less landing shock. Run back down the outdoor hill very easily. Treadmill uphill runs let you hop off for a break with no descent needed.
tags: type:decision-rule type:caution level:all subtopic:hill-running subtopic:downhill sport:running
evidence: author-experience
src: Ch 9 "Adding Supplemental Training"

### [DRF-C09-017] Information to gather before setting up a season

Gather before planning:
- Fitness level: current mileage and speeds of workouts, to establish the proper VDOT value for all types of training.
- Most important goal race of the season, plus races available or desired along the way. Some runners prefer racing shorter than the goal race, others over-distance; this shapes each week.
- Facilities: indoor track for poor weather, treadmill to avoid very cold, windy or hot days, smooth grass course for some workouts.
- Time available for training and which days give the most time; best times of day given other commitments (school schedules are consistent in high school, vary in college; jobs dictate for post-college runners).
- Runner profile (Figure 3.8, p. 48): weekly mileage, longest recent run, recent races and workouts.
Flexibility and dedication are needed to keep training when outdoor conditions are poor.
tags: type:methodology sport:running level:all subtopic:season-planning subtopic:runner-profile
evidence: author-experience
src: Ch 10 intro; Phase section | p. [n/a]

### [DRF-C09-018] Skipping phases based on recent training

Ask each runner what workouts they have recently done.
- Running regularly for 6 or more weeks: OK to go straight into phase II.
- Previous 3 or 4 weeks included relatively demanding workouts with no setbacks: fine to go into phase III.
tags: type:decision-rule sport:running level:all subtopic:season-planning
evidence: author-experience
src: Ch 10 "Break your season into phases" | p. [n/a]

### [DRF-C09-019] Late starters, injured or ill runners

A runner with only 3 weeks left in the season who has not been training: do only easy foundation (phase I) training; no sense in stressful quality work. Same approach for any runner returning from a break due to injury or illness. Worst thing: a demanding workout to make up for lost time. The less the body has been stressed recently, the more benefit from not imposing much stress. Better a little below optimum and healthy than unable to race through overtraining or illness.
tags: type:decision-rule type:caution sport:running level:all subtopic:return-from-injury subtopic:late-start
evidence: author-experience
src: Ch 10 "Adapt the phase lengths" | p. [n/a]

### [DRF-C09-020] 800 m: scheduling and race-week decision rules

- Q sessions must fall when runner is not fatigued from previous Q or race; skip a Q if not feeling up to it.
- Weekly race: 2 E days before races; 3 E days before championship or equally important races (20-60 mile plans).
- Weeks ending in races: eliminate Q3; the race replaces Q3 (Phases III-IV).
- On low-stress race days with adequate time: consider adding 6 × 200 R w/200 jg after racing.
- Very important race in Phase IV: one early-week limited Q, no faster than recent sessions.
- Final pre-race Q: 3-4 days before race, T pace, maybe light 200 m reps.
- Don't raise training speed more often than every 3rd-4th week.
- Weekly mileage may be reduced for a health problem or undue stress (50-60 mile plan).
- Non-Q days are E; an E day may be little or no running if a day off is desired.
tags: type:decision-rule sport:running level:all subtopic:race-week subtopic:800-meter
evidence: author-experience
src: Ch 11 Phases II-IV; mileage sections

### [DRF-C09-021] Race pacing for 1,500/mile (go out cautiously)

- In races of about 4 to 12 minutes, runners tend to go too fast too soon. Daniels encourages almost overdoing a cautious start.
- When aiming for a good time (tactics and weather not a major concern): run the first 400 m conservatively and make the second 400 about 2 seconds faster than the first.
- Reason: younger runners often go out several seconds too fast, so the second 400 is much slower. In many mile races the third 400 equals the second, and a fast first minute followed by a major slowdown gives a slow third 400 and a missed time.
- A cautious first 400 plus a focused faster second 400 often yields a good third 400; the last 400 is then hanging on.
- Try a variety of approaches to see what works best for you.
- Racing the mile makes 2-mile pace seem comfortable; racing the 2 mile or 3K makes the 1,500/mile seem short. Runners like both shorter and longer races.
tags: type:decision-rule sport:running phase:race metric:pace level:all subtopic:race-pacing
src: Ch 12 intro | n/a
evidence: author-experience

### [DRF-C09-022] 5K/10K plan scheduling rules (races, VDOT, Q sessions)

Applies to both 40-50 mile and 60-70 mile plans (Tables 13.1 and 13.2):
- Phase III, coming Saturday race: either move Q2 and Q3 to Tuesday and Wednesday, or for a more important Saturday race eliminate Q2 and make Q3 the Q2 of that week (place it on Tuesday).
- Races in phase III: adjust training VDOT as races predict, but do not increase VDOT more often than every 3rd week, even if a race suggests it.
- Phase III structure: alternate weeks of L and M runs; other two Q sessions usually on back-to-back days, an I or H day followed by a T or T and R day.
- Phase IV assumes weekend races:
  - Saturday race: follow the schedule, with E days Wednesday, Thursday, Friday leading up to that Saturday race.
  - Sunday race: move Q2 to the Wednesday before the race, with E days Thursday, Friday, Saturday leading up to the Sunday race.
- Include warm-up runs before any Q2 and Q3 sessions.
- Specified days for Q sessions can be rearranged to suit weather or personal demands.
- Numbers with no distance units mean miles (e.g., 2 E = 2 miles easy running).
- Phase IV week 3 (in any of the next 4 weeks with an important race): cut L run to 90 minutes.
- L run = lesser of 25% of week's mileage and 120 min, whichever comes first.
tags: type:decision-rule sport:running phase:build phase:race level:intermediate subtopic:race-week-scheduling
evidence: author-experience
src: Ch 13 plan intros | n/a

### [DRF-C09-023] Cross country: wind, course knowledge and terrain-specific training

- Drafting: going into a wind, draft off others; the same pace costs more energy alone or leading a group.
- Before a race, go over the course on race day to know the situations faced and when.
- Do some, if not most, training over typical terrain; it is hard to race on soft footing if all training was on track/hard road. Train on surfaces like those of the season's most important races.
- Home course: plan how to use it where it may be difficult for competitors; know how you react to a hill and what follows it, to know when to relax and when to push.
- I training: some runners do better by duration than distance, especially on hilly or difficult terrain; hitting specific times for set distances disappoints on poor footing or hills. Like I training at altitude: know you are working hard for a set time, which accomplishes the workout purpose.
- Example cited: Lynn Jennings (nine-time USA Cross Country champion) did intervals on grassy fields, fartlek and tempo on roads, and embraced variable weather and footing.
tags: type:decision-rule sport:running level:all subtopic:terrain subtopic:drafting subtopic:time-based-intervals
src: Ch 14 intro
evidence: author-experience

### [DRF-C09-024] Team strategy: pack running

In easy races, new runners can run in a pack. Pack running often works for the first third or so of a race, then better runners break away from slower teammates to see how they do in the latter stages. Benefits both: keeps better runners from going out too fast and builds confidence and tactics in slower runners.
tags: type:principle sport:running level:beginner subtopic:pack-running
src: Ch 14 intro
evidence: author-experience

### [DRF-C09-025] Conservative-start approach in cross country

Why: in many races (especially high school) the fastest runners all go out too fast, slow a fair amount, but still win because everyone else also went out too fast; they conclude fast starts win. A good runner who starts at a reasonable pace beats better runners who start too fast.
Example (national championship, author's women's team): first 400 m measured; team ran a solid 800 m preparation 8-10 min before the race, through the first 400 in 85 s (about a 17:42 5K if held). They were told to go no faster. In the race all seven went through 400 m in 84-87 s and were last seven of 180+ runners; front runners went under 75 s, others 75-82 s. At 1 mile one of them led the entire pack and won by more than 20 s (about 17:20); others placed 5th, 8th, 15th, 26th; team title won.
Points to keep in mind:
- Courses often narrow early; you can get squeezed back and lose confidence, but there is usually plenty of time to move in the middle mile of a 5K; battling for a front spot early costs reserve energy needed later.
- Young runners get discouraged seeing a mass ahead after a couple of minutes; coach should practice conservative starts in training and early-season races so they see the benefits.
- Pass as many runners as possible (even count them) in the middle mile. Math: passing 20 in the middle and being outkicked by 3 at the end = +17 team points; passing none and outkicking 3 at the end = +3. Outkicking others in the last 100 m often means not working hard enough mid-race.
- If running with a group mid-race and feeling bad, all near you feel the same, or they would have gone by.
- Initial pace gets faster the more important the race (conference < regional < state/national, even with the same runners), so pace control at the start matters more as championships approach.
Summary rule: start races conservatively, and be willing to work harder as the race progresses.
tags: type:decision-rule sport:running level:all subtopic:pacing subtopic:conservative-start metric:pace
src: Ch 14 "Using a Conservative-Start Approach"
evidence: author-experience

### [DRF-C09-026] In-race focus, hills and breathing in cross country

- Focus on the task at hand (breathing rhythm, stride rate) rather than distance remaining or covered. Do not worry about runners you beat before being ahead; they may be pacing poorly, or may be having a great day. Judge by what you do and feel, not by others.
- Uphills: rather than charging, use as little effort as possible to stay with others uphill, then concentrate on a solid effort after the top, when others often back off. Maintain solid pace counting 50 footfalls after the top; runners often recover well while moving ahead.
- Breathing: practice a 2-2 breathing rhythm for the first two-thirds of early-season races so it becomes normal in important races.
- Learn to go by feel, especially with varying terrain, wind and mud which slow pace; do not worry about split times heard on course (may be inaccurate).
tags: type:decision-rule sport:running level:all subtopic:breathing subtopic:hills subtopic:going-by-feel metric:rpe
src: Ch 14 "Preparing for the Race"
evidence: author-experience

### [DRF-C09-027] Cross country training summary rules

- Weekly L run, preferably Sunday.
- Do much Q training on grass or dirt trails similar to race conditions.
- 2 E days before most midseason races.
- Last Q session before a weekly race mostly T intensity.
- Races give benefits similar to I sessions.
- Stop hill work for the last several weeks before important races.
- Have a reasonable goal for each race.
- Do not try to train harder in the final few weeks of the season.
- Start races conservatively, work harder as the race progresses.
- Focus on the task at hand.
tags: type:decision-rule sport:running level:all subtopic:cross-country
src: Ch 14 summary

### [DRF-C09-028] Post-race recovery rule (15K-30K)

After any race, take 1 E day of running for every 3K of race distance before returning to the alien program. Examples: 15K race = 5 E days; half marathon = 7 E days. Then pick up the next Q day as appropriate and complete that week.
tags: type:decision-rule sport:running level:all subtopic:post-race-recovery phase:transition
evidence: author-experience
src: Ch 15 "Alien Training"; Table 15.1

### [DRF-C09-029] Race week and post-race recovery rules (5-week cycle)

- With a weekend race, replace the back-to-back midweek sessions with one relatively comfortable T session 4 days before the race.
- After races, plan 1 day of easy recovery running for each 3,000 meters of race distance: 3 E days after a 10K, 5 E days after a 15K, 7 E days after a half marathon, 14 E days after a full marathon.
- Last 3 weeks before the race: eliminate weekly strides and run mostly on flat terrain, to get the feeling of floating over the ground.
- Final 2 weeks: eliminate hill training and follow the 1st week of the training program, making the final week's L run a 90-minute effort. Instead of R sessions, focus on L and T sessions, completing the T session 4 or 5 days prior to the race.
tags: type:decision-rule type:recovery sport:running phase:taper phase:race metric:volume level:all subtopic:post-race-recovery subtopic:marathon
evidence: author-experience
src: Ch 16 "5-Week Cycle"

### [DRF-C09-030] Ultra: time-of-day and night/fatigue practice

- Long runs are preferred first thing in the morning (best fit for her day).
- For races lasting 20 hours or more, do some late-evening or night runs: the brain behaves differently after a full day awake, so practice how you feel mentally then.
- Become comfortable running in the dark with a headlamp.
- Running after a full work day (mentally fatigued) simulates the later stages of an ultra.
tags: type:decision-rule sport:running level:advanced subtopic:ultrarunning subtopic:night-running
evidence: author-experience
src: Ch 17 Q&A

### [DRF-C09-031] Building a triathlon week: choose workouts to fit daily life

Look over the sessions in the schedules and pick workouts you can put together in a given week. Paula Newby-Fraser (the Queen of Kona, 8 Ironman World Championship and 21 Ironman Triathlon titles) recommends building the program on the realities of daily life, and efficiently and comfortably accomplishing the weekly distances wanted in the water, on the bike and on the road.
tags: type:decision-rule sport:triathlon level:all subtopic:individualization
evidence: author-experience
src: Ch 18 "Triathlon"

## C10 Applied Guidance

### [DRF-C10-001] Stride rate: about 180 steps per minute

- Observation (1984 Los Angeles Olympics, ~50 runners, 800 to marathon, males and females): only one runner took fewer than 180 steps/min. Turnover well over 200/min in the 800 and sometimes 1,500; from 3,000 through marathon rate was similar and only stride length shortened as distance increased.
- Marathon gold medalist: 184 at 7:00/mile, 186 at 6:00, 190 at 5:00 pace; speed rises mainly via stride length; runners keep a preferred rhythm.
- Recommendation: stride rate around 180 steps/min to minimize landing shock: slower turnover means more air time, body mass elevated higher, harder landing; impact causes many small injuries.
- Minimize landing shock: roll over the ground rather than bound; avoid placing the foot out in front (braking, higher impact); land closer back under the center of gravity. Cue: imagine running over raw eggs without breaking them.
- Counting: count only one foot (right or left) and look for 90 per 60 s (assuming equal steps each side).
tags: type:principle type:dose sport:running level:all subtopic:stride-rate subtopic:landing-shock metric:cadence
evidence: author-experience
src: Ch 2 "Stride Rate", "180 Steps per Minute"

### [DRF-C10-002] Breathing rhythms: 2-2 recommended; ventilation comparison

- Minute ventilation = size × number of breaths. Breathing rate is typically in rhythm with stride rate. Rhythm notation: steps in / steps out (e.g., 2-2).
- Easy running: possibly 3-3, increasing breath size. Harder: 2-2. Most accomplished runners use 2-2 when fairly hard. Recommendation: use 2-2 in practice and competition, at least the first two-thirds of middle-distance races, and even in easy runs and in threshold, interval and repetition workouts so it becomes natural.
- Worked comparison (at 180 steps/min): 4-4 = 22.5 breaths/min (author writes about 22) × 4 L = 90 L/min; 3-3 = 30 breaths × 3.5 L = 105 L/min (16 percent more); 2-2 = 45 breaths × about 3 L = 135 L/min; 1-1 gives much smaller breaths, possibly less total air than slower deeper breathing, and more dead-space air (air not reaching the lungs for gas exchange) per minute. Faster rates also cost more energy of breathing muscles; ventilation and cost favor about 2-2 (or 2-1).
- 2-1 (three steps per cycle) = 60 breaths/min (1 per second): probably greatest air per minute, usually needed only when working very hard (e.g., last part of 5K or 10K).
- About 86 percent of elite runners tested in the lab automatically choose 2-2 until maximum, then go to 2-1 or 1-2.
- Variation: two similar Olympians at maximum effort breathed 160 and 224 L/min, with just over 2.6 and just over 3.6 L per breath.
- Altitude: thinner air offers less airway resistance so faster rates are easier; at least two great runners used 1-1 at altitude when working really hard.
tags: type:principle type:dose sport:running level:all subtopic:breathing-rhythm subtopic:ventilation subtopic:altitude
evidence: author-experience
src: Ch 2 "Breathing Rhythms"

### [DRF-C10-003] Heat preparation, hot-race warm-up and fluid-loss charting

- Cool area runner racing in a hotter climate: sometimes train in the warmer time of day, maybe wear a long-sleeved T-shirt that heats the body. Treadmill training indoors helps adjust to warmth because there is no wind to cool you.
- After each prolonged run, measure undressed body weight before and after. Keep a chart of: time on the run, air temperature, weight lost relative to time running, environmental conditions (cloud cover, wind), and how you felt. How you feel is a good indicator of how conditions affect you.
- Author's 25K study (32 runners, air temp mid-80s °F, very low humidity): two runners finished within 1 minute of each other, both drank exactly 1 liter, prerace weights within 1 pound. One lost 3.5 lb (1.6 kg), the other 8 lb (3.6 kg). Lesson: fluid loss differs greatly between individuals, so chart your own.
- Racing in unusually hot conditions: moderate warm-up; cool down with a wet towel across the shoulders; relax in shade, avoid sun; listen to your body telling you it is working too hard. Cooling the skin does not cool the muscles, so it does not undo the warm-up. Sunglasses can help relax face muscles; relaxing everything that can be relaxed helps performance.
tags: type:decision-rule sport:running level:all subtopic:heat subtopic:fluid-loss subtopic:weigh-in
evidence: author-experience
src: Ch 6 "Temperature Considerations"

### [DRF-C10-004] Treadmill hill training: uphill-only and downhill setup

- Overground hills always include uphill and downhill; on a treadmill you can run up for a period then hop off to recover, getting uphill benefit without downhill. Useful for a runner whose injury is aggravated by downhill landing shock but not uphill (uphill reduces impact). Demanding workouts can be done at a slow pace if the grade is steep enough.
- To train for races with uphill and downhill (e.g., Boston Marathon), create negative grade by placing stout boards (Daniels uses railroad ties) under the back support. Example: treadmill goes to 20%; block raised so display reads 5% when a carpenter's level shows belt at 0%; then setting 0 on the dial = -5%, giving a range of -5% to +15%.
- Treadmill must be solidly attached to the blocks so vibration or a person cannot knock it off.
tags: type:methodology sport:running level:all subtopic:hill-training subtopic:downhill-treadmill
evidence: author-experience
src: Ch 7 "Hill Training"

### [DRF-C10-005] Calories and body weight during a break

- Runners are used to a certain daily calorie intake and a desired body weight. Keeping intake the same during a break easily leads to weight gain.
- For some this may be fine, but during a prolonged break eating habits must change so the body does not gain excess fat tissue.
- "Staying healthy is rule number 1 when you are not exercising as much as you normally do."
- Weight gain further lowers returning VDOT (see Figure 9.2).
tags: type:nutrition level:all subtopic:weight-gain-during-break sport:running
evidence: author-experience
src: Ch 9 "Taking Breaks"

### [DRF-C10-006] Supplemental training: why and what

- New runners usually schedule only running time; non-running activities (stretching, resistance training, massage, ice baths, yoga) may also help running but take time/money; beneficial if you have both.
- Resistance training is shown to improve running performance; the author encourages all runners to include it weekly.
- Machine exercises useful for running: hamstring curls, knee extensions, hip abduction and adduction, abdominal and back exercises. Arm exercises make you feel better but give no real running benefit. With free weights, concentrate first on proper technique and add resistance gradually.
- Greatest benefit of leg strengthening: resistance to common running injuries, allowing more or faster running without injury; the harder training then makes you a better runner.
- Some resistance work also improves running economy (reasons unclear; assumed a more solid base, more controlled stride, less unnecessary movement). Hill running (up and down) also builds strength and economy.
- Supplemental training = cross-training; the point is to stress body areas that take a beating in running. If running longer than built up to, mechanics deteriorate and poor mechanics lead quickly to injury. Whenever technique feels "sloppy", terminate the run and do weights/circuit/other activity.
- Benefits: confidence, economy, fewer minor injuries; even home/backyard exercises count.
tags: type:principle level:all subtopic:resistance-training subtopic:running-economy subtopic:cross-training sport:running
evidence: author-experience
src: Ch 9 "Adding Supplemental Training"

### [DRF-C10-007] Race preparation: individualized warm-up and mental approach

- Team members are not equal; do not assume all follow the same warm-up (e.g., a 4:30 miler vs 5:30 miler). Ideally design the warm-up per runner. Each runner/coach should experiment with routines; some need a few miles, others tire of warming up after a shorter run.
- Warm-up should prepare physiologically and mentally; some runners want to be alone, others with a group; some want no contact with coach in the last hour, others want attention until the gun.
- Mental technique: replay in the mind a race with a positive experience (10-15 s just before the start); can also rerun a disappointing race with changes giving a more positive effort. Focus on positive experiences.
- Physiological: weather matters. Cold: wear more clothing until muscles are warm; hot: minimal clothing to avoid overheating and dehydration before the start.
- It typically takes about 10 minutes for running muscles to start increasing temperature. A couple of degrees aids performance; more than a couple of degrees can lead to subpar performance. For long races do not start with body temperature too high or you will overheat. Runners warm up at different rates; experiment under different environmental conditions.
tags: type:principle sport:running level:all subtopic:warm-up subtopic:mental-preparation
src: Ch 14 "Preparing for the Race"
evidence: author-experience

### [DRF-C10-008] Ultra nutrition: planned fueling and timing principles

- Fueling plan and staying fueled and hydrated is one of the biggest contributors to success in ultraracing. Nutrition must be tailored to the training cycle and race, with planned, purposeful fueling. The plan varies with total distance (e.g. 50K vs 100K), terrain (maybe altitude) and expected weather (heat is terrible).
- Practicing the nutrition plan in training is key: right nutrients, right time, sufficient amounts maximize recovery and positive adaptations. "Eating is training."
- Strategically plan nutrient timing: reduce fat, fiber and protein near workouts but increase them the rest of the day. Carbs are king for high-intensity or very prolonged exercise because the body prefers this fuel. With more than one daily session, top off glycogen in between. Recovery nutrition is critical in heavy training; sufficient protein for muscle repair is essential.
- Train the gut: taking key nutrients (carbohydrate, electrolytes, fluids) during activity increases the gut's absorptive capacity for them, reducing GI problems.
- Train the event-specific plan: leave nothing to chance; know what you will have before, during and after the race, and practice it. Find which forms (solid, liquid, gel) and flavors you tolerate; mimic event conditions as closely as possible.
- No gram or kcal amounts are given.
tags: type:nutrition sport:running level:advanced focus:meal-timing subtopic:gut-training subtopic:race-fueling subtopic:ultrarunning
evidence: author-experience
src: Ch 17 Q&A

### [DRF-C10-009] Ultra supplemental training (Lewy-Boulet)

- Core, balance and stability exercises at least two or three times each week.
- Hiking with a weight vest after long runs.
- Active isolated flexibility sessions at least three times each week, to maintain good range of motion.
tags: type:methodology sport:running level:advanced subtopic:supplemental-training subtopic:flexibility subtopic:ultrarunning
evidence: author-experience
src: Ch 17 Q&A

## C11 Recovery, Monitoring & Lifestyle

### [DRF-C11-001] Core lifestyle triad: eating, resting, hydrating

"Eat well, rest often, and maintain a good state of hydration."
Jack Daniels highlights this foundational triad at the outset of training principles as essential baseline habits supporting running training and recovery.
tags: type:principle type:recovery type:nutrition sport:running level:all subtopic:lifestyle subtopic:hydration subtopic:recovery
evidence: author-experience
src: Ch 2 epigraph

### [DRF-C11-002] Resting (waking) heart rate

- Note HR on waking in the morning to track fitness. With training it typically gets slower as the heart strengthens and increases stroke volume (more blood per beat, so fewer beats for the same delivery).
- A waking HR considerably higher than your normal can indicate overtraining: you may need rest or a health checkup.
tags: type:monitoring sport:running metric:hr subtopic:resting-hr subtopic:overtraining level:all
evidence: author-experience
src: Ch 3 "Resting Heart Rate"

## C12 Adaptations by Population / Context

### [DRF-C12-001] Four runner categories by ability and motivation; coaching each

Runners fall into four groups:
1. Great ability + high motivation: the champions.
2. Outstanding ability, little/no motivation: "coach frustrators". Should not be yelled at ("give it your all"), which drives them away. Provide an encouraging environment; a thoughtful coach may increase their interest, or encourage them toward whatever pursuit they are motivated for. One tactic: ask runners to feel the workouts and feel the same workout getting easier before increasing training stress; focusing only on raising stress can make running too stressful.
3. Little ability, high motivation: fun, supportive, do anything the coach says. Risk: they do whatever is asked and want more, so the coach must hold them back to avoid injury. Late bloomers exist (examples: a runner with best high school mile 4:34 later set an indoor world record and placed ninth in the Olympic 1,500; a woman not in the top seven until junior year of college won seven national collegiate titles).
4. Little ability, no interest: "no-shows"; do not come out.
Survey of high school runners ("Why did you take up running?"): only about 12 percent said "I wanted to be a runner"; others wanted fitness for another sport, were cut from another sport, or were pushed/encouraged. Daniels sees this as lack of physical education to spot talent; running is often used as punishment ("give me a lap").
Every runner needs short-term and long-term goals; the pace of others' progress should not discourage slower progressors. Treat everyone as an individual; teach runners to be optimists (internalize good things, externalize unfortunate things).
tags: type:principle level:general-population subtopic:motivation subtopic:coaching sport:running
src: Ch 1 "Intrinsic Motivation"
evidence: author-experience

### [DRF-C12-002] VDOT performance levels (1-10) by sex, and age adjustment (Tables 5.4-5.6)

Concept: VDOT-based comparative levels let male and female runners (and different ages) be compared. Increments are about 5 VDOT per level over 10 levels (a coach can say "seven level-6 girls"). Level 7-8 is a great achievement, level 9 rare, level 10 very few. Level groupings in Tables 5.5/5.6: Novice (1-2), Intermediate (3-5), Good (6-8), Elite (9-10), as printed in the headers.
Ages 18-38 are ranked equally (peak running age). From 39 up there is a yearly adjustment. Values beyond tested ages (below young runners / above 70s) are extrapolated assuming a consistent rate of performance drop. Few categories are shown for ages 13 to 6, to avoid encouraging very young runners to work too hard.
Reference points: VDOT 3.5 is lying down; an easy walk is about VDOT 10. VDOT 50 means a 5K under 20 min; VDOT 60 means a 17:03 5K or faster.
Examples: a 10-year-old girl with a 1,600 of 7:18 is level 6, equal to an 18-year-old girl at 5:26 or an 18-year-old male at 4:55. A 58-year-old woman running 1,600 in 7:00 equals a young woman at 5:04. VDOT values can be looked up in Table 5.1 for other distances (interpolate non-whole VDOT).
tags: type:principle type:zones level:masters level:general-population subtopic:age-grading subtopic:performance-levels sport:running
src: Ch 5 | Tables 5.4-5.6
evidence: author-experience

### [DRF-C12-003] Cold weather and extreme conditions: running guidance

- Cold weather can produce season-best performances even with long sleeves and mittens. Start with an extra upper-body layer and mittens and discard them after warming up. Author's marathon PR: 39 °F (4 °C), mittens off about 5 miles (8 km) in, clear, sunny, no wind; he lost 5 lb (2.7 kg), about 3 percent of body weight, without taking fluids.
- Runners in very cold/snowy areas: experiment with layering and outer garments in training to learn how to dress.
- Footing is more secure on an inch or two (2.5-5 cm) of snow than on cleared street or sidewalk, which is often more slippery.
- When conditions are too extreme (very cold or hot), use an indoor track or treadmill. Example: a runner did a weekly 20-mile (32 km) treadmill run for 12 weeks and placed second in the NYC marathon in 2:09.
tags: type:decision-rule sport:running level:all subtopic:cold subtopic:treadmill
evidence: author-experience
src: Ch 6 "Temperature Considerations"

### [DRF-C12-004] 800 m: resistance training and individual approach

- Better 800 runners include a fair amount of resistance training in their overall programs. Cruz did circuit training; coach Bob Timmons had Ryun doing a good amount of weight training, even in early training years.
- Phase I: resistance training generally three times per week. Options: free weights, circuit training, or body-resistance workouts (sit-ups, push-ups, bar dips, squat thrusts).
- Learn technique first with minimal resistance; do not use heavier resistance until technique is of high quality.
- Kratochvílová example: farm-girl strength plus twice-daily interval training (belief that endurance building did not diminish speed); set 800 world record at age 32.
- Coe example: father's regimen described as systematic, progressive, and hard.
tags: type:principle sport:running phase:base level:all subtopic:resistance-training subtopic:800-meter
evidence: author-experience
src: Ch 11 intro, Phase I | pp. n/a

### [DRF-C12-005] Racing season context for 1,500-2 mile specialists

- Track season lets you vary race distances (unlike cross country, where all races are about the same duration). Use short races (400-1,500) for anaerobic/speed stress and 3K/2 mile for aerobic stress, and adjust training in multi-race weeks.
- End-of-cross-country runners are in top aerobic shape: skip phase I, begin phase II R work for an indoor season of shorter races.
- Indoor-season runners may also race 800 and 1,000 m; this plan prepares for a variety of middle-distance events.
tags: type:decision-rule sport:running level:all subtopic:season-context
src: Ch 12 intro, Phases II and IV | n/a

### [DRF-C12-006] Novice marathon runners: two types and Rerun caution

The novice category has two types: true beginners (never had running training) and those who ran a fair amount years ago and want to return carefully, called "Reruns".
Reruns must not try to duplicate what they used to do, at least until they have built a reasonable base. Injuries plague Reruns more than true beginners, because beginners do not know what is possible and every bit of improvement is better than they imagined.
tags: type:caution sport:running level:beginner subtopic:reruns subtopic:injury-risk
src: Ch 16 Marathon | Novice Training
evidence: author-experience

### [DRF-C12-007] Meb Keflezighi marathon training example

Meb Keflezighi first showed his potential by running a 5:20 mile in a seventh-grade PE class. He trained for marathons in 9-day cycles compiling 140 to 150 miles, interspersing interval workouts, tempo runs and long runs, while monitoring his body and resting when needed to stay healthy.
tags: type:principle level:elite sport:running subtopic:elite-example subtopic:cycle-length
evidence: author-experience
src: Ch 16 "4-Week Cycle"

### [DRF-C12-008] Ultrarunning context: races and who runs them

- Race size: famous US races often limited to 200 to 400 entrants (National Park Service, US Forest Service, Bureau of Land Management restrictions); lotteries are common; Western States can take years to get into. Ultra-Trail du Mont-Blanc (Italy, France, Switzerland; weeklong festival in Chamonix) has more than 2,500 starters and is widely regarded as among the most difficult.
- Duration: most are under 24 hours; most on the calendar are 50K to 100K. 100 miles (160 km) is popular and many take over 24 hours. Multiple-day events: Marathon des Sables (Morocco, about 1,000 participants); TransRockies Run (Colorado), a 6-day stage race over 120 miles (193 km), point to point.
- Many runners repeat favorite races yearly, or volunteer at aid stations if they cannot run.
- Background: ultrarunning is typically not the start of a running career; most began with high-school cross country or later road races, or moved on from a marathon as a bucket-list item. Most aim to explore terrain, finish the distance and reach a personal time goal rather than race others; all shapes, ages and sizes.
tags: type:principle sport:running level:all subtopic:ultrarunning
evidence: author-experience
src: Ch 17 Q&A

### [DRF-C12-009] Author's multisport experience: rest and recovery lessons

- Modern pentathlon in the U.S. Army: same schedule for all athletes, 6 days/week (riding 2 h 6:00-8:00, fencing ~2 h, swimming 1.5 h, pistol shooting several hours, second fence or ride, about 1 h running at night). Run training as last session was difficult, especially for weaker runners, with no individualization.
- Later, in graduate school (Sweden), with less training (ride and shoot 1 day, fencing 2 days, early-morning swimming 5 days, running sometimes daily, often at lunch), fitness stayed the same in ride/shoot and improved in swim and run, probably due to more rest and recovery between sessions.
- Tim O'Donnell (fastest American at 2019 Kona Ironman World Championship): year-round base of about 5 h/day, one rest day per week, one day completely off every 3 weeks, ramped up in the final months before the race.
tags: type:principle sport:triathlon level:elite subtopic:rest-recovery subtopic:individualization
evidence: author-experience
src: Ch 18 "Triathlon"

## C13 Contraindications, Cautions & Safety Limits

### [DRF-C13-001] Cautions from Chapter 1: illness, injury, overtraining, race dropout

- Do not train when sick or injured (WHY: leads to longer setback than a few days off).
- Persistent feeling out of sorts: have a professional check it (WHY: usually a medical issue).
- Do not run a race when feeling poorly, especially long ones; dropping out is recommended over struggling (WHY: long recovery before running well again; marathon worse than 5K).
- Do not impose high mileage on beginners (e.g., 150 miles/241 km per week is a negative direction).
- Highly motivated runners: coach must restrain extra training to avoid injury; keep same load and intensity about four weeks before increasing.
- Do not use running as punishment (discourages youth from the sport).
tags: type:caution level:all subtopic:illness subtopic:injury subtopic:overtraining sport:running
src: Ch 1 | Laws 3, 10, 11; "Direction"; "Intrinsic Motivation"
evidence: author-experience

### [DRF-C13-002] Chapter 2 cautions summary

- Overstress can weaken or break down tissue; avoid overstressing via amount, intensity or frequency; rest is when strengthening occurs.
- Avoid overtraining; use constant runner-coach interaction; raise intensity only when race performance shows readiness (or after 4-6 weeks at an intensity with no felt increase in stress).
- Change only one training variable at a time; hold a stress level 6-8 weeks.
- Do not impose the same prescribed pace on all runners; poor technique under fatigue defeats the workout.
- Calf/shin discomfort: try mid/rear-foot landing for a few weeks; avoid toes-out landing (inside shin pain).
- Asthma or other breathing issues: discuss with a doctor.
- Don't copy extreme volumes/workouts of champions.
tags: type:caution sport:running level:all subtopic:overtraining subtopic:injury-prevention
evidence: author-experience
src: Ch 2 (various)

### [DRF-C13-003] Caution: do not push hemoglobin very high; low Hgb

- WHAT: trying to elevate hemoglobin to very high values.
- LIMIT: avoid; keep within normal (12 to 18 g/dL range; do not drive the number up).
- WHO: endurance athletes.
- WHY: raises blood viscosity, strains the heart and can slow circulation.
- Also: below-normal Hgb impairs feeling and performance; managed through iron-containing foods.
- Also: a considerably elevated morning HR may call for rest or a health checkup.
tags: type:caution sport:running subtopic:hemoglobin level:all
evidence: author-experience
src: Ch 3 "Hemoglobin Content", "Resting Heart Rate"

### [DRF-C13-004] Work-bout duration limits (I and R pace) and youth training restrictions

- Interval (I) work bout duration:
  - WHAT: Individual work bouts at I pace.
  - LIMIT: Maximum 5 minutes per work bout. Lowest VDOT permitted to run I miles is 66 (at VDOT < 66, a mile exceeds 5 minutes).
  - WHO: All runners.
  - WHY: Working beyond 5 minutes at VO2max pace exceeds the intended physiological stimulus and causes excessive fatigue.
  - EXCEPTION: None stated.
- Repetition (R) work bout duration:
  - WHAT: Individual work bouts at R pace.
  - LIMIT: Maximum 2 minutes per work bout. R 600 m bouts require VDOT ≥ 56; R 800 m bouts require VDOT ≥ 77.
  - WHO: All runners.
  - WHY: R pace emphasizes mechanics, neuromuscular speed, and economy without high lactate accumulation; bouts > 2 minutes compromise mechanics and purpose.
  - EXCEPTION: Minor deviations are acceptable now and then (e.g., a runner with VDOT 70 running R 800 m in 2:10).
- Youth runner training load and expectations:
  - WHAT: Structured performance and high training volume/intensity for children.
  - LIMIT: Table categories are deliberately omitted/limited for ages 6 to 13 (fewer categories as age decreases down to age 6).
  - WHO: Children aged 6 to 13 years.
  - WHY: Avoid encouraging very young runners to work too hard or overtrain.
  - EXCEPTION: None.
tags: type:caution level:all level:masters subtopic:work-bout-duration-limit subtopic:youth-running sport:running system:vo2max system:anaerobic
src: Ch 5 | "Using VDOT to Establish Training Intensities", "Achieving Performance Levels Based on VDOT and Age Categories"
evidence: author-experience

### [DRF-C13-005] Cautions: heat, hydration and altitude

- WHAT: training hard under adverse conditions all the time. LIMIT: do only some training in such conditions. WHO: runners preparing for different-climate races. WHY: cannot train as hard, fitness is lost.
- WHAT: dehydration in the first several days at altitude. LIMIT: drink plenty of fluids; rest regularly. WHY: maintains blood volume.
- WHAT: unacclimatized altitude racing. LIMIT: pace must be a fair bit slower than Table 6.1. WHY: table assumes some time at altitude.
- WHAT: going out at sea-level pace in an altitude distance race. LIMIT: avoid. WHY: excess anaerobic demand causes late slowing. EXCEPTION: races up to 800 m.
- WHAT: warm, humid sea-level marathon right after altitude. LIMIT: body responds poorly for 10 to 14 days.
- WHAT: extreme weather. LIMIT: use indoor track/treadmill; snow footing note: cleared pavement can be more slippery than 1-2 inches of snow.
tags: type:caution sport:running level:all subtopic:altitude subtopic:heat subtopic:dehydration
evidence: author-experience
src: Ch 6

### [DRF-C13-006] Downhill running cautions (treadmill)

- WHAT: downhill running increases landing shock (while uphill reduces it) and can easily increase quadriceps soreness if too fast or down more than a few degrees of grade.
- LIMITS: add downhill bouts gradually; spend about 4 weeks at one setting before increasing grade or speed. Do not increase downhill stress in the final couple of weeks before a race. Do not initiate downhill running during the final 4 to 6 weeks before an important race.
- Experiment with any new type of training in the off-season or early in a prolonged training cycle.
- WHO/Note: most treadmill training is level or moderate positive grade; negative-grade running need not concern average or elite runners except when racing on a course with downhill sections.
tags: type:caution sport:running level:all subtopic:downhill-running
evidence: author-experience
src: Ch 7 "Hill Training"

### [DRF-C13-007] Cautions before starting a running program (Ch 8)

- WHO: beginners. Get a physical exam before starting, to confirm the body can handle the stress.
- Consult qualified running coaches and experienced runners before spending much on shoes and apparel.
- Never train when injured or ill (all runners).
- First race after the white plan: optimally no longer than about 40 minutes. Walking during the race is acceptable. Start slower than the pace you think you can hold for the full distance.
tags: type:caution level:beginner subtopic:medical-clearance sport:running
src: Ch 8 intro; White plan
evidence: author-experience

### [DRF-C13-008] Safety limits: downhill running, deteriorating mechanics, and persistent injury

- Downhill running safety:
  - WHAT: Downhill running slope and surface.
  - LIMIT: Only gradual hills of 2% to 3% slope (well below the 6% maximum allowed on interstate highways). Avoid steep downhills and hard road surfaces. Avoid overstriding; maintain a light, fast leg turnover ("roll" rather than bound down); landing rear-footed rather than on the balls of the feet may reduce stress.
  - WHO: All runners performing downhill work.
  - WHY: Running on too steep a downhill or on hard surfaces increases the chance of injuring hips, knees, and feet.
  - EXCEPTION: None.
- Deteriorating mechanics (form breakdown):
  - WHAT: Running with fatigued or poor mechanics.
  - LIMIT: Terminate the run immediately whenever technique feels "sloppy".
  - WHO: All runners, especially when building up mileage or running longer than adapted to.
  - WHY: Nothing leads to running injury more quickly than poor running mechanics.
  - EXCEPTION: None. Substitute with weight room, circuit training, or other non-impact supplemental training.
- Persistent injury evaluation:
  - WHAT: Self-treating or training through pain.
  - LIMIT: Any injury lasting more than a couple of days must be evaluated by a physical therapist, athletic trainer, or medical doctor.
  - WHO: Any runner experiencing injury symptoms.
  - WHY: Minor symptoms left unaddressed can become major chronic issues; conversely, severe-seeming problems may have simple solutions under professional guidance.
tags: type:caution level:all subtopic:downhill-running subtopic:running-mechanics subtopic:injury-prevention sport:running
evidence: author-experience
src: Ch 9 "Nonplanned Breaks" & "Adding Supplemental Training"

### [DRF-C13-009] 800 m training: safety limits, progression cautions, and injury prevention

- **Resistance training load restriction**:
  - *What*: Heavier resistance in weight or resistance training.
  - *Limit*: Avoid completely until technique is learned and performed with high quality using minimal resistance.
  - *Who*: All runners undertaking resistance training (free weights, circuit training, body-resistance exercises).
  - *Why*: Prevent injury from improper lifting form.
- **Uphill strides descent caution**:
  - *What*: Returning down after uphill strides.
  - *Limit*: Be careful coming back down between uphill repetitions (maintain control, avoid jarring descent forces).
  - *Who*: Runners utilizing gradual uphill terrain for strides.
  - *Why*: High-impact downhill jogging or running poses injury risk to joints and soft tissue.
- **Training pace progression rate limit**:
  - *What*: Advancing training intensities (VDOT-derived paces).
  - *Limit*: Do not increase the speed of training any more often than every 3rd or 4th week, even if a recent race performance indicates a higher VDOT.
  - *Who*: All middle-distance runners across phases.
  - *Why*: The musculoskeletal and cardiovascular systems require several weeks performing at one level of stress before stepping up to the next stress tier.
- **Mileage reduction allowance**:
  - *What*: Weekly running volume.
  - *Limit*: Feel free to reduce total weekly mileage at any time.
  - *Who*: Any runner experiencing health problems or undue life/training stress.
  - *Why*: Accommodating recovery prevents overtraining and breakdown.
tags: type:caution sport:running level:all subtopic:injury-prevention subtopic:resistance-training subtopic:strides subtopic:pace-progression
src: Ch 11 intro, Phase I, Phase III, 50 to 60 miles per week

### [DRF-C13-010] Cross country cautions, safety limits, and execution limits

- Downhill recovery terrain limit: during uphill R-pace workouts, ensure the route back down is NOT steep; downhill running places excessive eccentric stress and impact on leg muscles and joints.
- Biomechanical deterioration limit: cut any L (long) run short immediately if running mechanics start to deteriorate.
- Warm-up temperature limit: muscle temperature increase should be limited to ~2 degrees; increasing body/muscle temperature beyond a couple of degrees leads to subpar performance, dehydration, and premature overheating, especially in longer races or warm conditions.
- Late-season hill training prohibition: avoid uphill training during the final couple of weeks leading up to an important race to ensure adequate recovery from musculoskeletal stress.
- End-of-season intensity caution: avoid trying to train harder during the final few weeks of the season; prioritize remaining rested.
- Interval duration ceiling: never schedule I-pace work bouts longer than 5 minutes per repetition.
- Session volume upper limits (per session maximums, not requirements):
  - I-pace: lesser of 8% of weekly mileage or 10K (6.2 miles).
  - R-pace: lesser of 5% of weekly mileage or 5 miles (8 km).
  - T-pace: 10% of weekly mileage.
  - Phase I Long (L) run: lesser of 30% of weekly mileage or 60 minutes.
  - Phase II Long (L) run: strictly less than 25% of weekly mileage.
  - Phase I daily volume: no single day to exceed 25% of total weekly mileage.
tags: type:caution sport:running level:all subtopic:safety-limits subtopic:volume-caps metric:volume
src: Ch 14 "Phase I", "Phase II", "Phase III", "Preparing for the Race", summary
evidence: author-experience

### [DRF-C13-011] Caution: Downhill jogging during hill strides

- What: Jogging downhill after completing uphill strides.
- Caution: Be careful jogging back down because downhill running can sometimes be stressful on the body.
- Who: Runners performing strides on moderate hills.
- Recommendation / Exception: Strides may be run up a moderate hill, but take care during the jog descent to avoid excess musculoskeletal stress.
tags: type:caution sport:running phase:all level:all subtopic:strides subtopic:downhill-running subtopic:injury-prevention
evidence: author-experience
src: Ch 16 "4-Week Cycle"

## C14 Formulas & Calculations

### [DRF-C14-001] Running economy per kilometer (ml/kg/km) and gender comparison example

- Formula: aerobic demand per km (ml O2/kg/km) = (1,000 m / speed in m/min) × relative VO2 (ml/kg/min). I.e. minutes per km × ml/kg/min.
- Naive comparison at 300 m/min: man 57 ml/kg/min, woman 60 ml/kg/min (5% difference). Man's max 73 → 78% of max. Woman's max 67 → 89.5% of max. So test her at a speed eliciting 78% of her max.
- Worked example: woman at 78% of max = 268 m/min, relative VO2 50 ml/kg/min. 1,000/268 = 3.73 min/km; 3.73 × 50 = 187 ml/kg/km.
- Man at 78% of max = 300 m/min, 3.33 min/km, 56 ml/kg/min: 56 × 3.33 = 187 ml/kg/km.
- Result: equal economy when running at the same relative intensity.
tags: type:formula sport:running system:aerobic metric:pace subtopic:running-economy level:all
evidence: author-experience
src: Ch 3 "Gender Differences"

### [DRF-C14-002] Figure 4.5 Intensity point system

Purpose: compare overall training stress across intensities; log and plan season-to-season (e.g., +10 points in I zone next season).
Points per minute:
- E (easy; include warm-up, cool-down, recovery run time): 0.2/min (average E intensity = 66% of VDOT; E zone 59-74% of VDOT)
- M: 0.4/min (about 75-84% of VDOT)
- T: 0.6/min (mid- to upper-80% of VDOT)
- 10K intensity (between T and I): 0.8/min
- I (count recovery runs as 0.2/min): 1/min (races about 2-5 miles / 3-8 km)
- R (count recovery runs as 0.2/min): 1.5/min (105-110% VDOT; race durations about 4:40-7:00, 1,500 m/1 mile)
- FR fast-rep (count recovery as 0.2/min): 2/min (115-120% VDOT; 800 m speeds)
Calculation: day's total = E + M + T + 10K + I + R + FR points; weekly total = sum over zones. The form also has blanks for typical HR at each intensity (R and FR HR typically maximum).
Daniels notes relative values may not be perfectly accurate but are useful for logging.
Suggested weekly totals: beginning high school runners 50 points/week, a year or two later 100; college up to 150; 200 or more after graduation. Some can accumulate more without injury.
tags: type:formula type:monitoring sport:running metric:load level:all subtopic:training-points
src: Ch 4 "Tracking Training Intensities", Figure 4.5
evidence: author-experience

### [DRF-C14-003] VDOT calculation from economy and fraction of max

VDOT = (oxygen cost of the race pace, ml/kg/min, from the economy curve) / (fraction of V̇O2max for the race duration, from the Fig 5.2 curve).
Example in the book: 51.7 / .936 = 55.2 (5 miles in 30 min, 6:00/mile pace).
1-minute volume conversion example: subject A, 30-s collection with VE 65 L and VO2 2,000 ml gives 130 L and 4,000 ml per minute. Subject B, 40-s collection with VE 75 L and VO2 2,500 ml gives 112.5 L and 3,750 ml per minute.
tags: type:formula level:all subtopic:vdot sport:running
src: Ch 5
evidence: author-experience

### [DRF-C14-004] Daniels' 6-Second Rule (estimating R, I, T from mile/1,500 pace)

R pace is close to current mile or 1,500 race pace per 400. Example: VDOT 60 has a mile of about 5:00 (Table 5.1 shows 4:57), about 75 s per 400, and Table 5.2 lists 75/400 for R.
- I pace = R pace per 400 + 6 s (VDOT 60: I = 81 s/400).
- T pace = I pace per 400 + 6 s (the VDOT 60 example in the text says 7 s slower; Table 5.2 gives T = 88 s/400).
- The rule applies most accurately to better runners. Make it a 7- or 8-second rule for runners in the 50 or 40 VDOT categories.
- Use: with only a recent mile or 1,500 time, take its per-400 pace as R and derive I and T without the tables.
tags: type:formula type:decision-rule level:all subtopic:six-second-rule sport:running metric:pace
src: Ch 5 | "Daniels' 6-Second Rule"
evidence: author-experience

### [DRF-C14-005] Treadmill belt speed formulas

- V (m/min) = (D × 60) / T, D = meters in 10 belt revolutions (belt length × 10), T = seconds for 10 revolutions. Example: (54.29 × 60)/13.03 = 250 m/min.
- T = (D × 60) / V. Example: 268 m/min -> 12.15 s.
- Belt length in cm = inches × 2.54; meters = cm / 100.
tags: type:formula sport:running metric:pace level:all subtopic:treadmill-calibration
evidence: author-experience
src: Ch 7 "Treadmill Calibration"

### [DRF-C14-006] Table 7.2 Conversions for Miles per Hour, Mile Pace, and Meters per Minute

*Note: 1,609.344 m is equal to 1 mile. Source: Jack Daniels' Running Calculator designed by the Run SMART Project.*

| Conversion / Formula | Example |
| :--- | :--- |
| **Miles per hour to mile pace** | **Example: 9.0 mph** |
| 60 ÷ mph = mile pace | 60 ÷ 9.0 = 6.6667 min/mile |
| Decimal minutes × 60 = seconds | .6667 × 60 = 40 sec, so 6:40 mile |
| **Mile pace to miles per hour** | **Example: 6:40 mile pace** |
| Seconds ÷ 60 = decimal minutes | 40 ÷ 60 = .6667 (plus 6 min = 6.6667 min) |
| 60 ÷ minutes per mile = mph | 60 ÷ 6.6667 = 9.0 mph |
| **Meters per minute to miles per hour** | **Example: 241.4 m/min** |
| m/min × 60 = m/hr | 241.4 × 60 = 14,484 m/hr |
| m/hr ÷ 1,609.344 m* = mph | 14,484 ÷ 1,609.344 = 9.0 mph |
| **Miles per hour to meters per minute** | **Example: 9.0 mph** |
| mph × 1,609.344* = m/hr | 9.0 × 1,609.344 = 14,484 m/hr |
| m/hr ÷ 60 = m/min | 14,484 ÷ 60 = 241.4 m/min |
| **Meters per minute to mile pace** | **Example: 241.4 m/min** |
| 1,609.344* ÷ (m/min) = min/mile | 1,609.344 ÷ 241.4 = 6.6667 min/mile |
| Decimal minutes × 60 = seconds | .6667 × 60 = 40 sec, so 6:40 mile |
| **Mile pace to meters per minute** | **Example: 6:40 mile pace** |
| Seconds ÷ 60 = decimal minutes | 40/60 = .6667 min |
| Minutes + decimal minutes = min/mile | 6 + .6667 = 6.6667 min/mile |
| 1,609.344* ÷ (min/mile) = m/min | 1,609.344 ÷ 6.6667 = 241.4 m/min |
tags: type:formula sport:running metric:pace level:all subtopic:unit-conversion
evidence: author-experience
src: Ch 7 Table 7.2

### [DRF-C14-007] Figure 9.2 Estimating Return VDOT Value

Steps:
- A = pre-setback weight in kg (pounds × .454 = kg).
- B = pre-setback VDOT (from recent race time).
- C = A × B.
- D = C ÷ current weight in kg = weight-adjusted VDOT.
- Apply the appropriate FVDOT value from Table 9.1 to D to get the VDOT on which to base comeback training. Once racing, use race results for the most accurate VDOT.

Worked example: pre-setback weight 132.2 lb → A = 60 kg (132.2 × .454); B = 50; C = 50 × 60 = 3,000; current weight 139 lb (139 × .454 = 63.1 kg); D = 3,000/63.1 = 47.5 (adjusted VDOT).
tags: type:formula level:all subtopic:vdot-adjustment subtopic:weight-change sport:running
evidence: author-experience
src: Ch 9 | Figure 9.2

### [DRF-C14-008] Peak (P) mileage and fraction of peak

Weekly mileage varies from about 80 to 100 percent of peak (P) mileage, P being the most mileage planned in any single week of the program. Example: peak 40 miles; .8P = 80 percent of 40 = 32 miles. Each week's fraction of P is in the second column of the program (table 16.3).
tags: type:formula sport:running level:all metric:volume subtopic:peak-mileage
src: Ch 16 Marathon | 2Q Program
evidence: author-experience

### [DRF-C14-009] Final T, I, R pace from goal M pace (no VDOT)

- T = M pace − 15 s/mile.
- I = T − 6 s/400 m.
- R = I − 3 s/200 m.
- Worked example: M 6:00/mile (3:43/km) → T 5:45/mile (about 86 s/400; 3:35/km) → I 80 s/400 (3:20/km) → R 74/400 (37/200).
- Early-phase adjustment: weeks 1-8 use paces 10 s/mile slower; weeks 9-16 within 4 s/mile (2.5 s/km) of final.
tags: type:formula sport:running metric:pace level:all subtopic:pace-calculation
evidence: author-experience
src: Ch 16 "4-Week Cycle"

## C15 Evidence & Key References

### [DRF-C15-001] Key scientific references in Chapter 4

- Daniels, J., and N. Scardina. "Interval Training and Performance." Sports Medicine 1, no. 4 (1984): 327-334. [Note: chapter text misprints title as "Internal Training and Performance"]. Basis for Figure 4.1 defining the five training intensities (E, M, T, I, R), work-to-rest ratios, bout durations, and weekly mileage caps.
- Karlsson, J., et al. Energikraven Vid Lopning [Energy Requirements When Running]. Stockholm: Trygg, 1970, p. 39. Basis for Figure 4.4 showing oxygen uptake kinetics and accumulation of time at VO2max during short interval bouts with truncated recovery periods.
tags: type:evidence sport:running level:all subtopic:interval-training subtopic:training-zones
src: Ch 4 Figure 4.1, Figure 4.4

## C16 Notable Statements

### [DRF-C16-001] Notable statements: Chapter 1

- "First you are a person, second you are a student, and third you are a runner—and don't ever rearrange that order of importance while you are in school."
- "A good coach can always answer the questions 'What is the purpose of this workout' and 'Why are we doing this workout today?'"
- "Consistency in training is the single most important thing that leads to success. That consistency comes from concentrating on the task at hand—neither dwelling on the past nor looking too far forward."
- "Training should be rewarding. It's not always fun, but it should always be rewarding."
- "A good run or race is never a fluke. Sometimes a bad run is a fluke, but if you do run a great race, it is because you are capable of doing it."
tags: type:principle level:all subtopic:coaching subtopic:philosophy sport:running
src: Ch 1 | "Direction", "Daniels' Basic Laws of Running", closing

### [DRF-C16-002] Notable statements: Chapter 2

- "Eat well, rest often, and maintain a good state of hydration." (Ch 2 opening)
- "Whenever you are not sure which of two training sessions to take on at any particular time, select the less stressful of the two." (Principle 3)
- "I would personally rather have my top seven runners all healthy and a little below their capability than to have three of them in peak shape and the other four injured and not even able to start the championship race." (Principle 3)
- "Prove to me in a race that you are ready to train faster." (Principle 5)
- "Always try to achieve the greatest possible benefit from the least amount of training rather than getting the greatest possible benefit from the hardest training possible." (Training Plan Development)
- "At all times you should be able to answer the question 'What is the purpose of this workout?'" (Training Plan Development)
tags: type:philosophy sport:running level:all subtopic:coaching-philosophy subtopic:quotes
evidence: author-experience
src: Ch 2 (various)

### [DRF-C16-003] Notable statements: VDOT and race expectations

"Set a realistic goal for every race you run."
"Using a person's race times gives a far better prediction of training intensities and other race performances than do tests in the lab."
"Think of the VDOT values as good predictors of physiological capability."

## C17 Validity Notes

### [DRF-C17-001] Validity Notes

[VALIDITY NOTE — not from source] The author's "220 − age" example is presented only as an inaccurate estimate. No change needed; measured max HR (hill repeats) is the author's recommendation.

[VALIDITY NOTE — not from source] The 1,500-m world record is given as "3:26:00" (Hicham El Guerrouj, 1998). The commonly cited record is 3:26.00 (min:sec.hundredths); the printed format is a typographic quirk, not a validity change.

[VALIDITY NOTE — not from source] Where the book states hemoglobin anemia cutoffs (below 13.5 g/dL for men, below 12 for women), current WHO thresholds are 13.0 for men and 12.0 for non-pregnant women. This is a difference in threshold; consult current clinical guidance for diagnosis.

Otherwise no validity notes.
tags: type:caution level:all subtopic:validity
src: Synthesis
